DOL Proposes Major Overhaul of PERM Labor Certification Program

Published Nov 16, 2026

The U.S. Department of Labor has filed a proposed rule for public inspection that would overhaul the Permanent Labor Certification (PERM) program used for EB-2 and EB-3 employment-based immigrant visas. The proposal is only a Notice of Proposed Rulemaking, not a final or effective rule, and is scheduled to be published in the Federal Register.

Key points

  • The rule would eliminate further administration of Schedule A, which currently exempts certain job opportunities from the standard labor market test.
  • It would strengthen minimum recruitment standards so employers must reach a broader pool of qualified U.S. workers.
  • It would add protections for U.S. workers affected by layoffs, including layoffs by employers using third-party staffing companies, and require employers to report layoffs.
  • It would expand requirements for employers to give U.S. workers a notice of filing (NOF) for permanent labor certification applications.
  • It would codify DOL's existing practice of receiving, processing and issuing final determinations on prevailing wage and labor certification requests electronically.
  • The proposal affects employers sponsoring workers for EB-2 and EB-3 immigrant visas through the PERM process administered by DOL's Office of Foreign Labor Certification.

Who is affected

Employers filing PERM labor certification applications to sponsor EB-2 and EB-3 immigrant visas, the foreign workers they sponsor, and U.S. workers in affected occupations.

What to do

Interested parties may submit written comments through the Federal eRulemaking Portal (regulations.gov), citing docket number ETA-2026-0133, within 60 days after the rule is published in the Federal Register.

Full text

The complete official text was archived on this site on 2026-11-17. It stays available here even if the source link changes or is removed.

Department of Labor
Employment and Training Administration
20 CFR Part 656
[DOL Docket No. ETA-2026-0133]
RIN 1205-AC29
( printed page 73916)
AGENCY:
Employment and Training Administration, Department of Labor.
ACTION:
Notice of proposed rulemaking.
SUMMARY:
The Department of Labor (Department or DOL) proposes to amend its regulations regarding the certification of job opportunities offered by employers seeking to employ aliens on a permanent basis in the United States. This Notice of Proposed Rulemaking (NPRM or proposed rule) strengthens the standards by which the Department receives and reviews employers' applications for permanent labor certification and improves the integrity of the overall employment-based immigrant visa program to more effectively protect the wages and job opportunities of U.S. workers similarly employed in the United States. Among the issues addressed in this proposed rule are eliminating further administration of Schedule A, which exempts certain job opportunities from the statutorily required labor market test; strengthening the minimum standards of recruitment to ensure all employers seeking labor certification access the broadest pool of high-quality U.S. workers using the latest technologies and methods; enhancing safeguards for U.S. workers impacted by layoffs, including those laid off by employers using third-party staffing companies and establishing standards for employers to report layoffs; expanding requirements for employers to provide U.S. workers with notice of filing (NOF) applications for permanent labor certification; improving employer compliance with program requirements and consideration of qualified U.S. workers that is comparable to job opportunities for which permanent labor certification is not required; and codifying the Department's practice of electronically receiving, processing, and issuing final determinations on employer requests for prevailing wages and permanent labor certifications. The changes in this proposed rule are consistent with the Secretary of Labor's (Secretary) statutory responsibilities to certify that there are not sufficient workers in the United States who are able, willing, qualified, and available to fill the petitioning employer's job opportunity at the time of application for a visa and admission to the United States, and that the employment of such alien will not adversely affect the wages and working conditions of similarly employed workers in the United States.
DATES:
Interested persons are invited to submit written comments on all aspects of this proposed rule and the changes to the information collection on or before January 19, 2027.

II. Revisions to 20 CFR Part 656
A. Statutory Framework
The Immigration and Nationality Act (INA or the Act), as amended by the Immigration Reform and Control Act of 1986, assigns responsibilities to the Secretary relating to the admission and employment of certain aliens who intend to work and live permanently in the United States. The Act prohibits the admission of certain employment-based immigrants to perform skilled or unskilled labor as permanent residents unless the Secretary has determined and certified to the Secretaries of Homeland Security and State that (1) there are not sufficient workers who are able, willing, qualified, and available at the time of application for a visa and admission to the United States and at the place where the alien is to perform such skilled or unskilled labor; and (2) the employment of such alien will not adversely affect the wages and working conditions of workers in the United States similarly employed.[1] See section 212(a)(5)(A)(i), 8 U.S.C. 1182(a)(5)(A)(i). In addition, the Act specifies that for aliens who are members of the teaching profession, referring to college and university teachers, and those possessing exceptional ability in the sciences or the arts, the Secretary must consider only equally or more qualified United States (U.S.) workers as qualified for the job opportunity offered.[2] See section 212(a)(5)(A)(ii), 8 U.S.C. 1182(a)(5)(A)(ii). For all other job opportunities requiring the Secretary's determination and certification, each prospective U.S. worker who possesses the minimum qualifications required for the job opportunity and who will be available at the time and place needed to perform the services or labor required is considered available.
The INA provides for five “preference” categories, or immigrant visa classes, only two of which—the second and third preference employment categories (commonly called the EB-2 and EB-3 immigrant visa classifications)—generally require a labor certification.[3] An employer seeking to sponsor an alien worker for an immigrant visa under the EB-2 or EB-3 preference categories generally must file a visa petition with the Department of Homeland Security (DHS) on the alien's behalf, and the petition must include a labor certification from the Secretary.[4] Further, the Department of State (DOS) may not issue a visa unless the Secretary has issued a labor certification in conformity with the relevant provisions of the INA.[5] The INA provides one exception, which applies only to certain aliens eligible for the EB-2 visa classification. If the Secretary of Homeland Security determines it is in the national interest to waive the permanent job offer requirement—permitting the alien to self-petition under the EB-2 visa classification—the Secretary's certification is not required for admission to the United States.[6] See section 203(b)(2)(B); 8 U.S.C. 1153(b)(2)(B). All other EB-2 and EB-3 aliens cannot be admitted to the United States without being sponsored for permanent employment with a U.S. employer that first obtains the Secretary's statutory certification for the job offered. See section 203(b)(2)(A), (b)(3)(C); 8 U.S.C. 1153(b)(2)(A), (b)(3)(C).
Within the Department, the Secretary has delegated authority to make the required determination and issue the related certification to the Assistant Secretary for the Employment and Training Administration (ETA).[7] The Assistant Secretary for ETA, in turn, has delegated that authority to the Office of Foreign Labor Certification (OFLC). As a result, OFLC reviews requests for permanent labor certification, issues certifications, and carries out other statutory responsibilities as required by 8 U.S.C. 1182(a)(5)(A).
B. Current Regulatory Framework
A U.S. employer sponsoring an alien for permanent admission based on the ( printed page 73918) EB-2 or EB-3 visa classifications must demonstrate that the job requirements qualify for the visa classification it seeks, that the alien qualifies for the job opportunity, and—as it relates to a labor certification for the permanent labor certification program or PERM labor certification program—that qualified U.S. workers are not available to fill the position and that employment of the alien will not adversely affect wages and working conditions of similarly employed U.S. workers.
The Department's regulations, 20 CFR part 656, implement the statutory provisions that govern the permanent labor certification (“PERM labor certification”) process and set forth the responsibilities of U.S. employers who seek to permanently employ aliens covered by the statutory labor certification requirements.
On May 6, 2002, the Department published an NPRM that proposed to streamline the permanent labor certification program.[8] A final rule implementing significant revisions to the permanent labor certification program was published on December 27, 2004, and took effect on March 28, 2005.[9] In addition, on February 13, 2006, the Department published an NPRM designed to enhance program integrity and reduce the incentives and opportunities for fraud and abuse in the permanent labor certification program.[10] The Department subsequently published a final rule on May 17, 2007, which took effect on July 16, 2007, commonly referred to as “the PERM fraud rule.” [11] This rule prohibited, among other things, the substitution of beneficiaries on permanent labor certification applications and resulting certifications; established a 180-day validity period for the use of approved labor certifications filed in connection with an I-140 Immigrant Petition with USCIS; prohibited the sale, barter or purchase of permanent labor certifications and applications; required employers to pay the costs of preparing, filing and obtaining certification, and strictly prohibited transferring such cost to the alien beneficiary; and established procedures for debarment of employers from the permanent labor certification program.[12]
To receive a PERM labor certification under current regulations, the employer must first obtain a prevailing wage determination (PWD), conduct a good faith test of the domestic labor market for qualified U.S. workers, and post a NOF to employees before submitting an Application for Permanent Employment Certification (the current Form ETA-9089) to OFLC for review and processing. A request for a PWD, which is collected through the Application for Prevailing Wage Determination (the current Form ETA-9141), enables OFLC to review the duties, requirements, and worksite location(s) of the job opportunity and provide the employer with the applicable prevailing wage rate(s).[13] The employer must offer a wage that equals or exceeds the PWD determined by OFLC and guarantee that the wage the employer will pay to the alien will equal or exceed the prevailing wage that is applicable at the time the alien begins work or from the time of admission to take up the certified employment. The PWD ensures that the recruitment of U.S. workers based on the employer's job opportunity offers at least the average wage paid to similarly employed workers in the specific occupation in the area of intended employment (AIE) or the applicable collective bargaining agreement (CBA) rate, and if the employer permanently hires aliens at the wage rate offered, doing so will not adversely affect workers similarly employed in the United States.[14]
The statutorily required NOF, which must be provided to bargaining representative(s) or posted in conspicuous locations at all worksite locations, as applicable, helps inform the employer's employees of the job offered to the alien in the PERM labor certification process and provides U.S. workers and others with an opportunity to contact OFLC with information concerning the application.[15] The existing test of the domestic labor market generally involves the employer following a prescribed list of recruitment requirements, such as placing a job order with the State Workforce Agency (SWA) for 30 days and print newspaper advertisements on two different Sundays, which are intended to apprise U.S. workers of the job opportunity and give them an opportunity to apply.[16] Together, these pre-filing requirements enable the employer to provide information essential to OFLC's ability to reach the statutorily required determinations when reviewing an Application for Permanent Employment Certification.
Upon completion of pre-filing requirements, the employer typically submits the Application for Permanent Employment Certification electronically to OFLC using the Foreign Labor Application Gateway (FLAG) system, which contains a series of attestations made by the employer, guaranteeing the employer has taken or will take steps to comply with the PERM labor certification process under 20 CFR part 656. After performing an initial review of the application, OFLC may grant or deny a PERM labor certification, request further information, select one or more applications for audit examination, and/or require the employer to undergo additional recruitment of U.S. workers under OFLC's supervision.
Where a PERM labor certification is approved, OFLC issues a Form ETA-9089— Final Determination: Permanent Employment Certification Approval (Final Determination), to the employer and the authorized attorney or agent, as applicable, that can be used to file an Immigrant Petition with USCIS. If, however, OFLC denies a PERM labor certification, the employer may appeal the denial. An employer currently has the option of requesting the Department's reconsideration or requesting review before the Board of Alien Labor Certification Appeals (BALCA or the Board). Presently, the employer must request reconsideration or BALCA review no later than 30 days from the date of the denial.[17] Where OFLC upholds its denial decision on reconsideration, an employer may further seek BALCA review within 30 days of the decision on reconsideration. If the employer does not pursue an appeal or if the employer's appeal is unsuccessful in overturning the denial decision, the employer may file a new Application for Permanent Employment ( printed page 73919) Certification with OFLC that meets the requirements of 20 CFR part 656.
The PERM labor certification program is largely an attestation-based program, requiring the employer to attest that it has completed the required steps for the PERM labor certification process, met the conditions of employment on the application, and retained all applications and supporting documentation demonstrating compliance with program requirements. For example, the employer attests that the position has been open to any U.S. worker; U.S. workers who did apply were rejected for lawful, job-related reasons; the pay offered is at least the prevailing wage rate; and the job opportunity's terms and conditions are not contrary to federal, state, or local law.[18] The Department has integrity measures in place to protect and strengthen the PERM labor certification program. OFLC conducts pre-certification audits to ensure employer compliance with the requirements of 20 CFR part 656. During audit review, OFLC currently requests all applications and supporting documentation to verify, among other requirements, that the employer posted the required NOF to its employees and conducted all applicable pre-filing recruitment steps, as required and as attested to on its application, and that no qualified U.S. worker was available for the job opportunity for which the employer seeks PERM certification.[19] In addition to audits, OFLC has the discretion to require an employer to conduct supervised recruitment to ensure an adequate and more current test of the U.S. labor market is performed to determine whether qualified U.S. workers are available to fill the employer's job opportunity.[20]
With respect to program integrity measures, if OFLC discovers after issuing a certification that certification was not justified, OFLC may take further action, such as revocation. Additionally, if the Department learns an employer, attorney, or agent is involved in possible fraud or willful misrepresentation in connection with the PERM program, OFLC will refer the matter to other government entities, as appropriate, for investigation, and send a copy of the referral to the DOL Office of Inspector General (DOL-OIG). In these cases, or if the Department is notified by other government entities that an employer, attorney, or agent is under investigation for possible fraud or willful misrepresentation in connection with the PERM program, OFLC may suspend processing of any Application for Permanent Employment Certification involving such employer, attorney, or agent for an initial period of up to 180 calendar days and may extend the suspension of processing until completion of any investigation and/or judicial proceedings. Where OFLC finds the employer, attorney, agent, or any combination thereof, has violated the regulatory requirement(s) under 20 CFR part 656, OFLC may pursue debarment action(s) in accordance with the standard and procedures set forth in 20 CFR 656.31(f) for a reasonable period of not more than 3 years.[21]
C. Recent Administrative Action Related to the Permanent Labor Certification Process
Beginning in 1965 and for the purpose of reducing delays in processing an alien's requests for an employment-based immigrant visa, the Department established through rulemaking, schedules or listings of occupations where the Secretary could pre-certify whether sufficient qualified workers in the United States were available to fill the employer's need for permanent labor.[22] One such schedule, referred to as Schedule A, set forth a list of occupations that the Secretary designated as in short supply of qualified U.S. workers, which permitted employers to bypass the statutory labor certification requirement prior to filing an Immigrant Petition requesting admission of an alien to perform permanent skilled or unskilled labor.[23] During the ensuing thirty years, the occupational listings in Schedule A were periodically reviewed and only revised eight times, the last time in 2004.[24]
On December 21, 2023, the Department published a request for information, Labor Certification for Permanent Employment of Foreign Workers in the United States; Modernizing Schedule A To Include Consideration of Additional Occupations in Science, Technology, Engineering, and Mathematics (STEM) and Non-STEM Occupations.[25] The Department published the request for information to allow the public to provide input, including data, statistical metrics or models, studies, and other relevant information, on how the Department may establish a reliable, objective, and transparent methodology for revising Schedule A to include STEM and other non-STEM occupations where there may exist shortages of qualified U.S. workers, consistent with requirements of the INA. Specifically, the Department sought public comment on evaluating the utility of expanding Schedule A to include STEM and non-STEM occupations for which there may be an insufficient number of ready, willing, able, and qualified U.S. workers. Through the request for information, the Department requested input on the appropriate data sources and methods for determining whether labor shortages exist and asked the public how the Department could establish a reliable, objective, and transparent methodology for identifying both STEM and non-STEM occupations that are experiencing labor shortages.
The Department received over 2,000 comments in response to the request for information. The comments received ranged from opposition to the continuation of Schedule A to support for its expansion. Those opposed to the continuation and/or expansion of Schedule A characterized Schedule A as unnecessary, undermining statutory intent, a mechanism to bypass immigration rules, and/or harmful to U.S. workers. Additionally, none of the commenters who expressed support for Schedule A provided the Department with a suitable rationale or administratively feasible methodology to expand or maintain the current occupations listed on Schedule A. Several commenters supported either limiting or reducing Schedule A occupations, and provided some examples and citations, but they also did not provide a suitable rationale or administratively feasible methodology to determine which occupations to retain or remove from Schedule A. Although a handful of commenters ( printed page 73920) made concerted efforts to respond to questions posed in the request for information, responses received from commenters overall did not provide sufficient substantive information that would allow the Department to proceed with proposing changes to the regulations governing Schedule A. While many commenters successfully identified potential specific sources of economic or labor data that could be used, the commenters almost uniformly failed to specify which precise data markers or thresholds should be established or used to determine whether there was a labor shortage covering a particular geographic area warranting inclusion on Schedule A.
In terms of identifying STEM occupations, one consistent recommendation was that the Department should mimic DHS's “STEM Designated Degree Program List for the Optional Practical Training (OPT) STEM extension” to define STEM for purposes of identifying occupations for expanding Schedule A. However, the commenters failed to explain how a program which determines eligibility for aliens to engage in a limited period of post-graduation employment in the United States is applicable to the PERM program. The DHS STEM Designated Degree Program List enumerates the fields of study that DHS designates as STEM for purposes of the 24-month STEM OPT extension described at 8 CFR 214.2(f)(10)(ii)(C). It has nothing to do with labor shortages and the comments did not address how the process for updating OPT is applicable to defining labor shortages within those degree fields and related occupational classifications. There are considerable substantive differences between the OPT and PERM program requirements, so while many commenters suggested that DHS' program for updating OPT would be useful for PERM, these commenters did not explain how it could be shaped to meet the very different nature of the two programs, and the respective objectives of each program. Commenters also suggested adding numerous non-STEM industries to Schedule A, but the commenters likewise failed to provide information that would sustain a factual determination that there is a labor shortage of those occupations in any particular geographic area.
In the absence of a reliable, objective, and transparent methodology for updating Schedule A or the relevant substantive information needed to develop such a methodology, the Department decided it could not add new occupations to Schedule A nor demonstrate that the occupations currently listed in Schedule A are still shortage occupations. The Department is now proposing through this rulemaking to eliminate Schedule A, which is explained further below in section III.C.
D. Need for Rulemaking
It is the policy of the Department to vigorously enforce all laws within its jurisdiction governing administration of the PERM labor certification program, which informs USCIS' approval of EB-2 and EB-3 immigrant visa petitions. The Department has not updated its regulations associated with the PERM labor certification process in nearly 20 years and changes in labor market dynamics, including increases in remote work, advances in electronic methods of recruiting highly qualified talent, high rates of turnover or layoffs in certain sectors of the U.S. economy, necessitate a need to strengthen the program's standards and procedures. Further, with the Trump Administration's executive actions to strengthen the nation's southern border, enhance interior enforcement efforts, and promote economic growth, employers may increasingly turn to legal labor pathways—like permanent and temporary employment work visas, and the Department must ensure that employers first access and consider the broadest pool of qualified and available U.S. talent before seeking to sponsor and hire an alien for permanent employment in the United States.[26]
This proposed rule is also part of a broader Government effort to more faithfully enforce the immigration laws of the United States as set out by Congress.[27] In coordination with other federal agencies, the Department has launched major investigations and intensified enforcement actions, particularly related to third-party staffing firms in the technology sector, to combat fraud and abuse involving the PERM labor certification process and the H-1B visa classification where the majority of nonimmigrant workers are often sponsored for permanent employment in the United States.[28] In collaboration with other federal law enforcement partners, the DOL-OIG has uncovered widespread schemes in which employers and labor brokers submitted fraudulent applications, exploited aliens through coercive wage-kickback arrangements, and undercut American workers by flooding the market with below-wage labor. Where these federal law enforcement partners are investigating fraud or willful misrepresentations involving the PERM program, the Secretary has initiated suspensions of current and future filed PERM applications by certain employers.[29] Meanwhile, U.S. workers continue to face a challenging and disadvantageous labor market.[30] U.S. workers in computer-related occupations—particularly recent U.S. college graduates—are facing high unemployment and underemployment ( printed page 73921) rates.[31] In FY 2025, job opportunities in computer and technology related occupational classifications constituted the vast majority of H-1B and PERM jobs certified by DOL.[32] Further, employers in the technology sector are laying off large numbers of their U.S. workers while simultaneously filing applications requesting DOL certification for temporary H-1B and/or permanent workers.[33] As noted in Presidential Proclamation 10973 of September 18, 2026, Restriction on Entry of Certain Nonimmigrant Workers, certain employers, including information technology (IT) staffing and outsourcing firms, have abused the system to suppress wages and displace American workers, flooding the domestic labor market with an abundant supply of low-paid entry-level workers and making it more difficult for American workers and recent graduates, especially in science, technology, engineering, and math (STEM) fields, to find employment.[34]
Through its program experience, the Department observes that many employers seeking to employ H-1B workers in these skilled positions, including those who engage in layoffs or utilize third-party staffing firms, are often concurrently sponsoring foreign nationals for PERM labor certification where a good faith test of the domestic labor market is a prerequisite to obtaining a certification from the Secretary, as Congress intended. In light of these circumstances, the Department is concerned that current regulations governing the PERM labor certification process do not require employers to conduct a sufficiently robust labor market test—one that utilizes methods that align with modern recruitment practices—and creates incentives for some employers to use different, potentially discriminatory, procedures for considering qualified U.S. applicants that they would otherwise not employ for the same or comparably similar job opportunities not subject to PERM labor certification.
Accordingly, this proposed rule seeks to strengthen the integrity of the PERM labor certification process to improve the program's protections for the U.S. workforce while ensuring that, consistent with statutory obligations, employers with true workforce shortages can access legal immigrant labor possessing the highest quality talent, without undue cost or administrative burden. In particular, and as discussed in detail within this preamble, this rule proposes the following major changes to the Department's PERM labor certification regulations, followed by a section-by-section discussion of all proposed changes:
Modernizing Recruitment To Attract High-Quality American Talent
Establishes a single set of recruitment requirements for all occupations and, except as permitted by Congress, eliminates the Department's continued management of Schedule A that administratively exempts certain occupations from the statutory labor certification requirement. Additionally, this rule proposes replacing PERM's print-intensive and self-selective menu of recruitment options with a modern set of electronic recruitment requirements that leverages use of the employer website(s), job search website(s), social media platform(s), and other job fairs or on-campus recruiting methods. These provisions will better align PERM's labor market test with modern recruitment practices and ensure employers conduct a more robust labor market test to access the broadest pool of qualified and available U.S. workers.[35]
Expands the length of time advertisements are available to prospective U.S. applicants, including the job order placed with the SWA, to a period of no less than 90 calendar days or the total number of days the employer has customarily advertised for job opportunities in the same occupational classification during the most recent 12 months that did not require PERM labor certification, whichever is longer. This proposed rule also requires that all recruitment be completed no more than 60 calendar days and no fewer than 30 calendar days before the expected filing of the application with the submission of a recruitment report to DOL. Finally, this rule clarifies the minimum advertising content for all recruitment efforts and extends long-standing optional competitive recruitment procedures for college and university teachers, with minor updates, to professional athletes and professional sports team coaches. These provisions will provide U.S. workers with a fair chance to apply for permanent job opportunities and ensure that employer advertising and consideration of qualified U.S. workers is performed in the same manner as other job opportunities for which PERM labor certification is not required.
Improving Safeguards for U.S. Workers Impacted by Layoffs
Prohibits employers who have laid off U.S. workers in the same or similar occupation and at the place(s) of employment within the most recent 2 years from the date on which the employer requests PERM labor certification.
Expands the current requirement that employers engaging in layoffs consider qualified U.S. workers to a post-filing requirement—from the date on which the employer requests PERM labor certification through the date on which the Form I-140 petition is approved by USCIS or the final determination date, if theApplication for Permanent Employment Certification or Form I-140 petition is denied or withdrawn, whichever is later. If such layoffs occur, requires employers to document and provide such documentation to DOL that it has offered the job opportunity to those laid-off U.S. workers and, where not rejected ( printed page 73922) for a lawful, job-related reason(s), the U.S. worker was offered the job opportunity. Additionally, this rule prohibits third-party staffing companies from placing the alien(s) with another employer engaging in layoffs where there are indicia of an employment relationship between the alien and that other employer during the same layoff timeframe. These provisions will safeguard the rights of U.S. workers impacted by layoffs when such employers are concurrently seeking PERM labor certification from DOL and admission under an EB-2 or EB-3 visa classification from DHS.
Establishes an affirmative obligation for employers engaging in layoffs, including the placement of the alien(s) with another employer engaging in layoffs, to affirmatively notify DOL and, where a Form I-140 petition is pending for the alien(s), DHS not later than 5 business days after such layoff(s) occurs, and continually update their recruitment reports throughout the expanded layoff timeframe. The notification will trigger the INA 204(b) consultation process between the departments whereby DOL will determine the adequacy of the employer's compliance with the post-certification obligation to consider laid off U.S. workers, and inform USCIS of the continued validity, or revocation of the underlying labor certification based on DOL's assessment of the employer's compliance. These provisions will provide greater transparency to DOL and DHS, as applicable, on employers engaging in layoffs who are concurrently seeking PERM labor certification and better align the consideration of U.S. workers impacted by layoffs with the Department's statutory mandate to certify an insufficient number of qualified U.S. workers available at the time of application for a visa and admission of the alien(s) into the United States to perform the skilled or unskilled services for permanent employment.
Strengthening Employer Compliance and Program Integrity
Revises the standards and procedures for employers to provide notice to U.S. workers and the bargaining representative(s), as applicable, of filing the application by clarifying the requirements for providing such notice electronically to U.S. workers, including the intended placement of the alien(s) at third-party worksite locations, and expanding the timeframe to begin within 10 business days before the application is filed and remain available to U.S. workers through the date on which the Form I-140 petition is approved or the date of determination, if theApplication for Permanent Employment Certification or Form I-140 petition is denied or withdrawn, whichever is later. These provisions will better align with the statutory requirement and strengthen program integrity by permitting any person or employee of the employer, particularly those impacted by layoffs during the post-filing adjudication process of the application or who may have information related to fraud or misrepresentations, to take action and report such information bearing on the application to OFLC for appropriate action.
Clarifies the definitions of “employer” and “attorney” and “agent,” the use of these terms in the filing of an application, and enhances the obligations employers seeking to employ aliens must declare, under penalty of perjury, to assure compliance with terms and conditions of employment and program requirements, including the recruitment and consideration of U.S. workers in “good faith” and in a manner that is comparable to job opportunities that do not require PERM labor certification. These provisions will strengthen protections for similarly employed U.S. workers by providing better clarity regarding the responsibilities of employers and their authorized attorneys or agents, as applicable, and promote greater program compliance.
Enhances the Department's debarment authority by holding agents and attorneys, and their successors in interest, accountable for their own misconduct independent of the employer's violation(s) and extending debarment from the PERM labor certification program to all of the Department's labor certification and labor condition application programs. Prohibiting debarred entities from filing any new applications with OFLC or receiving any approvals from OFLC during the debarment period strengthens the debarment penalty, when imposed. Any application requesting PERM labor certification filed by debarred entities during the period of debarment will be denied without review. These provisions will improve program integrity and prevent unscrupulous employers from circumventing the PERM labor market test requirements.
Modernizing the Prevailing Wage and PERM Application Process
Establishes a single point of entry by requiring that employers, except in limited circumstances, electronically file applications requesting PWDs and PERM labor certifications and all supporting documentation through a centralized electronic system maintained by DOL and permits the use of electronic signatures meeting valid signature standards. These provisions will minimize unnecessary costs and burdens on DOL and employers, improve the quality of applications, reduce the frequency of delays associated with deficient applications, and better facilitate information sharing with other federal agencies.
Requires electronic methods for the OFLC Certifying Officer (CO) to send notices and requests to employers and issue PWDs and PERM labor certification decisions directly to USCIS. These provisions will codify OFLC's standard operating procedures to minimize delays, reduce unnecessary administrative costs for the employer and DOL, and expedite the delivery of final decisions to employers and their authorized attorneys or agents, as applicable, and to other federal agencies, while maintaining program integrity.
The Department invites all interested parties to participate in this rulemaking by submitting comments on all aspects of this rule, as described above. Comments that include written data, sources, and specific feedback on all aspects of this proposed rule would be most helpful to the Department.
III. Summary of Proposed Revisions to 20 CFR Part 656
A. Discussion of Proposed Technical and Summary Changes to 20 CFR Part 656
The Department proposes to revise various terms and phrases used throughout the regulation. These revisions are intended to improve readability, accuracy, or clarity and increase consistency within part 656 and across OFLC regulations and program administration. These modifications are explained below.
The Department proposes to use the term “ Application for Permanent Employment Certification,” the current Form ETA-9089 form title, when referring to Form ETA-9089 and the related Form ETA-9141, Application for Prevailing Wage Determination, which contains information about the job opportunity necessary to the CO's review of the employer's request for permanent labor certification. This term would replace the form number throughout part 656 for clarity, and create consistency with other OFLC programs, with one exception—where Form ETA-9089 is used in section 656.3 ( printed page 73923) for the definition of Application for Permanent Employment Certification.
The Department proposes to replace the term “Form ETA 750” throughout part 656, with Application for Permanent Employment Certification. “Form ETA 750” has been eliminated as the form's use is no longer needed and the information collection has been discontinued. Consistent with directions provided to the public when implementing the form changes, the persons and entities that used the Form ETA 750 now must use the Form ETA-9089, which is now true for all Applications for Permanent Employment Certification processed under part 656.[36] The use of the terms “Form ETA-9089” and “Form ETA 750” is being deemphasized to ensure the continuing accuracy of the regulation regardless of changes to the form, the form number, or its associated appendices and related information collections.
The Department proposes to change the term “labor certification” to “permanent labor certification” throughout part 656 for clarity. This change would more clearly distinguish the type of labor certification program addressed in part 656—permanent ( i.e., immigrant)—from the labor certifications addressed in OFLC's regulations in part 655—temporary ( i.e., nonimmigrant).
The Department proposes to change the terms “alien labor certification” and “alien employment certification” to “permanent labor certification” for internal consistency throughout part 656.
The Department proposes to use the term “permanent employment” throughout part 656 wherever the term “employment” alone could be unclear and to ensure consistency with the definition of “permanent employment” in section 656.3.
The Department proposes to use the term “U.S. worker,” rather than “United States worker,” throughout part 656 to ensure consistency throughout the regulation and with the definition in section 656.3.
The Department proposes additional changes throughout the text to correct typographical errors and improve clarity and readability, including the use of acronyms, such as using “DOL” for “Department of Labor” or “Department,” “BALCA” or “Board” in place of “Board of Alien Labor Certification Appeals,” or “CO” for “Certifying Officer,” where appropriate. Such changes are non-substantive and do not change the meaning of the current text. Substantive changes to the current regulatory text are discussed in the corresponding section of the preamble.
As warranted, the Department is proposing to retitle numerous sections to align with the subject matter as it has been reorganized, which includes retitling or removing the title for various paragraphs, subparagraphs, clauses, etc.
B. Discussion of Proposed Revisions to 20 CFR Part 656, Subpart A
Under this proposed rule, subpart A would be expanded and renumbered from 20 CFR 656.1 through 656.3 to now include 20 CFR 656.1 through 656.7. Section 656.5, which currently is in subpart B, would be relocated to be repurposed under subpart A.
1. Proposed 20 CFR 656.1, Purpose and Scope
The Department is proposing to revise part 656, subpart A, and rename subpart A, “ General Provisions.” The Department proposes to reorganize and revise the content of current paragraphs (a) and (b) in proposed paragraph (a) in addition to revising the remaining language that will be placed under proposed paragraph (b). Specifically, paragraph (a) will be subtitled “Purpose” and paragraph (b) will be subtitled “Scope.” The Department is proposing to add new language on the scope of this subpart at proposed section 656.1(b). The Department believes that this reorganization and proposed revisions will make this subpart clearer and easier to follow.
For proposed section 656.1(a), which discusses the purpose of this subpart and the Department's responsibilities under the INA, the Department proposes to eliminate content from current section 656.1(a) that restates the INA and to relocate content from current section 656.1(b) that describes the purpose of part 656 to proposed section 656.1(a), with revisions. After relocating content from current paragraph (b) to proposed paragraph (a), the Department proposes revisions to update terminology and to better address the purpose of part 656, which is to establish the requirements and procedures by which the Department makes determinations in connection with requests for permanent labor certification and certifies its determination to DHS and DOS. For example, the Department is proposing to revise the language at current section 656.1(b), which states that the regulations “set forth procedures through which such immigrant labor certifications may be applied for, and granted or denied,” and replace it with language stating that part 656 establishes “the requirements and the procedures by which the Department of Labor makes a determination in connection with applications for permanent employment certification and certifies its determination to the Department of Homeland Security and Department of State.” In addition, the Department proposes to retain the current sections containing the two statutory requirements for the Secretary's determination and certification—at current and proposed sections 656.1(a)(1) and (2)—with revisions to update terminology and improve readability. The Department believes that this proposed language simplifies the explanation for the regulations by using plain English, to make them easier to understand by the general public, and addresses the necessary details more thoroughly than the previous regulatory text.
The Department is also proposing new language at section 656.1(b), which discusses the scope of this part. The proposed language in this paragraph reiterates that part 656 contains the procedures that govern the PERM process. In addition, the new language proposed in section 656.1(b) makes clear that this part establishes standards and obligations regarding the terms and conditions with which employers must comply for permanent labor certification and sets forth integrity measures for ensuring employers' good faith recruitment of U.S. workers.
The Department is also taking this opportunity to remind stakeholders that compliance with the requirements contained in this part is the minimum standard for granting permanent labor certification. Compliance with this part does not establish compliance with, and is not a defense to separate liability under, Title VII of the Civil Rights Act of 1964 (42 U.S.C. 2000e et seq.), 8 U.S.C. 1324b, or any other Federal, State, or local law governing employment. As discussed in this proposed rule, information obtained by the Department under this part may be provided to other federal enforcement agencies, including the Equal Employment Opportunity Commission (EEOC) and the Department of Justice, ( printed page 73924) Civil Rights Division, Immigrant and Employee Rights Section (DOJ-IER).
The Department is also proposing to eliminate section 656.1(c), which currently describes how stakeholders may send written correspondence or queries regarding the Department's regulations under part 656. The address provided in this section is no longer accurate and, rather than continually having to update an office or physical location address through future regulatory changes, the Department prefers to provide current contact information to stakeholders through more efficient means that allow for faster updates to the public, such as on OFLC's website and on its form instructions. Further, given OFLC's proposal to transition to full electronic processing, providing a street address would be less relevant, and the Department prefers to offer stakeholders alternative means of communication using email and online portal platforms.
2. Proposed 20 CFR 656.2, Statutory Authority and Burden of Proof
The Department proposes to revise the heading of section 656.2 of subpart A from “Description of the Immigration and Nationality Act and of the Department of Labor's role thereunder” to “Statutory Authority and Burden of Proof” and the content of this section by removing and replacing the content of current paragraph (a), revising the content of current paragraph (b), and eliminating current paragraph (c).
The proposed revisions to section 656.2 are consistent with the Department's efforts in this rulemaking to simplify part 656 by removing unnecessary statutory restatements and content that extends beyond the Department's authority. The topics addressed in proposed section 656.2 are the delegation of the Secretary's statutory authority to issue permanent labor certifications and carry out other responsibilities required by 8 U.S.C. 1182(a)(5)(A) and the burden of proof when an employer requests permanent labor certification. In proposed sections 656.2(a) and (b), the Department proposes new and revised content that more clearly and simply states the delegation of the Secretary's statutory authority to OFLC and the employer's burden of proof.
Currently, section 656.3 defines relevant components of DOL. However, part 656 does not clearly address the delegation of the Secretary's authority within DOL. The proposed new language at 656.2(a) states in plain English that the Secretary's authority to issue permanent labor certifications and carry out other responsibilities required by 8 U.S.C. 1182(a)(5)(A) has been delegated to OFLC. Within OFLC, the OFLC Administrator makes determinations on Applications for Permanent Employment Certification and designates staff ( e.g., a CO) to share this responsibility. The proposed provision is consistent with the Department's current practice in the permanent labor certification program and with the Department's temporary labor certification program regulations.[37] In addition, the proposed changes offer clarity about the delegation of authority to OFLC, the OFLC Administrator, and COs and closes a regulatory gap in part 656.
Under this proposed rule, the OFLC Administrator and COs will continue to exercise the Secretary's authority to review Applications for Permanent Employment Certification and, where the employer establishes eligibility to receive certification of an Application for Permanent Employment Certification ( i.e., all statutory and regulatory requirements are met), to issue the PERM labor certification to DHS or DOS.
3. Proposed 20 CFR 656.3, Definition of Terms
This rule proposes several changes to section 656.3, which includes the definitions of the terms used in part 656. Throughout this section, the Department proposes changes to add definitions for terms used throughout part 656 but not previously defined in 656.3; to align the definitions across the Department's administration of employment-based visa programs, specifically the H-2A, H-2B, and H-1B visa classifications; and to improve accuracy and clarity of definitions.
First, the Department is proposing to add the definition of Administrative Law Judge (ALJ) to this section. While this definition is already included at sections 655.5 and 655.103 for the H-2B and H-2A programs, respectively, the Department is proposing to include the same definition at section 656.3, as it is also used in this section.
The Department is also proposing to add definitions of Actual minimum requirements and Business Necessity to this section. These terms are currently used at sections 656.17(h) and (i) to explain the CO's assessment of job duties and requirements on the Application for Permanent Employment Certification, but they are not currently defined. Current section 656.17(i)(1)—at section 656.19(d) in this proposed rule—states that the job requirements, as described, must represent the employer's actual minimum requirements for the job opportunity. In that provision, the Department outlines scenarios for which employers are required to demonstrate certain requirements as a business necessity ( e.g., a foreign language requirement or a combination of occupations). Current section 656.17(h)(1)—at section 656.19(c) in this proposed rule—extends beyond those enumerated scenarios, providing that job requirements must be those normally required for the occupation and must not exceed the Specific Vocational Preparation (SVP) level assigned to the occupation as shown in the Occupational Network (O*NET) Job Zones, unless the employer justifies the requirements as arising from business necessity.
The proposed definition of “actual minimum requirements” states that they are the minimum requirements for education, experience, training, skills, licenses, or other requirements that are necessary to perform the employer's job opportunity in a normally acceptable manner. In addition, consistent with current section 656.17(h), the proposed definition also provides that the employer's stated actual minimum requirements must be normal to the occupation and must not exceed the SVP for the Standard Occupational Classification (SOC) code applicable to the employer's job opportunity, unless justified as arising from business necessity. Through O*NET, the Department makes occupational information available to the general public, such as the academic preparation ( e.g., Bachelor's degree) and SVP ( e.g., career and technical education, training, and experience) normally required for average performance in an occupation. Under the proposed rule, the CO would continue to use the occupational information in the O*NET as one source to assess the normalcy of the employer's asserted actual minimum requirements. To help guard against employers “tailoring” the requirements of a permanent position to the specific qualifications of a foreign national, the Department is also proposing a revision to its review of applications by comparing the actual minimum requirements stated in the Application for Permanent Employment Certification to the requirements stated in the employer's postings for job opportunities in the same or substantially comparable occupational classifications that were not subject to ( printed page 73925) PERM labor certification for the 12 months preceding the date on which recruitment began for the job opportunity stated in the Application for Permanent Employment Certification. In the event of an audit and where the PERM job opportunity imposes materially more stringent job requirements, including any combination of education, experience, training, skills, licenses, or other requirements, than the employer's most recent efforts to recruit U.S. workers for non-PERM positions, the employer will be required to submit written justifications for each material difference under the business necessity standard.
Current section 656.17(h)(1)—proposed section 656.19(d)(1)—states that to establish “business necessity” an employer must demonstrate that the job duties and requirements bear a reasonable relationship to the occupation in the context of the employer's business and are essential to perform the job in a reasonable manner. The Department proposes to include this definition in proposed section 656.3.
The proposal to define these terms in proposed section 656.3 is to make the terms and their meaning easier to locate. The proposed definitions are consistent with the Department's longstanding practice with some revisions to reflect both the Department's program experience reviewing applications and the experience of other federal agencies enforcing the current regulatory framework. The Department invites comments about whether and how these terms could be further clarified or defined to strengthen program integrity and ensure qualified U.S. workers are provided first access to these job opportunities and fairly considered for hire.
The Department proposes to revise the definition for Agent to make it similar to the definition of agent included in the Department's H-2B and H-2A regulations at sections 655.5 and 655.103, respectively.
The Department proposes only minor revisions to the definition of the term Applicant, such as deleting the reference to the form number. Under both the current and proposed rules, the term Applicant means a U.S. worker who is applying for a job opportunity for which an employer has filed an Application for Permanent Employment Certification. The current definition of Applicant identifies the form number for the Application for Permanent Employment Certification, a detail that is not relevant to this definition. By deleting unnecessary information, the Department's proposal simplifies part 656.
The Department also proposes revising the currently defined term Application for clarity and better align with the Department's current approach to information collection. The Department proposes to change the term from Application to Application for Permanent Employment Certification and revise the definition to include the form number, which could be helpful for identifying the required form. In addition, the proposed definition clarifies the term Application for Permanent Employment Certification encompasses both the Form ETA-9089 and the Form ETA-9141, Application for Prevailing Wage Determination, and includes a reference to appendices and potential successor forms. When the Department last revised Form ETA-9089 in preparation for adding the PERM labor certification program process to the FLAG system in June 2023, the Department took steps to reduce duplicative information collected from employers through Form ETA-9141 and Form ETA-9089 and leverage efficiencies available in the FLAG system. As a result, the Department removed duplicative fields from the Form ETA-9089 and instead incorporates the information collected on the Form ETA-9141 into the CO's review under section 656.40. The proposed definition continues to include the purpose of the form, with minor revisions for conformity with terminology used throughout the proposed rule, which is to secure a permanent labor certification determination from the Department.
The Department is also proposing to revise the definition of Area of intended employment (AIE) to increase consistency of defined terms across the Department's programs, while simplifying the definition by deleting old terminology. The Department proposes to eliminate language in the current definition that includes the Primary Metropolitan Statistical Area and the Consolidated Metropolitan Statistical Area (CMSA), which are no longer commonly used when describing an AIE. The Department proposes not to replace CMSA with an updated term—Combined Statistical Area (CSA)—so that the definition proposed in section 656.3 aligns with the same term's definition in the H-2A and H-2B programs, at sections 655.103(b) and 655.5, respectively. The proposed revision of the definition of AIE is not intended to change the CO's assessment of normal commuting distance, for example, in employer-provided surveys submitted with an Application for Prevailing Wage Determination.
The Department proposes minor revisions to the definition of Attorney, which includes adding clarification that an attorney who is under suspension, debarment, expulsion, or disbarment from practice before any court may not represent an employer under this part.
DOL is also proposing minor revisions to Barter, such as deleting the references to form numbers from the definition.
The Department proposes minor revisions to the definition of Board of Alien Labor Certification Appeals (BALCA or Board), which includes deleting the reference to BALCA's location.
The Department proposes to define the term Business Day to mean any day other than a Saturday, Sunday, or Federal holiday, and notes that use of the terms “business day” and “working day,” wherever used in this part, have the same meaning. The Department notes that this proposed definition follows the convention used in other programs, which calculate deadlines by reference to Federal holidays as defined at 5 U.S.C. 6103 and roll due dates to “the next business day if the due date falls on a Saturday, Sunday or Federal holiday” ( e.g.,20 CFR 655.11 (H-2B)). Fed. R. Civ. P. 6(a) follows this convention too.
DOL proposes revising the definition of Certifying Officer (CO) to add that the OFLC Administrator is the national CO and that other COs may be designated by the OFLC Administrator, similar to definition at sections 655.5 and 655.103.
The Department proposes adding a definition of Chief Administrative Law Judge (Chief ALJ) to this subpart to align with the definition of Chief Administrative Law Judge in sections 655.5 and 655.103.
DOL proposes adding a definition of Department of Homeland Security (DHS) to this subpart to state that this agency is established by 6 U.S.C. 111.
The Department proposes adding a definition of Employee to this subpart to align with the definition provided in sections 655.5 and 655.103.
The Department proposes revising the definition of Employer for clarity and improve alignment with similar definitions in sections 655.5 and 655.103. The revised definition describes an employer as an individual or entity (including a partnership, association, corporation, cooperative, firm, joint stock company, trust, public institution, or other organization with legal rights and duties), rather than a “person” followed by a nonexclusive list of entity types. The definition continues to require an “employer” to ( printed page 73926) possess a valid Federal Employer Identification Number (FEIN) and a social security number cannot be used in lieu of an FEIN. In addition, the definition continues to require that the “employer” offer full-time, permanent employment at a place within the United States and to have a place of business (physical location) in the United States to which U.S. workers can be referred for employment. In addition to having a place of business in the United States to which U.S. workers may be referred for employment, the NPRM adds that an “employer” must also have a means by which it may be contacted for employment. The Department proposes to remove the explicit exclusion of independent contractors from the definition of “employer,” and add, instead, that an “employer” must have—or will have after hiring—an employment relationship (such as the ability to hire, pay, fire, supervise, or otherwise control the work of the employee) with respect to a worker, which includes the alien and U.S. worker applicants, as it relates to this program. Additionally, in proposed paragraph (2), the Department retains the language excluding “persons who are temporarily in the United States” and the associated list of examples from the definition of “employer.” Finally, the Department is proposing a new paragraph (3) to exclude foreign missions from this definition. As defined by the Foreign Missions Act under 22 U.S.C. 4302(a)(3), a foreign mission means “any mission or entity in the United States involved in diplomatic, consular, or other activities that is substantially owned or effectively controlled by a foreign government, or by an organization representing a territory or political entity that has been granted diplomatic or other official privileges and immunities under the laws of the United States, or that engages in some aspect of the conduct of the international affairs of such territory or political entity, including any real property of such a mission.” The Department notes that foreign missions have special accommodations while operating in the United States and are not subject to the same level of obligations and accountability ( e.g., immunity from suit under the Foreign Sovereign Immunities Act) and their employee compensation is exempted from federal, state, and local income and other taxes as domestic employers, which may undermine the ability of federal agencies to investigate, audit, and enforce compliance with PERM requirements. Thus, the Department invites public comments on this proposed revision to exclude foreign missions, as defined by the Foreign Missions Act under 22 U.S.C. 4302(a)(3), the definition of “employer” for purposes of obtaining PERM labor certification from DOL.
The Department invites comments about whether additional revisions to the definition of “employer” should be made for clarity or to avoid unintended results, such as leaving any doubt that self-petitioning ( i.e., being both employer and employee) on an Application for Permanent Employment Certification is prohibited.
The Department also proposes eliminating the current definition of Employment from this section. The Department is proposing several modifications related to term “employment” throughout this proposed rule and does not believe the limited definition in this section is useful.
The Department is proposing to add a definition of Familial relationship to clarify the types of relationships between an employer, or its employees, and the alien that must be disclosed on the Application for Permanent Employment Certification. The proposed definition is intentionally broad as it is designed to ensure the integrity of the labor certification process by requiring the disclosure of all relationships that could influence the employer's recruitment or suggest the absence of a bona fide job opportunity. In addition, the proposed definition retains the current requirement that the employer must prepare and maintain documentation of the relationship(s), for submission upon request by the CO, in compliance with proposed 656.10(e)(2). For job opportunities subject to such review under proposed section 656.10(e)(2), the Department reiterates that familial relationships must be disclosed on the Application for Permanent Employment Certification. Failure to disclose familial relationships, currently and under the proposed rule, may be grounds for a finding of material misrepresentation.
The Department is proposing to add the definition of Federal holiday as a legal public holiday as defined at 5 U.S.C. 6103. This definition aligns with the definition of Federal holiday in sections 655.5 and 655.103.
The proposed rule would eliminate the definition of Immigration Officer —a term used to identify adjudicators within USCIS—from this section. This deletion would further the Department's goal of removing regulatory text from current part 656 that discusses topics that are under the purview of another agency.
The Department is proposing to add the definition of Immigrant Petition to part 656, which will define this as the USCIS Form I-140, Immigrant Petition for Alien Workers, or successor form, and accompanying documentation required by DHS for employers seeking to employ foreign persons under an employment-based immigrant visa.
The Department is proposing minor revisions to the definition of Job opportunity to clarify that a job opportunity must be full-time employment at a place in the United States to which U.S. workers can be referred. Consistent with long-standing practice and in the event of an audit, the employer must be prepared to document the permanent and full-time nature of the position by furnishing position descriptions and payroll records for the job opportunity involved in the Application for Permanent Employment Certification.
The Department is proposing to add definitions of Job offer, Job order, and Layoffs to align with similar definitions in sections 655.5 and 655.103 that cover H-2B regulations.[38] While these definitions are included elsewhere in DOL regulations specific to other OFLC programs, they are not currently included in section 656.3, despite the terms being repeatedly used throughout part 656. The Department is also proposing to add the definition of Metropolitan Statistical Area (MSA) to this part to align with the definition in sections 655.5 and 655.103.
The Department proposes to delete the terms Nonprofessional occupation and Professional occupation from section 656.3, since this proposed rule does not distinguish between nonprofessional occupations and professional occupations in the filing and recruitment requirements sections.
The Department proposes to retain its current definition of the term Non-profit or tax-exempt organization, with one change. The Department proposes to delete the reference to section 656.40, as it could be misunderstood as limiting the definition to PWD purposes.
The proposed rule would add the term Offered wage in section 656.3. The proposed definition of Offered wage is the salary or wage, or salary or wage range, offered by the employer for the job opportunity. In addition, the definition clarifies that the offered wage must be included in the employer's Application for Permanent Employment Certification and advertisements and ( printed page 73927) must equal or exceed the highest of the prevailing wage or Federal, State, local minimum wage. The proposed definition is consistent with a similar definition at section 655.5 and conforms with other provisions in this proposed rule, such as section 656.17(e)(2)(iv) (advertisement content) and section 656.13(c) (offered wage assurances), where the Department discusses wage requirements further.
The Department proposes to retain the definitions of Office of Foreign Labor Certification and O*NET in section 656.3, with minor conforming changes.
The proposed rule also would add the term OFLC Administrator, defined as the primary official of OFLC, or, within the context of the regulation, the OFLC Administrator's designee. In addition, the Department proposes adding a definition of National Processing Center (NPC) to identify and clarify the term for the offices within OFLC out of which the COs operate and adjudicate Applications for Permanent Employment Certification.
The proposed rule would add the terms Permanent employment and Permanent labor certification to the definitions at section 656.3. Through the proposed definition of Permanent employment, the Department clarifies that a “permanent” job opportunity is employment of indefinite duration, in which an employee will ordinarily have an expectation of continued employment that will not end in the near, definable future. In addition, the definition clarifies that to establish a job opportunity offered in an Application for Permanent Employment Certification as “permanent,” an employer would be required to establish, at a minimum, that it: (1) has a bona fide need for a worker to fill a job opportunity whose services or labor are performed year-round, except for job opportunities such as members of the teaching profession and professional athletes, for which less than year-round performance is normal to the occupation; and (2) the employer will not require breaks or periods of inactivity, except where such periods stem from an established employer policy for leave that applies to other employees similarly employed by the employer. The Department invites comments on other occupations in which permanent employment does not require performance in all months of the year. The proposed definition of the term Permanent labor certification explains that it is a certification made by the OFLC Administrator for an employer seeking to file an Immigrant Petition with DHS to employ an alien under an immigrant visa, pursuant to section 212(a)(5)(A) of the Act, 8 U.S.C. 1182(a)(5)(A), and this part. The definition clarifies that the certification is based on the Application for Permanent Employment Certification and all supporting documentation.
The Department proposes adding a definition for Place of employment. The proposed definition aligns with a similar definition in section 655.5 and emphasizes that the actual work under the job order will be performed at the worksite(s) or physical location(s) indicated in the Application for Permanent Employment Certification.
In addition to adding “place of employment” as a defined term, the Department is considering changes to how this term is applied to Applications for Permanent Employment Certification both for job opportunities that will require work in various locations either throughout a geographic area or throughout the United States, where some locations cannot be anticipated at the time of filing, and which, currently, are commonly referred to as job opportunities for “roving” and “peripatetic” work (used synonymously herein), and for job opportunities covering modern concepts such as telework. The Department has determined that the current policy guidance does not adequately reflect the geographic area(s) where the job opportunity will be performed in some instances, such as for roving and telework employment where the worker reports to employer-designated worksite(s), routinely, to perform tasks but the location may change across a local or regional area. The current guidance does not allow the Department to fully assess U.S. worker availability and adverse impact on wages and working conditions in such scenarios.
The Department is considering new approaches, which may be adopted through this rulemaking, to better approximate the geographic area for roving and peripatetic job opportunities, and to notify U.S. workers who would be impacted by roving work, remote work, and telework job opportunities. There are various modern employment scenarios due to technological advancements, whether roving, remote, or telework (including hybrids thereof), for which the Department seeks better ways to identify the geographic area of such job opportunities for purposes of better apprising U.S. workers of the job opportunities available and further ensuring that the employment of aliens for such job opportunities will not adversely affect U.S. workers.
Employers have followed the 1994 policy guidance provided in Field Memorandum No. 48-94, Policy Guidance on Alien Labor Certification Issues, in which the then-ETA Administrator for Regional Management, Barbara Ann Farmer, instructed employers to indicate “various unanticipated locations throughout the U.S.” on the permanent labor certification application and use the employer's main or headquarters office for recruitment and other program requirements linked to the location where work is to be performed ( e.g., current requirements for PWD, NOF, job order placement with the SWA).[39] Currently, when completing the worksite information for the Application for Permanent Employment Certification, employers indicate “various unanticipated locations throughout the U.S.” on the application, and, also, identify the employer's main or headquarters location on the application. This has allowed the Department to collect some available details on job opportunities identified as involving unanticipated worksites throughout the U.S., and to base the wage assessment on the employer's main or headquarters location. Based on the Department's program experience in recent years, however, the employment scenarios that exist in the current labor market are not adequately covered by the current policy guidance and the employer's main or headquarters office may not always be the most appropriate physical location to use for PERM labor certification program purposes. The Department seeks better approaches to protect U.S. workers from adverse effects from these employment scenarios under the PERM program.
Place of Employment for Roving or Peripatetic Situations
For these jobs, the nature and requirements of the job's functions necessitate changes of worksite locations within the United States, likely frequent changes, and often of undetermined duration and scenario, to perform tasks or assignments for the employer either at the employer's own offices or worksite locations, and/or, at third-party or client locations at the employer's direction. This may include traveling to the employer's facilities to perform tasks, traveling to third-party sites or client sites to perform tasks, or other travel within set or varying geographic locations to perform work tasks or assignments for the employer. This, also, may include jobs for which the nature of the job opportunity requires travel to locations throughout the United States, whether local or non- ( printed page 73928) local, whether regional or national, from location to location to perform work for the job as a part of the worker's normal or routine duties, or the job duties require the employee to spend most work time at one location, but there is occasional travel for short or brief periods of work at other locations.
For a roving or peripatetic job opportunity under this NPRM, the Department proposes that the place of employment used for compliance with part 656 requirements would be the employer's nearest business office in the United States that is closest to where the work will be performed, not the location in the United States where the worker may reside, or travels from, to perform work, assignments, or tasks for the employer. Therefore, for completion of the Application for Prevailing Wage Determination, the employer would add the known physical address(es) of the employer's nearest business office(s) as the place(s) of employment or worksite(s), and for the Application for Permanent Employment Certification, for the place(s) of employment or worksite location(s). In addition, the employer would continue to add the phrase “various unanticipated locations throughout the U.S.” along with the physical address(es) of the employer's nearest business office(s) to the work to be performed. This would replace the current requirement from the Farmer memo where the employer adds to the Application for Permanent Employment Certification “various unanticipated locations throughout the U.S.” and adds the employer's main or headquarters address for the place of employment information. This proposed change is intended to better capture the geographic area or region for the work, to better apprise U.S. workers of the job opportunity, and to give better insight into any regional aspect(s) of the work to be performed, rather than listing only the employer's main or business headquarters. The collection of the nearest employer's office will, in many instances, provide U.S. workers with a better idea of the geographic area for travel or residence associated with the job duties. In such scenarios involving roving work, the employer would be required to provide all known places of employment ( i.e., the phrase “various unanticipated locations throughout the U.S.” and the employer's nearest business office(s) for the work to be performed) that the employer knows of at the time it initiates the PERM application process. For a roving or peripatetic job opportunity, the employer's obligations regarding the NOF, the prevailing wage, and working conditions would be based on the employer's nearest office location(s) to where the work will be performed.
As an example, if an employer has offices in Atlanta, GA and Charlotte, NC and the job opportunity is for roving work in locations in the State of Georgia, the employer would identify the physical address for its Atlanta, GA office location, and all known locations for the work to be performed, as the place(s) of employment on the Application for Prevailing Wage Determination and Application for Permanent Employment Certification as well as indicating “various unanticipated locations throughout the U.S.” on the Application for Permanent Employment Certification to comply with part 656 requirements. The Department proposes use of the employer's nearest office as the place of employment in efforts to reach as many U.S. workers as possible and inform U.S. workers of the geographic or regional area. Nonetheless, the Department recognizes that an employer's closest office may not be close— e.g., an employer may only have one office on the west coast, while the job opportunity is in Miami, FL, at a third-party client location. The Department invites comments from the public that provide practical approaches for identifying the place(s) of employment on the Application for Prevailing Wage Determination and Application for Permanent Employment Certification, where work cannot be easily tied to an employer's nearest office or geographic region.
Place of Employment for Remote Work and Telework (and Hybrid Telework) Situations
For purposes of this NPRM, a job opportunity is considered remote when the job opportunity will not be performed at the employer's office or worksite location(s), nor at a third-party client location where the worker is assigned, but, rather, will be performed offsite or virtually by the worker ( e.g., at the worker's residence, a co-working space, shared workspace, or another place of the employer or worker's discretion, that is not under the control or operation of the employer). Also, for purposes of this NPRM, a job opportunity is considered to be a telework (or a hybrid telework opportunity) when the employer divides some combination of work time for the job opportunity between tasks performed at the employer's office(s) or worksite location(s), place(s) of business, or third-party or client worksite(s), with offsite or virtual work ( e.g., at a worker's residence or a co-working space).
