For remote work, where the worker will not routinely travel to different locations to perform work, assignments, or tasks for the employer, and the work can be performed virtually at any fixed site location, the place of employment for purposes of the Application for Prevailing Wage Determination and the Application for Permanent Employment Certification would be the city, state, county ( i.e., county, borough, parish, township), ZIP code, and Bureau of Labor Statistics (BLS) area for the alien subject to the Application for Permanent Employment Certification. Therefore, on the Application for Permanent Employment Certification, for the place of employment or worksite location, the employer would add the alien's city, state, county ( i.e., county, borough, parish, township), ZIP code, and BLS area within the United States, rather than a residential address, or the employer's address. This would replace the current requirement to list a full address for a remote job opportunity. For remote work, the Department proposes to require the employer to post the NOF electronically, if there is no applicable bargaining representative to be notified. See discussion in proposed section 656.14. In addition, both the NOF and recruitment would be required to notify U.S. workers that the job opportunity is a remote job opportunity, without geographic restrictions on where in the United States the worker may reside.
The Department also recognizes that fully remote employment presents distinct and novel questions under the PERM program, including whether positions that do not require physical presence at an employer's worksite should qualify for labor certification. The Department is considering whether to exclude fully remote positions from eligibility for PERM labor certification to protect U.S. workers and advance the objectives of the program. The Department solicits comments on whether, and to what extent, fully remote positions should be eligible for certification, including whether a categorical exclusion, minimum onsite-work requirement, or other restriction would be appropriate. Fully remote positions also raise questions about the appropriate geographic basis for determining the prevailing wage, particularly where employees may work from anywhere in the United States. The Department solicits comments on whether the prevailing wage should be based on, or the highest of, the alien's location, the employer's nearest physical location, or another geographic ( printed page 73929) measure ( e.g., national prevailing wage), and what safeguards are most appropriate for apprising prospective U.S. applicants of the job opportunity and necessary to protect U.S. workers similarly employed in higher-wage labor markets.
For telework situations, where the worker splits time between the employer's worksite location and the worker's virtual telework site, but does not routinely travel to different employer locations or client-site or third-party locations to perform roving or peripatetic work, the place of employment for purposes of the Application for Prevailing Wage Determination and the Application for Permanent Employment Certification would be the physical location where the worker is expected to perform the job opportunity, rather than a residential address. The NOF and recruitment would be required to notify U.S. workers that the job opportunity is a telework opportunity in efforts to fully apprise U.S. workers of the applicable telework flexibilities.
Examples of Roving Employment and Peripatetic Work
Roving and peripatetic job opportunities may function as technicians doing brief work at different locations, or short-term consultants for third-party clients on a project-to-project basis, while others may require independent work within a third-party client's organization for longer term periods, based on a contract-for-services agreement. Some employers may assign a worker for rotations or assignments at its own location(s) or at third-party client's location(s), perhaps within a particular geographic area or region of the United States, or where the worker will provide services to a variety of third-party clients, while other job opportunities may assign a worker to an employer's location or a single third-party client site for an extended period. Some examples may include computer technicians, pharmaceutical sales representatives, consultants, sales representatives, plumbers, maintenance workers, and project engineers, etc.
Examples of Remote and Telework
For some job opportunities, employees may perform some or all of their job duties virtually. Workers who exclusively perform their job duties from their personal residence or shared or cooperative workspace are sometimes referred to as “remote workers.” Workers who perform their duties both on-site and from their personal residence or shared or cooperative workspace are generally referred to as “teleworkers.”
An example of a remote job opportunity is where the employer performs onboarding activities or orientation at the employer's location, but then, daily work is performed virtually at the worker's residence. The employee may relocate for reasons unrelated to projects or clients.
An example of telework is where the employer determines that a worker must report to the employer's office, or a third-party worksite, for three days per week and the worker can perform work virtually at the worker's residence two days per week, or some combination of virtual and in-person work. In telework cases, the employee may relocate when assigned to new projects or clients after the Application for Permanent Employment Certification is filed in order to commute to the new on-site location. In these scenarios the place where work is performed may be untethered from the employer's own office locations, including the employer's main or headquarters office, but is tethered to physical business address(es).
Prospective/Speculative Employment
While the NPRM proposes to update guidance for appropriately identifying and applying the place(s) of employment for the types of job opportunities discussed above to accommodate modern work scenarios and business advancements, the Department reminds employers that the job opportunity for which the employer seeks permanent labor certification must be a bona fide job. An Application for Permanent Employment Certification cannot be based on temporary employment, speculative employment, or a scenario where work does not yet exist.
The Department will question instances that appear to be contrived or abusive of the PERM program. Further, as is the case with the Department's H-1B program requirements, roving and peripatetic work must not be used as “strikebreaker” work ( i.e., placing aliens at worksites where U.S. workers are striking) to disadvantage U.S. workers.
Solicitation of Public Comments
The Department solicits public comments on how the NPRM's approaches for identifying the known place(s) of employment on Applications for Permanent Employment Certification for roving, remote and telework job opportunities can be improved to better apprise U.S. workers and to protect the wages and working conditions of similarly employed U.S. workers to support the Department's statutory obligations.
The Department's proposed rule includes the current term Prevailing wage determination (PWD) with revisions to simplify the definition. The definition removes references to Schedule A and sheepherder petitions, as the Department proposes to eliminate Schedule A and alternative procedures for sheepherders under this rule. The proposed definition continues to indicate the PWD is issued by an OFLC NPC. It clarifies that the PWD issued provides the prevailing wage for the job opportunity that is the subject of the Application for Permanent Employment Certification.
The Department proposes to relocate the definition of the term Professional Athlete from its current location in section 656.40 to the definitions at section 656.3.
The proposed rule retains, with minor changes, the term Purchase, defined as the transfer of ownership of a permanent labor certification application or certification from one person to another by voluntary act and agreement, based on valuable consideration, and term Sale, defined as agreement between two parties, called, respectively, the seller (or vendor) and the buyer (or purchaser) by which the seller, in consideration of the payment or promise of payment of a certain price in money terms, transfers ownership of a permanent labor certification application or certification to the buyer.
The proposed rule makes minor technical edits to the current definitions for Secretary of Homeland Security, Secretary of Labor, and Secretary of State. It also proposes technical edits to the definition of State Workforce Agency (SWA) to be consistent with the Department's H-2A regulations at 655.103.
The Department proposes to remove Specific Vocational Preparation (SVP) as a defined term. SVP is a component of the occupational information O*NET provides to the general public, which OFLC uses to assess whether an employer's job opportunity reflects duties, qualifications, and requirements that are normal for the occupation. The current definition of O*NET, which the Department proposes to retain in section 656.3, provides a link where interested parties can find additional information about O*NET, including SVP. By removing SVP as a defined term and deferring to current O*NET information, the Department can ensure that part 656 will stay consistent and current with the meaning and usage of SVP in O*NET.
The proposed rule adds the term Strike to section 656.3, using the same ( printed page 73930) definition as the Department uses at section 655.103. Strike would be defined as “[a] concerted stoppage of work by employees as a result of a labor dispute, or any concerted slowdown or other concerted interruption of operation (including stoppage by reason of the expiration of a collective bargaining agreement).”
The proposed rule would add the term Substantially comparable, as a defined term at 656.3. The Department's regulations currently address this term at section 656.17(i)(5)(ii) and state that a “substantially comparable” job or position is “a job or position requiring performance of the same job duties more than 50 percent of the time.” In the definition added at section 656.3, the Department proposes changes to the definition for clarity and improved identification of substantially similar jobs or positions. The definition would require a “substantially similar” job or position to share “more than a majority ( i.e., 51 percent or more) of the same duties performed.” In addition, the proposed definition would continue the CO's current practice of reviewing the duties disclosed in the Application for Permanent Employment Certification as the starting point for comparison. The proposed definition continues to notify the employer that additional documentation ( e.g., organizational charts and position descriptions, the amount and percentage of time spent performing the duties, payroll records) may be required to justify a determination that two jobs or positions are substantially comparable or not. The additional documentation, if necessary to the CO's assessment, would be submitted to the CO upon request.
For clarity, the Department notes that “substantially comparable” and “substantially equivalent” are distinct terms, used for distinct assessments. “Substantially comparable,” as discussed here, involves the CO's comparison of the duties of multiple jobs or positions to determine whether the jobs or positions are different, while “substantially equivalent”—addressed in current section 656.17(h)(4) and the proposed definition in section 656.19(d)(4)—involves the CO's assessment of an employer's stated primary and alternative minimum requirements for the job opportunity described in the Application for Permanent Employment Certification. The Department invites comments about how to further clarify these terms.
The proposed rule would revise the term United States and add the term Wages to align with definitions in sections 655.5 and 655.103. The Department also proposes to add U.S. Citizenship and Immigration Services (USCIS), an operational component of DHS, as a defined term.
For the term U.S. Worker, the Department proposes to reorganize and modify the current definition. As proposed, a U.S. worker for purposes of the permanent labor certification program would be a U.S. citizen or national of the United States; an individual who is lawfully admitted for permanent residence in the United States, is admitted as a refugee under 8 U.S.C. 1157, is granted asylum under 8 U.S.C. 1158.
To provide greater clarity regarding how the Department will administer and enforce program integrity provisions ( e.g., revocation, debarment) under this part, the proposed rule adds the terms Fraud, Material,Pattern or Practice, Willful, and Willful Misrepresentation. The Department proposes to define the term Fraud as a false representation of a material fact, or the concealment of a material fact, made with knowledge of its falsity and with the intent to deceive the Department, DHS, DOS, or another Government entity, or to obtain a benefit under this part or the Act to which the person is not entitled. The proposed definition for the term Material refers to a fact, statement, representation, or omission that has a natural tendency to influence, or is capable of influencing, a decision of the Department under this part or a decision of DHS or DOS on an Immigrant Petition or visa application that relies on a permanent labor certification, regardless of whether it actually influenced the decision. In circumstances where the Department could have discovered the truth through its own independent inquiry, a misrepresentation will not be considered immaterial. This proposed definition is consistent with Kungys v. United States, 485 U.S. 759, 770 (1988) (a “misrepresentation is material if it `has a natural tendency to influence, or was capable of influencing, the decision of' the decisionmaking body” (quoting Weinstock v. U.S., 231 F.2d 699, 701 (D.C. Cir. 1956)). The same test governs 18 U.S.C. 1001. United States v. Gaudin, 515 U.S. 506, 509 (1995). BALCA”) uses a similar standard, holding that whether “misrepresentations were material is whether they had a natural tendency to influence the decision of the [agency].” Bestkey Sols., Inc., 2014-PER-01248, slip op. at 4. (Apr. 6, 2018) (quotation omitted). However, reaching a conclusion that a material misrepresentation was made in the application does not require a change in the final adjudication of the case. Injeti v. U.S. Citizenship and Immigration Servs., 737 F.3d 311, 316 (4th Cir. 2013) (“finding that a misrepresentation is material does not require concluding that it necessarily would have changed the relevant decision”); United States v. Stelmokas, 100 F.3d 302, 317 (3d Cir. 1996) (“Materiality, after all, refers to the effect of a representation on a decision maker regardless of the nature of the decision. In other words, no matter what is being decided, the misrepresentation is material under Kungys if it has the natural tendency to affect the decision.”).
With regard to potentially noncompliant conduct by an employer, attorney, or agent that could lead to debarment under 656.31, the Department is proposing to define pattern or practice as a course of conduct in which the act or omission in question is repeated, routine, or regular, rather than an isolated, sporadic, or accidental act. Such conduct may be established involving two or more Applications for Permanent Employment Certification, whether filed by or on behalf of the same employer or different employers, and, in the case of an attorney or agent, by conduct across the applications of different clients. This proposed definition is based on principles in Int'l Bhd. of Teamsters v. United States, 431 U.S. 324, 336, 388 n.16 (1977) (construing Title VII § 707, 42 U.S.C. 2000e-6) noting that a pattern or practice is conduct that is “the regular rather than the unusual practice,” “more than the mere occurrence of isolated or `accidental' or sporadic . . . acts.”
In addition, the Department is proposing to define Willful as knowingly, deliberately, and voluntarily, or in reckless disregard of the requirements of this part or of the truth or falsity of a representation, as distinguished from an act or omission that is accidental, inadvertent, or merely negligent. In the Department's proposal, a person acts willfully with respect to a requirement of this part when the person knows of the requirement or shows reckless disregard for whether the person's conduct complies with it. The Department is proposing this definition for consistency with 20 CFR 655.805(c), which states that a “`willful failure' means a knowing failure or a reckless disregard with respect to whether the conduct was contrary to [the statute or regulations].” (citing McLaughlin v. Richland Shoe Co., 486 U.S. 128, 133 (1988) (“willful” refers to conduct that is “voluntary,” “deliberate,” or “intentional,” and not merely “negligent” and further confirms a willful violation occurs if an employer “either knew or showed reckless ( printed page 73931) disregard for the matter of whether its conduct was prohibited”); Trans World Airlines, Inc. v. Thurston, 469 U.S. 111, 128 (1985) (defining willfulness under the Age Discrimination in Employment Act to mean the employer “either knew or showed reckless disregard” for whether its conduct was prohibited)).
A willful misrepresentation is proposed as constituting a false representation of a material fact, made willfully or with reckless disregard, in connection with an Application for Permanent Employment Certification, a prevailing wage determination, or any other document, form, or communication submitted under this part. The Department also proposes to clarify that the intent to deceive is not an element of willful misrepresentation. Mwongera v. INS, 187 F.3d 323, 330 (3d Cir. 1999) (stating that the government “does not need to show intent to deceive; rather, knowledge of the falsity of the representation will suffice”) ; Forbes v. INS, 48 F.3d 439, 442 (9th Cir. 1995) (same). It is sufficient that the person knew the representation was false or made it with reckless disregard for its truth or falsity, and made it voluntarily. The proposed definition is consistent with Matter of Kai Hing Hui, 15 I&N Dec. 288, 289-90 (BIA 1975) (a misrepresentation is willful if made deliberately and voluntarily with knowledge of its falsity). “[T]he intent to deceive is no longer required before the wilful [ sic] misrepresentation charge comes into play.” Id. at 290.
Overall, the terms and definitions included in this proposal will clarify program requirements to improve employer compliance, strengthen program integrity, and enhance consistency across programs.
4. Proposed 20 CFR 656.4, Persons and Entities Authorized To File
The Department proposes adding a new section at 656.4 to address persons and entities authorized to file an Application for Permanent Employment Certification with OFLC and the employer's signature requirements, including when represented by an attorney or agent. The proposed new section would update and expand employer signature requirements in the PERM labor certification program, while providing consistency with the Department's H-2B program regulations at 20 CFR 655.7 and improving program integrity by requiring the employer to affirm its accountability for documentation submitted to the Department.
Proposed new paragraph (a) of section 656.4 clarifies that an employer may file the Application for Permanent Employment Certification or may authorize an agent or attorney, as defined at section 656.3, to do so on the employer's behalf. This paragraph would codify current program practices, as either the employer, or its agent or attorney, are allowed to file applications.
In paragraph (b), the Department proposes to require the employer to sign the Application for Permanent Employment Certification and all documentation submitted to the Department, regardless of whether the employer is represented by an attorney or agent. Requiring the employer to sign the Application for Permanent Employment Certification is a current practice under section 656.17(a)(1). Similarly, the employer's signature on the recruitment report is currently required under section 656.17(g)(1). Most employers would not experience a change in practice under this proposal, but those employers selected for audit or supervised recruitment, for example, would be required to sign all additional submissions to the CO.
Requiring employers to sign all documents submitted to the Department may expand signature requirements for some employers, but it does not expand program obligations. Under current section 656.10(b)(1), employers currently must attest to taking full responsibility for the accuracy of any representations made to the Department by an attorney or agent when signing the application form. Continuing current practice, the Department will not permit an agent or attorney to sign for the employer, even if the employer seeks to delegate authority to the agent or attorney, such as through a Power of Attorney or similar document. It has been established by BALCA that the employer is responsible for ensuring the accuracy of the application and all other submissions.[40] The Department proposes to codify a signature requirement on all documents submitted as a means of ensuring employer accountability and, thereby, improving program integrity.
5. Proposed 20 CFR 656.5, Requirements for Attorneys and Agents
As noted above, the Department proposes expanding subpart A to include sections 656.5, 656.6, and 656.7. In section 656.5, the Department proposes to repurpose the section that currently contains Schedule A, which is the only current provision in subpart B.[41]
The proposed section 656.5 addresses the requirements for agents and attorneys. Specifically, the Department proposes to address documentation requirements for agents and attorneys who file Applications for Permanent Employment Certification with OFLC on behalf of employers, as well as attorney and/or agent involvement in the consideration of U.S. workers during the PERM labor certification program's labor market test.
In paragraph (a), the Department proposes adding a new document retention requirement for agents and attorneys. Agents and attorneys would be required to retain documentation—an original agreement or other document—that demonstrates the employer authorized them to submit the Application for Permanent Employment Certification on the employer's behalf and otherwise represent the employer in connection with all aspects of that application. Adding this documentation requirement for agents is consistent with the Department's current document retention requirements for agents who participate in the H-2A and H-2B temporary labor certification programs, and expanding it to attorneys in the PERM program regulations would advance the Department's continued efforts to strengthen the integrity of the PERM program.[42] In recent years OFLC has experienced situations that have prompted the need to propose this requirement. For example, there have been PERM and/or PWD applications filed on behalf of employers that have not retained the professional services of attorneys or agents nor have authorized the filing of those applications. In addition, there have been other instances where OFLC has reviewed applications that fail to disclose information pertaining to attorneys and/or agents that have been retained by the employer or work for the employer for purposes of providing legal or professional services in connection with a PERM application.[43] These instances, along with other issues OFLC has experienced while processing applications, and the fact that requiring the retention of such agreements is an existing practice and requirement established for other programs, have ( printed page 73932) prompted the need to propose the establishment of this requirement in this program. The Department proposes to require agents and attorneys to retain this documentation for the same proposed record retention period as would apply to employers who file Applications for Permanent Employment Certification under proposed section 656.10(h)—a period of 5 years from the date on which the Immigrant Petition is approved, denied or withdrawn. Or, if the employer does not proceed to file an Immigrant Petition, agents and attorneys would be required to retain all records associated with the Application for Permanent Employment Certification for a period of 5 years from the expiration of the labor certification or from the date of denial or withdrawal.
To be clear, the Department is proposing to require that any agreement the employer and its agents or attorneys might have entered into for purposes of filing a PERM application be retained to be provided to the Department, upon request, in order to substantiate the nature and scope of its relationship with the agent and/or attorney and the services to be rendered, as it relates to the entire PERM recruitment and application process. OFLC is not proposing to regulate the content of these representation agreements, as that is determined and controlled by the parties involved; OFLC is merely proposing that such agreements be retained and produced upon request, because it assumes that an agreement was entered into in order for the employer to authorize its attorney or agent to file a PERM application on its behalf. Moreover, the scope of the agreement must be specific enough to clearly delineate the role and responsibilities of the attorney and/or agent to ensure the agent or attorney does not act in a manner that goes beyond what was permitted or authorized, such as the consideration of U.S. workers who apply for the job opportunity unless they qualify as the employer's representative, as provided in proposed section 656.5(b). OFLC wants to further remind employers that legal representation or securing an agent for purposes of filing an Application for Permanent Employment Certification is not required, and participation in the PERM program is voluntary.
For the PERM labor certification program, the agent or attorney would not be required to submit evidence of their authority to represent the employer when submitting the Application for Permanent Employment Certification to OFLC at the time of filing. Rather, the agent or attorney would be required to retain the evidence and be prepared to provide it to OFLC upon request, for example, in the event of an audit or if the need to request proof of representation arises while the CO is processing the application.
In addition, the Department proposes to relocate the provision that addresses alien, attorney, or agent involvement in considering U.S. workers who apply or are referred to the employer during the recruitment period, from current section 656.10(b)(2) to proposed section 656.5(b), without any substantive changes, apart from removing explanatory policy language from the proposed regulatory text to simplify the rule. The Department proposes to add a paragraph heading to 656.5(b), “Consideration of U.S. workers,” for clarity. The Department would continue to put attorneys and agents on notice in proposed paragraph (b)(1) that the alien, and agents or attorneys of the employer or the alien, may not participate in interviewing or considering U.S. workers for the job offered to the alien, with one exception stated in proposed paragraph (b)(2). The Department proposes to change the regulatory content relocated from current section 656.10(b)(2)(i) to proposed section 656.5(b)(1) by removing the policy statement below from regulatory text:
It is contrary to the best interests of U.S. workers to have the [foreign worker] and/or agents or attorneys for either the employer or [foreign worker] participate in interviewing or considering U.S. workers for the job offered the [foreign worker]. As the beneficiary of a labor certification application, the foreign worker cannot represent the best interests of U.S. workers in the job opportunity. The foreign worker's agent and/or attorney cannot represent the foreign worker effectively and at the same time truly be seeking U.S. workers for the job opportunity.
Although the Department's policy statement above remains in effect, removing it from regulatory text conforms with the Department's goal of simplifying the PERM labor certification regulations. As a result, proposed section 656.5(b)(1) continues to prohibit the alien and/or the alien's agent and/or attorney from interviewing or considering U.S. workers for the job offered to the alien, unless the agent and/or attorney is the employer's representative, as described in paragraph (2) of this section, while the preamble explains the reason the Department is retaining the prohibition in the proposed rule.
The Department continues to recognize in paragraph (b)(2) that, in some cases, an employer may have an agent or attorney who acts as the employer's representative in recruitment, normally, for job opportunities such as the one specified on the Application for Permanent Employment Certification. Only if the agent or attorney normally acts as the employer's representative in recruitment for such job opportunities when unconnected to labor certification may that agent or attorney be involved in interviewing or considering U.S. workers during permanent labor certification program recruitment.
6. Proposed 20 CFR 656.6, Successors in Interest
The Department proposes to add a new section to its PERM program regulations at 656.6 to address successors in interest to an employer, attorney, or agent who has violated PERM labor certification program requirements. The language proposed in this section mirrors the Department's current H-2A program regulations for successors in interest at 655.104. Specifically, this section proposes to define “successors in interest,” clarify liability for successors in interest, and explain the effect of an employer, attorney, or agent's debarment on a successor in interest. The Department has determined that this proposal, similar to the H-2A program regulations, would improve program integrity by enabling the Department to hold entities accountable for their misconduct.
Proposed paragraph (b) provides a non-exhaustive list of factors the Department would use to identify a successor in interest. These factors would enable the Department to compare and contrast key information for a new employer, attorney, or agent who files an Application for Permanent Employment Certification against information for a previous filer who violated program requirements, during the violator's debarment period. Key information encompassed in this assessment includes: substantial continuity of the same business operations; use of the same facilities; continuity of the workforce; similarity of jobs and working conditions; similarity of supervisory personnel; whether former management or ownership retains a direct or indirect interest in the new enterprise; similarity in machinery, equipment, and production methods; and similarity of products and services. In addition, the Department would consider whether the predecessor has the ability to provide relief for their violation(s). For purposes of debarment, the Department also would consider whether—and, if so, the extent to which—the employer, ( printed page 73933) attorney, or agent's ownership, management, supervisors, and others associated with them were involved in the violation(s) at issue. If, after engaging in this assessment, the Department were to find substantial continuity or similarity between the two, the Department would deem the new employer, attorney, or agent to be a successor in interest to the predecessor. As proposed, no one factor would be dispositive. All the circumstances would be considered as a whole.
Proposed paragraph (a) clarifies that successors in interest could be liable for the duties and obligations of the predecessor employer, agent, or attorney, regardless of whether they have succeeded to all the rights and liabilities of the predecessor. The Department emphasizes that this liability and enforcement standard is tailored specifically to prevent the evasion of PERM program obligations and debarment. It does not alter, displace, or satisfy the distinct substantive standards applied by DHS when determining whether an entity qualifies as a valid successor in interest for purposes of immigrant visa petition adjudication under Title 8 of the Code of Federal Regulations. In addition, proposed paragraph (c) would permit the CO to deny an Application for Permanent Employment Certification filed by or on behalf of a successor in interest of a debarred entity during the period of debarment, without review beyond the successor in interest assessment. The CO would issue the denial solely on the basis that it was filed by a successor in interest to a debarred employer, agent, or attorney. Similarly, OFLC would revoke a PERM labor certification issued to a successor in interest to a debarred employer, agent, or attorney solely on that basis. The employer, agent, or attorney would have the option to appeal such a denial or revocation, pursuant to the procedures for appeals of CO determinations in proposed section 656.26.
This proposal would bolster the Department's ability to impose sanctions on program violators. It would prevent debarred employers, attorneys, and agents from circumventing debarment by obscuring their identity through reincorporation, restructuring, or other means. The Department could look behind the name or FEIN on a new filing to assess the employer, attorney, or agent as a successor in interest to a debarred entity. Adding this section would improve program integrity and promote greater compliance with program requirements.
7. Proposed 20 CFR 656.7, Transition Procedures
The Department proposes to add a new section 656.7 to clarify which set of regulations—the 2004 PERM Final Rule,[44] as amended, or this proposed rule, once finalized—an employer would be required to satisfy when filing an Application for Permanent Employment Certification or Application for Permanent Prevailing Wage Determination with OFLC. In this section, the Department proposes to use the date an employer submits an Application for Permanent Employment Certification to determine which set of rules govern that application, meaning that an Application for Permanent Employment Certification or Application for Prevailing Wage Determination must satisfy the regulations applicable on the date submitted and OFLC will process the application in accordance with those regulations. This proposed transition approach would ensure that each Application for Permanent Employment Certification or Application for Prevailing Wage Determination is subject to only one regulatory framework.
The Department recognizes that an employer's compliance with program requirements begins before filing an Application for Permanent Employment Certification, with pre-filing recruitment of U.S. workers and obtaining the required PWD. Under current pre-filing recruitment requirements, an employer's recruitment compliance efforts could begin as much as 180 calendar days before the employer intends to file an Application for Permanent Employment Certification.[45] Requesting a PWD could begin earlier than 180 calendar days before filing the Application for Permanent Employment Certification or it could be the employer's last action before filing.[46] To provide continuity for employers who were already engaged in pre-filing recruitment activities on the date the Department publishes the new rule and an opportunity for both employers and OFLC to adjust to the new requirements, the Department is considering a transition period.
The Department is proposing a 90-day transition period—consisting of a 60-day delayed effective date plus an additional 30 calendar days for transition. The Department used this approach when publishing the current PERM labor certification program regulations and proposes the same approach for this proposed rule, once finalized. Using a 90-day transition period would support a smooth transition between current and new requirements. It would enable employers who had already begun pre-filing recruitment for compliance with the current rule to complete all required recruitment activities—including placement of a job order with the SWA—at least 30 calendar days before filing an Application for Permanent Employment Certification under the current regulatory framework. In addition, it would provide opportunity for the Department to engage with stakeholders seeking to understand the new rule, to train staff, and to prepare and troubleshoot systems for processing under the new rule.
As proposed, the new rule would go into effect 60 calendar days after publication, the Department would be granting 30 additional calendar days for transition from the old rule to the new rule, and all Applications for Permanent Employment Certification or Application for Prevailing Wage Determination filed on or after the 90th day after publication would be subject to the new rule. OFLC would review an Application for Permanent Employment Certification or Application for Prevailing Wage Determination submitted no more than 29 days after the effective date of the new rule using current regulations at part 656. Beginning 30 days after the effective date of the new rule, any Application for Permanent Employment Certification or Application for Prevailing Wage Determination filed with OFLC would be subject to the new rule.
The Department invites comments on the length of the proposed transition period, and ways to facilitate transition from the current regulations to the new rule, once finalized.
C. Discussion of Proposed Revisions to 20 CFR Part 656, Subpart B
The Department is proposing to eliminate the provisions for Schedule A in this subpart because it lacks the authority to establish an exception to the INA's labor market test; and, further, even if it possessed this authority the Department can no longer justify ( printed page 73934) continuing to waive the labor market test for jobs that it has been unable to prove are subject to a national shortage of qualified U.S. workers using a reliable, objective, and transparent methodology or prove should otherwise be exempted from the statutory labor certification requirement, apart from a determination from the Secretary of Homeland Security that it is in the national interest to waive the permanent job offer requirement.
During the mid-1960s, in an effort to reduce administrative processing delays associated with the admission of aliens for permanent employment, the Department first established, by regulation, a mechanism for pre-certifying jobs for which U.S. workers were purportedly in short supply nationwide, which became known as Schedule A of the PERM labor certification program.[47] Currently, Schedule A is set forth in the Department's PERM labor certification regulations at 20 CFR 656.5 and enumerates a list of occupations for which the Department predetermined that the statutory requirements have been met.[48] The occupations currently listed in Schedule A are divided into two groups. Group I consists of physical therapists and professional nurses; Group II consists of aliens who possess exceptional ability in the sciences, arts, or performing arts.[49] An employer seeking to hire an alien in an occupation listed in Schedule A is able to forego the labor market test for U.S. worker availability that is normally required for PERM labor certification. The employer bypasses filing an Application for Permanent Employment Certification with the Department and instead files an uncertified Application for Permanent Employment Certification directly with USCIS at the time the employer files its Form I-140 petition.[50]
Prior to 2004, the occupational listings in Schedule A were reviewed and modified multiple times to reflect changing economic and labor market conditions and to prevent adverse effects on the wages or working conditions of U.S. workers. Schedule A has been revised eight times, the last time in 2004.[51] The most recent revisions to Schedule A listings in 2004 only added aliens of exceptional ability in the performing arts to Group II; the other revision in 2004 was procedural and clarified the professional qualifications for eligible nurses under Schedule A.[52] The two occupations listed in Group I of Schedule A have been unchanged for over 30 years.
In addition, the Department has determined that the existence of Schedule A—both Group I and Group II—is incompatible with the plain language of the INA. Regarding Schedule A, generally, and Group I, specifically, the INA requires that before “ any alien” may be admitted into the United States to perform skilled or unskilled labor, the Secretary must certify that there are no qualified workers in the United States that are able and willing to perform the work. 8 U.S.C. 1182(a)(5)(A)(i)(I) (emphasis added). The INA does not exempt workers in a particular occupation from this requirement, nor does it provide the Department with the authority to exempt any occupations from this requirement. Had Congress wanted to empower the Department to establish an exemption from the labor certification requirement it would have clearly done so. Instead, the statute includes no such authority. To the extent that Group II implements the exception embedded in 1182(a)(5)(A)(i)(I) and defined in (A)(ii)—a slightly modified labor market test for members of the teaching profession and aliens with exceptional ability in the sciences or arts—such implementation is consistent with the statute.[53] The statute, however, does not exempt those occupations from the labor certification requirement.
Since Schedule A does not require the employer to file an Application for Permanent Employment Certification with OFLC, the Department does not have comprehensive data on how many employers utilize Schedule A and the types of work performed thereunder.[54] In response to growing stakeholder interest in modifying Schedule A and President Biden's October 2023 Executive Order 14110, Safe, Secure, and Trustworthy Development and Use of Artificial Intelligence, the Department published an RFI,[55] seeking public comment on evaluating the utility of expanding Schedule A to include STEM or non-STEM occupations, input on the appropriate data sources and methods for determining whether labor shortages exist, and if so, how the Department could establish a reliable, objective, and transparent methodology for identifying STEM and non-STEM occupations that are experiencing labor shortages and use that methodology to make regular updates to Schedule A. As previously discussed, the Department received over 2,000 comments in response to the RFI, none of which in whole or in part provided the Department with information and evidence upon which the Department could rely to establish a consistent and reliable methodology for regularly reviewing and updating Schedule A occupations. In the absence of such a methodology, the Department can no longer confirm that a nationwide shortage of workers exists to reasonably justify keeping the existing occupations in Schedule A or expanding Schedule A to other occupations—even assuming it had the authority to exempt certain occupations from the required labor market test, the Department has no reasonable basis upon which to determine that a national shortage of workers exists in any occupation. The RFI process solidified the Department's growing belief that Schedule A, first established as an administrative measure to reduce processing delays, should be eliminated and all job opportunities subject to part 656 must undergo a labor market test for the reasons explained below. Consequently, ( printed page 73935) the Department is unable to confidently conclude that Schedule A does not undermine INA section 212(a)(5)(A), 8 U.S.C. 1182(a)(5)(A), which requires a PERM labor certification from the Department confirming the Secretary's determination that there are insufficient U.S. workers who are able, willing, qualified and available for the job the alien seeks and employment of the alien will not adversely affect the wages and working conditions of U.S. workers in similar jobs.[56] Being unable to justify the continued presence of the current occupations on Schedule A, the Department likewise cannot justify continuing to waive recruitment for U.S. workers as it no longer possesses evidence that there is a nationwide shortage of U.S. workers able, willing, qualified, and available to work as physical therapists or professional nurses, as required for their placement on Schedule A. Further, the INA does not authorize the Department to make a finding of such a shortage or to use such a finding to exempt certain occupations from the requirements of the PERM program.
With regard to Schedule A, Group II, the Department further notes that even if a methodology could be identified to update Schedule A, the Department would still pursue elimination of Schedule A, Group II. Schedule A was intended for occupations for which the Department could identify a shortage of available U.S. workers, not individuals or classes of aliens.[57] Group II focuses on the aliens' qualifications and how the aliens possess exceptional ability. As written now, the regulation does not focus on the occupations, which as stated, was the primary concern for Schedule A.
It appears that the Department added Group II to Schedule A in connection with Congress' October 20, 1976 amendments to the INA at section 212(a)(5)(A)(ii), which required an “equally qualified” assessment for U.S. workers applying for positions requiring exceptional ability in the sciences or the arts, as a mechanism to route these applications to DHS, where DHS would then assess whether the alien possessed the exceptional ability claimed and compare the alien's qualifications to those of U.S. workers available for the job opportunity.[58] Continuing current practice, the Department will not assess the alien's “exceptional ability.” DHS has the authority and experience to assess whether the alien possesses “exceptional ability.” However, the Department has determined that it better serves and protects U.S. workers to first have employers seeking to employ aliens under current Schedule A Group II conduct a labor market test, with the CO assessing the employer's consideration of U.S. workers, and then allow DHS to make the “exceptional ability” determination, if the employer pursues an Immigrant Petition.
Even if the Department does not adopt its proposal to delete the content of section 656.15 in its entirety and reserve the section, some regulatory text would be deleted from 656.15, consistent with the Department's goal of eliminating regulatory text covering topics outside the scope of the Department's jurisdiction. For example, current sections 656.15(c) and 656.15(d) list documentation to be submitted to USCIS with the I-140 petition, for use in USCIS' assessment of whether the alien possesses the requisite exceptional ability. DHS addresses documentation requirements for USCIS' assessment of exceptional ability in its regulations at 8 CFR 204.5(k)(3)(ii). At best, sections 656.15(c) and (d) of the Department's regulations create confusion to the extent there are differences between those sections and DHS' regulations. At worst, they hamper USCIS' ability to assess exceptional ability claims. With the proposed elimination of section 656.15(c) and (d) content, USCIS' regulations at 8 CFR 204.5(k)(3)(ii) would clearly—and solely—govern.
While some employers may have relied on Schedule A to reduce burden when hiring aliens ( i.e., avoiding time and cost burden associated with pre-filing recruitment and OFLC's Application for Permanent Employment Certification review process), the Department also has determined that this reliance interest does not outweigh the Department's interest in rigorous adherence to the requirements of the INA and Congress' mandate to protect the wages and working conditions of U.S. workers.
Therefore, the Department asserts that a labor market test should be conducted for these job opportunities to satisfy the Department's obligations under the INA. The Department has experience assessing U.S. worker availability using the “equally qualified” standard of current section 656.24(b)(2)(ii), which the Department applies to pre-filing recruitment for college and university teacher positions. In this rule, as discussed below, the Department proposes to expand section 656.24(b)(2)(ii) to encompass pre-filing recruitment for job opportunities offered to aliens claimed to have exceptional ability in the sciences or the arts and eliminate Schedule A, Group II. Under this proposed rule, the employer would identify the job opportunity on the Application for Permanent Employment Certification as offered to an alien of claimed exceptional ability. The employer would conduct pre-filing recruitment following the requirements of proposed section 656.17 and prepare a recruitment report and all other supporting documentation demonstrating compliance with the requirements of proposed section 656.17(f) for submission with its Application for Permanent Employment Certification. OFLC would review the employer's pre-filing recruitment activities conducted pursuant to 656.17 and those recruitment results to assess the availability of U.S. workers at least as qualified as the alien. However, OFLC would not assess whether the alien qualifies for the EB-2 immigrant visa classification as an alien of exceptional ability; that determination resides with USCIS. If the employer files a Form I-140 Petition asserting exceptional ability under the EB-2 immigrant visa classification, and USCIS denies it after determining the alien's qualifications do not rise to the level of exceptional ability pursuant to the requirements in the relevant DHS regulations at 8 CFR 204.5(k), the employer could file a new Application for Permanent Employment Certification without the assertion of exceptional ability.
As a result, the Department is proposing to eliminate Schedule A for Group I, both at the current section 656.5, in related provisions at section 656.15, and elsewhere as necessary. Employers seeking to permanently employ aliens currently covered in Group I of the current Schedule A would follow the standard PERM labor certification process, including the pre-filing recruitment requirements of ( printed page 73936) proposed section 656.17, the filing instructions of proposed section 656.10, the processing requirements of proposed section 656.19, and all other PERM regulatory requirements that apply to all other occupations, without exception. As required by statute, a recruitment report and all other supporting documentation submitted for a job opportunity formerly classified under Schedule A, Group II, will be assessed under section 656.24(b)(2)(ii), using the same standard as that which applies to Applications for Permanent Employment Certification for college or university teachers, while a recruitment report for a job opportunity formerly classified under Schedule A, Group I, will be assessed under section 656.24(b)(2)(i), which applies to all other occupations.[59] The Department notes that aliens claiming to have exceptional ability in business are not currently listed in Schedule A, Group II, or in the statutory provision at 8 U.S.C. 1182(a)(5)(A)(ii), which establishes the alternative assessment for U.S. worker availability at section 656.24(b)(2)(ii); recruitment results for such job opportunities would continue to be subject to section 656.24(b)(2)(i).
In light of the foregoing, the Department proposes to eliminate the provision that classifies aliens of exceptional ability, defined in Group II of current section 656.5, under the umbrella definition of Schedule A. Employers seeking to permanently employ aliens currently covered in Group II of the current Schedule A would follow the standard PERM labor certification process ( i.e., proposed sections 656.10, 656.17, and 656.19, and all other PERM regulatory requirements that apply to all other occupations); however, the recruitment report submitted for a case currently covered in Group II will be assessed under section 656.24(b)(2)(ii), as required by the INA, using the same standard that applies to Applications for Permanent Employment Certification for college or university teachers.[60]
As the Department has determined that there is no need to classify either Group I or Group II under Schedule A and is proposing to delete these provisions, the Department further proposes the deletion of the current section 656.5 of part 656, subpart B in its entirety. The Department's proposal would result in subpart B not containing any regulatory sections, so the Department proposes to reserve current part 656, subpart B in full for future rulemaking.
The elimination of Schedule A would enable the Department to better ensure that U.S. workers have an opportunity to apply for available job opportunities, and adhere to the statutory requirements to protect U.S. workers assigned to it, as intended by Congress under the INA.
D. Discussion of Proposed Revisions to 20 CFR Part 656, Subpart C
In subpart C, the Department proposes adding two new sections at 20 CFR 656.13 and 656.14. The Department is also proposing to delete the language currently contained in sections 656.15 and 656.16, so they can be reserved. Under this proposed rule, several sections of subpart C would be retained but repurposed and/or revised, as discussed below.
1. Proposed 20 CFR 656.10, Application Filing Requirements
The Department proposes to clarify the process for filing an Application for Permanent Employment Certification, in part, by reorganizing the PERM labor certification program's regulatory framework to reflect program requirements by processing stage, rather than by occupation. This approach is consistent with the Department's existing H-2A and H-2B temporary labor certification program regulations and will help clarify and simplify the PERM labor certification program regulations.[61] In keeping with this reorganizational approach, the Department proposes to centralize the filing requirements for Applications for Permanent Employment Certification at section 656.10, as discussed below. In addition, the Department proposes certain changes to the information and documentation required at the time of filing and modifications to regulatory language in conformity with other proposals in this rulemaking. The filing requirements proposed in section 656.10 would apply to all employers seeking to permanently employ an alien in a job opportunity that requires a PERM labor certification, regardless of occupation. These proposed changes are explained below.
To accomplish the centralization of filing requirements in section 656.10, the Department proposes to move paragraphs both out of and into section 656.10. The proposed rule would move the current paragraphs (b) (representation), (c) (attestations), and (d) (notice) out of section 656.10, as they address topics other than filing requirements. The content of these paragraphs would be relocated and addressed in other sections of the proposed rule, including sections 656.3, 656.4(b), 656.5(b), 656.13, and 656.14. Under the proposed rule, regulatory content that would move into revised section 656.10 include language currently provided for under: sections 656.17(c) (filing date); 656.17(l) (documentation related to alien influence over the job opportunity); and 656.19(b) (documentation for live-in household domestic service workers). Following these organizational changes, proposed section 656.10 will focus only on filing requirements and will also address the related topics of filing date, submission of incomplete applications, and record retention.
Specifically, in proposed paragraphs (a) (What to file) and (d) (Original signature) of section 656.10, the Department addresses what all employers would be required to submit to OFLC. Consistent with current practice, proposed sections 656.10(a)(1) and 656.10(d) would require the employer to submit a complete Application for Permanent Employment Certification with original signature(s) [62] that includes a PWD obtained in advance pursuant to section 656.40 [63] and information provided in all other required fields. Proposed section 656.10(a)(1) highlights certain key employer information that must be provided ( i.e., legal name(s), trade name(s), valid FEIN, valid place of business [physical location] in the United States, and a means by which prospective U.S. applicants may contact the employer for employment) on the Application for Permanent Employment ( printed page 73937) Certification. In the current rule, the definition of Employer in section 656.3 requires the employer to have a FEIN and “a location within the United States to which U.S. workers may be referred for employment” and the Form ETA-9089 and instructions identify those, plus employer name, trade name (if applicable), and contact information for the employer, as required. To improve clarity that this key employer information is required at the time of filing—collected through required form fields—the Department proposes to include this content in proposed section 656.10(a)(1) and add legal name, trade name(s), and means by which prospective U.S. applicants may contact the employer for employment to proposed section 656.10(a)(1), consistent with required form fields and H-2A program regulations.[64] If the employer does not use a trade name, “N/A” as an affirmative response is required, consistent with current form instructions.
In proposed sections 656.10(a)(2) through (4), the Department proposes additional records and documentation that employers must submit concurrently with the Application for Permanent Employment Certification. The proposal would change both the timing and frequency of employers' submission to OFLC of records and documentation demonstrating compliance with its notice to and consideration of U.S. workers.
For recruitment-related documentation, the current rule requires that employers retain copies of records and documentation to show compliance with pre-filing recruitment requirements ( e.g., copies of advertisements and resumes for U.S. workers who applied for the job opportunity) and a recruitment report describing the recruitment steps undertaken and the results achieved, including the lawful, job-related reason for not hiring any of the U.S. workers who applied. See current sections 656.10(f) and 656.17(g)(1). The current rule does not require employers to submit this documentation to the NPC, unless requested by the CO.
Similarly, employers prepare and retain evidence of the notice they provide to employees of the filing of the Application for Permanent Employment Certification —through bargaining representative(s) or posted notice to employees—and information about contacting DOL with information about the application, under the current rule. See current sections 656.10(d) and (f). For employers not subject to supervised recruitment, the employer provides or posts the NOF before filing the Application for Permanent Employment Certification and retains evidence of it for submission, upon request by the CO. If the CO imposes supervised recruitment during application processing, the CO may order the employer to update the NOF with revised information, consistent with the CO's ordered content for additional advertising.
For employers subject to supervised recruitment when filing future Applications for Permanent Employment Certification under the current rule, the CO instructs the employer not to provide or post the NOF until the CO has an opportunity to approve its content, along with advertising content, during the supervised recruitment process. While this practice ensures the NOF contains correct information, the Department has determined that delaying NOF posting until after filing the Application for Permanent Employment Certification is contrary to statute, which requires NOF at the time of filing.[65] Even if the initial NOF posted contains imperfect information, which the CO orders the employer to revise during supervised recruitment, the pre-filing NOF informs employees that the employer is filing an Application for Permanent Employment Certification and ensures that employees have an opportunity to provide information or concerns to DOL about the job opportunity described in the NOF. The proposed rule would require the notice to the bargaining representative(s) or NOF posting to begin within 10 business days before the employer files the Application for Permanent Employment Certification in all cases. See proposed sections 656.10(a)(2) and 656.14(f).
The Department also proposes to add clarifying language in proposed section 656.21(c)(7) to put employers subject to supervised recruitment on notice that the CO may require them to update the NOF during processing with revised content.
In addition, proposed section 656.10(a)(2) would require all employers to submit documentation demonstrating their compliance with NOF requirements concurrently with the Application for Permanent Employment Certification. Similarly, proposed sections 656.10(a)(3) and (4) would require employers to submit documents and records evidencing compliance with all pre-filing recruitment requirements, including resumes and applications for U.S. workers who applied for the job opportunity, concurrently with the Application for Permanent Employment Certification.[66]
Requiring submission of the NOF, documentation of recruitment activity and results, and the recruitment report prior to the issuance of a PERM labor certification decision will enhance transparency about the employer's efforts to conduct a good faith test of the domestic labor market and bolster program integrity efforts, with minor impact to burden. Further, the proposed change would strengthen the incentives for employers to comply with program requirements and ensure the Department's final determinations are more frequently based on actual evidence of a lack of qualified U.S. workers. While currently all employers must prepare and retain this documentation, only some employers are required to submit it to the NPC before the CO issues a final determination. Under the current rule, the CO certifies applications in the vast majority of circumstances without having reviewed the employer's consideration of U.S. workers; the CO relies on the employer's attestations alone. As proposed, the CO has discretion to review some or all recruitment reports prior to issuance of a final determination. In the event a recruitment report raised concerns regarding the employer's compliance with recruitment requirements or consideration of U.S. workers, the CO could review the supporting documentation ( e.g., copies of advertisements, U.S. worker resumes and applications) to further assess the content, location, and timing of the employer's advertising of the job opportunity and its consideration of the U.S. workers who applied or were referred for the job opportunity, without unnecessary correspondence or subjecting the application for full audit examination. Thus, the Department expects that this additional review of readily-available documents will enable the CO to reach a final determination based on evidence, as opposed to attestations, without additional processing. For example, if the employer's advertisements included a lower wage offer or higher qualifications than indicated on the Application for ( printed page 73938) Permanent Employment Certification, the application could be denied on that basis without a lengthier audit examination process.
The Department also would like to emphasize that employers are already required to prepare the NOF and recruitment reports and maintain copies of them, along with supporting documentation, to be provided to the CO upon request, so no additional burden would result from the proposed change to the requirements beyond the burden associated with uploading that documentation in the FLAG system at the time the employer files the application, which, in the Department's opinion, constitutes a de minimis burden that results in a negligible burden increase.
In addition, this proposal would increase consistency across the Department's temporary and permanent labor certification programs, while accommodating program differences and supporting processing efficiency. For example, similar to the H-2B and CW-1 temporary labor certification programs, this proposal would require submission of the employer's recruitment report for CO review before the CO issues a final determination.[67] However, the timing of recruitment report submission before final determination would be different, based on when required recruitment activities are completed—pre-filing for the PERM labor certification program and during application review for the H-2B and CW-1 programs. Because recruitment—and a final recruitment report—must be completed pre-filing, requiring submission of the employer's recruitment report at the time of filing the Application for Permanent Employment Certification is an appropriate programmatic difference, which furthers the Department's processing efficiency goals. As proposed for the PERM labor certification program, the CO would be able to review employers' NOFs and recruitment reports—and related supporting documentation—before reaching final determinations without additional, unnecessary correspondence.
Timeliness for Filing
The Department is proposing a single filing timeframe requirement for submission of Applications for Permanent Employment Certification to OFLC in proposed section 656.10(b). An employer would be required to submit the complete Application for Permanent Employment Certification, with all documentation and information required at the time of filing, within 30 calendar days after the employer completes all pre-filing recruitment requirements. Together with proposed section 656.17(d), this would mean an employer must complete all recruitment activities between 30 to 60 calendar days before the employer expects to file the Application for Permanent Employment Certification and then file the application between 1 and 29 calendar days after the last recruitment activity ends. This 30-day timing requirement would apply to both employers who engage in the pre-filing recruitment procedures set forth in proposed section 656.17 and employers who engage in the optional pre-filing recruitment procedures for college and university teachers or professional athletes and professional sports team coaches set forth in proposed section 656.18. Currently, employers filing under section 656.17 must submit the application within 180 calendar days of beginning pre-filing recruitment activities and NOF posting, while employers filing under section 656.18 must do so within 18 months of the alien's selection, which is often many months before the actual filing of the application.[68]
The Department proposes this filing timeframe change to simplify part 656 and better provide the CO with more current information about the availability of U.S. workers for job opportunities that are the subject of an Application for Permanent Employment Certification. Under the current rule, an employer's recruitment and recruitment results can become stale before the employer submits its Application for Permanent Employment Certification. The Department considers the current timeframes—180 calendar days and 18 months in current sections 656.17 and 656.18, respectively—longer than necessary to enable employers to prepare their recruitment reports after completing pre-filing recruitment requirements. The Department considers the proposed 30-day timeframe after completion of pre-filing recruitment to be a reasonable period for preparation of recruitment reports for submission with the Application for Permanent Employment Certification and those recruitment reports would provide better information about the current availability of U.S. workers to the CO. In addition, as proposed, employers would file the Application for Permanent Employment Certification in closer proximity to the labor market test, which would ensure that U.S. workers who may have availability for the job opportunity are made aware of the job in closer proximity to the filing of the Application for Permanent Employment Certification, rather than many months ahead of time, and the recruitment report provided to the CO reflects current labor market results.
The Department invites comments on the proposed filing timeframe requirement that is consistent with the Department's goal of simplifying part 656 and ensuring recruitment activities and the report of recruitment results are not stale when submitted to the NPC. In particular, the Department invites comments about whether the 30-day timeliness requirement should be applied to Applications for Permanent Employment Certification filed using the proposed competitive recruitment and selection process of proposed section 656.18 based on the last advertisement date or the date of the organizational or administrative body's final report making the recommendation or selection of the alien. See proposed section 656.18(a)(2).
Location and Method of Filing
In paragraph (c), the Department proposes to require electronic filing for Applications for Permanent Employment Certification submitted to OFLC, except in limited circumstances. This proposal does not constitute a change for those employers who file Applications for Permanent Employment Certification using the Department's FLAG system, available at https://flag.dol.gov/​. FLAG continues to be the OFLC Administrator's designated electronic filing method for Applications for Permanent Employment Certification. Consistent with the Department's approach when mandating electronic filing in temporary labor certification programs, the Department proposes similar procedures in the PERM labor certification program under paragraphs (c)(2) and (3), through which an employer may request authorization to file by mail by demonstrating to the OFLC Administrator that they are unable or limited in their ability to use or access electronic filing. An employer may contact the NPC in writing to the address designated in a notice published in the Federal Register , or for individuals with hearing or speech impairments, 1-877-889-5627 (this is the TTY toll-free Federal Information Relay Service number) for assistance in using, accessing, or filing any form or documentation required under this part, including the Application for Permanent Employment Certification. ( printed page 73939) This proposal—and the parallel proposal in section 656.40(a)(2) to require electronic filing for Applications for Prevailing Wage Determination —would bring consistency across the foreign labor certification and labor condition application programs administered by the Department. If adopted, employers would submit all applications electronically to OFLC through the FLAG system, unless a limited exception applied.
Based on extensive experience administering permanent and temporary labor certification programs, the Department has determined that mandating electronic filing reduces costs ( e.g., mailing fees) and burdens for most employers and for the Department, reduces the frequency of delays related to filing applications, improves the quality of information collected, promotes administrative efficiency and accountability, and increases consistency across labor certification programs. For employers and their authorized representatives, the Department's proposal to require electronic filing would improve customer experience by permitting more prompt processing of applications and reducing paperwork burdens and mailing costs. Electronic filing provides system prompts, which include warnings that an application is incomplete or obviously inaccurate and provides employers with an immediate opportunity to correct the errors or upload the missing documentation. Mandatory electronic filing furthers efficiency in communications between the Department and the employer and its representatives.
The Department anticipates that requiring e-filing will not require a change of practice for the vast majority of employers. Based on fiscal year (FY) 2021-FY 2025 data, approximately 99.61 percent of PERM labor certification applications were filed electronically.[69] Almost all of the remaining 0.39 percent of PERM labor certification applications filed by mail also disclosed valid email addresses on the application form, thereby suggesting that employers and, if applicable, their authorized attorneys and agents have access to the internet and are likely capable of filing electronically.
Additional Documentation Required for Certain Applications
After eliminating the content of current paragraph (e), as discussed below, the Department proposes to repurpose paragraph (e) to address additional documentation requirements for certain job opportunities. The Department proposes to relocate the content of current sections 656.19(b) (documentation for live-in household domestic service workers) and 656.17(l) (documentation related to alien influence over the job opportunity) to proposed sections 656.10(e)(1) and (e)(2), respectively, and add new regulatory content in proposed section 656.10(e)(3) to codify, with minor changes, the current practice for employers seeking to permanently employ professional athletes or professional sports team coaches, who meet the definition set forth in section 656.3.
After relocating the content of current section 656.19(b) to proposed section 656.10(e)(1), the Department proposes non-substantive changes for readability and changes to simplify documentation, while better ensuring the employment contract fully discloses all appropriate information about live-in household domestic service jobs offered. Under the current rule, the employer must prepare and retain both a statement describing the household living accommodations, which contains the information listed in current section 656.19(b)(1), and an employment contract, which contains information listed in current section 656.19(b)(2). The proposed rule would eliminate the separate statement describing household living accommodations and relocate its required content to the employment contract, resulting in one document that contains all required information about the job offered. This proposal simplifies documentation requirements, while better ensuring that the alien is apprised of the household living accommodations information. In addition, the proposed rule would reduce the number of copies of the employment contract the employer must retain. Currently, an employer seeking to permanently employ an alien in a live-in household domestic service job opportunity must retain two copies of the employment contract, but the proposed rule would only require retention of one.[70]
The Department's proposed section 656.10(e)(1)(i) eliminates two key pieces of information from the content required of employment contracts for live-in household domestic service job opportunity ( i.e., the wage offer and description of duties required under current section 656.19(b)(2)(i) and (v), respectively). However, the two pieces of information are still required. The proposed language that introduces the enumerated list in proposed section 656.10(e)(1)(i) states that the employment contract must “contain[ ], at a minimum, the same material terms of employment specified on the Application for Permanent Employment Certification and the following information and employment guarantees.” By requiring the employment contract to contain the same material terms of employment as the Application for Permanent Employment Certification, the Department ensures not only that the employment contract will continue to contain the wage offer and description of duties, but also any other material terms of employment the employer specified on the Application for Permanent Employment Certification, which could not be adequately captured in a static list. Moreover, through this approach, the Department would better ensure that the employment contract contains all essential information and is consistent with the Application for Permanent Employment Certification used to test the U.S. labor market.
Proposed section 656.10(e)(2) retains content of the additional documentation requirements found in current section 656.17(l) (Alien influence and control over job opportunity) with one change to simplify the rule by eliminating redundant language and, otherwise, only minor changes to conform with terminology used throughout this proposed rule. Proposed section 656.10(e)(2) eliminates the phrase “must be able to demonstrate the existence of a bona fide job opportunity, i.e., the job is available to all U.S. workers,” which appears in current section 656.17(l). This is not a requirement limited to job opportunities involving closely held corporations or partnerships in which the alien has an ownership interest; or familial relationships between the stockholders, corporate officers, incorporators, or partners, and the alien; or the alien as one of a small number of employees. It is a requirement applicable to all Applications for Permanent Employment Certification under both the current and proposed rules. The current rule addresses the requirement in section 656.10(c)(8) as well as section 656.17(l), while the proposed rule addresses this requirement in proposed section 656.13(a).
Under proposed section 656.10(e)(3), employers filing Applications for Permanent Employment Certification for professional athletes or professional ( printed page 73940) sports team coaches would be required to prepare and maintain the following documentation, consistent with longstanding practice: (1) a copy of the executed written contract, signed and dated by the employer and alien, covering the entire year or period that indicates the specific payment(s) made to the alien; (2) an employment letter stating that the alien will work a minimum of ten (10) hours per week during the off-season period; and (3) for an alien being sponsored for permanent employment as a professional athlete, a copy of a letter or response, whether electronic or print, from the Players' Association ( e.g., NBA, NHL, NFL, MLB) or applicable trade union(s) stating that it was provided notice from the employer, as required by section 656.14(a)(1), and has no objection to the filing of the Application for Permanent Employment Certification. The Department recognizes that Players' Association and trade union(s) may vary in their responsiveness to an employer's notice and request for confirmation of “no objection” to the filing of an Application for Permanent Employment Certification. The Department invites comments about whether and how an employer could document compliance with this notice and request requirement, in the absence of a response from the Players' Association or applicable trade union(s).
The Department does not propose to change the timing of submission for this additional documentation.
