Employers filing applications subject to the additional documentation requirements under proposed section 656.10(e) would continue to prepare and maintain the additional documentation required for the job opportunity in their record retention file and only submit it upon request. However, by locating the additional documentation requirements in proposed section 656.10, the Department clarifies that—both currently and under the proposed rule—preparation and retention of the additional documentation is a filing requirement, meaning it must be prepared and already saved in the employer's record retention file at the time of Application for Permanent Employment Certification submission. No new burden would be incurred by employers as a result of this change.
Filing Date
The Department proposes to relocate the filing date provision currently in section 656.17(c) to the centralized filing requirements in proposed section 656.10(f), with modifications to conform with other proposed regulatory changes herein. The proposed paragraph continues to deem the date OFLC accepts for processing a complete Application for Permanent Employment Certification to be the “filing date.” For an electronically filed application, the “filing date” is the date the application is submitted and accepted in the electronic system based on the time of acceptance in the Eastern time zone of the United States. For an application submitted by mail under the procedures proposed at section 656.10(c)(2) and (3), the “filing date” would be the postmark date affixed to the employer's Application for Permanent Employment Certification submission, provided that the NPC determines the application is complete. This would be a change for applications submitted by mail. Currently, the filing date is the date the NPC accepts the application for processing and affixes a date stamp on the submission. However, the current approach is less straightforward than establishing the filing date using the postmark and delay between delivery and affixing a date stamp has resulted in denials for noncompliance with timing requirements ( e.g., recruitment dates within the 180 days prior to the filing date) that BALCA has overturned.[71] In the interest of simplicity, efficiency, and certainty, the Department proposes to use the postmark date as the filing date for complete applications submitted by mail.
Completeness
Current section 656.17(a)(1) provides that incomplete applications will be denied. In the proposed section 656.10(g), the Department proposes, instead, to return incomplete applications to employers unprocessed.
Under the proposed rule, a complete application consists of a Form ETA-9089, Application for Permanent Employment Certification, with all required fields completed with valid information, a valid PWD, and the employer's recruitment report for the job opportunity offered on the Application for Permanent Employment Certification, as outlined in proposed section 656.10(a). Despite form instructions and FLAG system prompts designed to ensure employers submit complete Applications for Permanent Employment Certification, the Department receives applications missing required information. The Department has received applications where the employer overrode system prompts or entered nonsensical or placeholder information ( e.g., the employer's point of contact as a movie or cartoon character or “TBD” (to be determined) or “Test Subject” as the alien's name) in order to circumvent system prompts meant to ensure only complete, valid applications are submitted. The Department anticipates that it will continue to receive such applications, which slow processing of legitimate, complete applications filed by employers with a genuine need for workers. With this rule's proposed addition of the employer's recruitment report and other recruitment related documentation as an application filing requirement, the Department also anticipates that an unscrupulous employer may try to circumvent these requirements by uploading into FLAG something other than their recruitment documentation to likewise evade system checks. Without, for example, a valid recruitment report or documentation demonstrating compliance with the advertising requirements or a signed Form ETA-9089, Appendix E, the application will be deemed incomplete.
Under the proposed rule, incomplete Applications for Permanent Employment Certification submitted to the NPC will not be processed. Rather, when an incomplete Application for Permanent Employment Certification is discovered—which would occur when the application is assigned to an analyst for review, as provided in proposed section 656.19(a)—the NPC would return the application unprocessed. As the CO will not reach any substantive determinations before returning an incomplete application unprocessed, proposed section 656.10(g) clarifies that an employer cannot request reconsideration or review pursuant to section 656.26 after an application is returned by the NPC unprocessed due to incompleteness. In addition, the “filing date” of the incomplete application would not be retained and cannot be claimed for purposes of establishing a priority date. Rather, the employer may file a new Application for Permanent Employment Certification that is complete and satisfies all requirements of part 656 and the filing date would be the date the NPC accepts the new complete application for processing, in accordance with proposed section 656.10(f).
The Department recognizes that the proposed consequences for submission of an incomplete application are more strict than under the current rule. However, in light of the Administration's broad efforts to combat visa fraud and abuse and ensure the broadest pool of qualified U.S. workers ( printed page 73941) have fair access to these job opportunities before a permanent labor certification is granted, the Department considers the consequences appropriate to safeguard limited resources and facilitate the processing of complete, genuinely filed applications.
Likewise, recognizing the consequence of returning an application unprocessed, the Department only intends to exercise this authority in situations where it is clear that the application is incomplete. For example, when a document other than the recruitment report is submitted, when the included PWD is invalid, when the employer is listed as a fictional entity, or when required fields include placeholders like “TBD.” This provision is not intended to allow the Department to make substantive denials without permitting reconsideration or review by BALCA. The provision is intended to permit OFLC to avoid spending time reviewing and drafting denials for applications which fail to meet the basic standards of a legitimate, complete application.
Record Retention Requirements
The Department proposes to retain the employer's record retention requirements, but it is seeking to relocate this provision from current section 656.10(f) to proposed section 656.10(h), with a change to the timeframe that would be required for employers to retain all documentation. Currently, employers are required to retain a copy of the Application for Permanent Employment Certification and all supporting documentation to demonstrate compliance with program requirements for a 5-year period, with the 5-year period beginning on the date the Application for Permanent Employment Certification was filed. The Department proposes to retain the 5-year period of retention, but to count the 5-year period of retention from the date on which the Immigrant Petition is approved, denied, or withdrawn. If the employer does not proceed to file an Immigrant Petition, it would be required to retain all records associated with the Application for Permanent Employment Certification for a period of 5 years from the expiration of the labor certification or the date of denial or withdrawal.
The current record retention timeframe overlaps with application processing timeframes and, in doing so, it fails to provide a full 5-year record retention period. By starting the record retention clock with the final determination for denied or withdrawn applications or the expiration of a certified application, or from the date on which the Immigrant Petition, if any, is approved, denied, or withdrawn, essential records will be available for the full period intended, which allows the Department, and other partner agencies, to request and obtain these records, if necessary, in order to conduct further review, investigations or any integrity actions.
Applicability of Section 656.10
The Department reiterates for clarity that the filing procedures proposed in section 656.10 would apply to all employers required to obtain a PERM labor certification from OFLC for purposes of submitting an I-140 petition to USCIS. This would include job opportunities currently covered under Schedule A and certain sheepherders, consistent with the Department's proposals to eliminate current alternative regulatory procedures at sections 656.15 (Schedule A) and 656.16 (Sheepherders).
With this proposed change, employers seeking to permanently hire aliens to perform job opportunities currently defined as Schedule A or as qualifying sheepherders under part 656 would continue to obtain a PWD from OFLC but, going forward, also would be required to engage in pre-filing recruitment and submit their application to OFLC, rather than to USCIS, along with their recruitment report.
Similarly, the filing requirements in proposed section 656.10 would apply to employers seeking to permanently employ aliens as professional athletes or professional sports team coaches in job opportunities subject to the EB-2 or EB-3 visa classifications requirements, including those currently covered by Schedule A, which would require a PERM labor certification under this proposed rule.[72]
Eliminated Provision
The Department proposes to eliminate section 656.10(e) of the current rule in its entirety. The proposed elimination of current section 656.10(e) is consistent with the Department's goal of simplifying its PERM program regulations.
Current section 656.10(e)(1) states that the public may submit evidence to the CO that may have bearing on an Application for Permanent Employment Certification. This provision duplicates the function of the NOF, which similarly invites any person to provide information or other documentation bearing on an Application for Permanent Employment Certification to OFLC for consideration in reaching the determinations required for PERM labor certification. See proposed section 656.14(d). The type of information or other documentation to be submitted to OFLC using the contact information provided in the NOF is the same as that described in current 656.10(e)(1)—information on available workers, information on wages and working conditions, and information on the employer's failure to meet the terms and conditions for the employment of aliens and similarly employed workers. In addition, as discussed below in connection with the Department's proposed expansion of NOF visibility, the proposed elimination of current section 656.10(e)(1) would simplify the regulation without impact to program integrity.
The Department also proposes to eliminate current section 656.10(e)(2), which discusses the submission of evidence to DHS. Although the Department is aware that DHS accepts and considers such information, the elimination of current section 656.10(e)(2) is consistent with the Department's intent to simplify part 656 by eliminating regulatory content that discusses another agency's processes.
2. Proposed 20 CFR 656.11, Substitutions and Modifications to Applications
In this rulemaking, the Department proposes to retain current section 656.11's prohibitions on substituting aliens and modifying Applications for Permanent Employment Certification, with revisions to update terminology, eliminate outdated language, and improve clarity. For example, proposed revisions replace “application” with “ Application for Permanent Employment Certification,” among other minor revisions.
The Department also proposes removing references to July 16, 2007, which are no longer necessary. Prior to the establishment of current section 656.11, the Department allowed employers to substitute an alien named on a permanent labor certification with another prospective alien. Labor certification substitution occurred either while the PERM labor certification application was pending with the Department or, by the Department's delegation to DHS, when a Form I-140 petition was submitted or was pending with DHS based on an approved PERM labor certification. This practice was ( printed page 73942) permitted due to the length of time it took to obtain a PERM labor certification and Form I-140 petition approval.
On May 17, 2007, the Department issued the Final Rule, Labor Certification for the Permanent Employment of Aliens in the United States; Reducing the Incentives and Opportunities for Fraud and Abuse and Enhancing Program Integrity (“2007 Fraud Rule”), through which the Department eliminated the practice of allowing the substitution of aliens on PERM labor certifications through rulemaking.[73] In the 2007 Fraud Rule, the Department established that substitution or change to the identity of an alien was prohibited on any application filed with the Department for PERM labor certification and on any resulting certification.[74] See current 20 CFR 656.11(a). The 2007 rule also revised current section 656.30(c)(2) to state that a PERM labor certification is only valid for the alien named on the original PERM labor certification application.[75]
Further, the 2007 Fraud Rule clarified that requests for modifications to an application, where the application was filed after the final rule's effective date, would not be accepted. See current 20 CFR 656.11(b). The Department explained that it had implemented technological changes in the PERM program to alert applicants to technical grounds for deniability, thus eliminating the need for many modifications.[76]
The Department has no intention of changing section 656.11(b) to permit substantive changes to an Application for Permanent Employment Certification, while in process or after certification, if certified. A substantive change is one that would correct an error that would lead to denial, if not corrected. The employer has the burden of proof to establish eligibility for a PERM labor certification and the responsibility to submit a complete and accurate Application for Permanent Employment Certification.
The Department has considered how it might appropriately permit minor modifications to an Application for Permanent Employment Certification while it is pending with the NPC. By minor modifications, the Department refers to errors that have no bearing on the CO's assessment of the employer's compliance with program requirements. An error is not minor—and its correction would not be a minor modification—if its correction would prevent denial. In addition, the Department has no interest in permitting correction of errors that clearly could have been prevented with closer attention, such as ridiculous errors ( e.g., place holders or cartoon characters) or multiple errors—even if arguably clerical—in the same application. Rather, the Department would consider allowing correction of isolated instances of purely clerical errors for which correction could be appropriate to avoid wasted resources of withdrawing and refiling for both the employer and the NPC.
The Department invites comments about whether part 656 should be revised to permit any minor modifications and, if so, the conditions under which minor modifications could be permitted without impact to program integrity or program efficiency. Helpful comments will discuss time period limitations for requesting a minor modification ( e.g., within the 30 calendar days after filing or prior to the issuance of a final determination by the CO); the scope of minor modifications with examples ( e.g., correcting a birthdate, visa classification or phone number for the alien or replacing an agent or attorney information with a new representative's information) and identifying form fields that do not contain substantive information, as substantive information could not be modified.
3. Proposed 20 CFR 656.12, Improper Commerce and Payment
Through the 2007 Fraud Rule, the Department imposed clear limitations on the acquisition and use of a pending or approved permanent labor certification application to reduce the incentives and opportunities for fraud and abuse in the permanent labor certification program and to propose measures to enhance the integrity of the permanent labor certification program.[77] The 2007 Fraud Rule revised section 656.12(a) to create a complete ban on the sale, barter, and purchase of labor certification applications and certifications. The revisions applied to applications filed on or after the effective date of the 2007 Fraud Rule regardless of whether the labor certification application involved was filed under the regulations of 20 CFR part 656 in effect before March 28, 2005, or, on or after, March 28, 2005.[78]
Under this proposed rule, the Department proposes to eliminate the transition procedures in the introductory part of section 656.12 related to the 2004 PERM Final Rule [79] and the 2007 Fraud Rule because the transition procedures in this section are obsolete.
The current section 656.12 states that it applies to applications “under this part” and “certifications resulting from those applications,” while stating in sections 656.12(a) and (c) that those provisions only apply to “ approved permanent labor certifications.” The Department's proposed introductory language serves to clarify that the provisions of section 656.12 apply to any Application for Permanent Employment Certification and any PERM labor certification. The proposed terminology changes serve to better frame the scope of various possible outcomes for Applications for Permanent Employment Certification ( e.g., certification, withdrawal, invalidation, revocation) and continue to place employers on notice that their Applications for Permanent Employment Certification and PERM labor certifications under part 656 are subject to the provisions of section 656.12.
4. Proposed New 20 CFR 656.13, Assurances and Obligations of Employers
The Department is proposing the creation of a new section at 656.13 to enumerate by topic the assurances and obligations under the PERM program to which an employer must attest compliance. Organizing all existing and newly proposed required attestations into one section will improve clarity and navigation within part 656 and part 656 with similar regulatory provisions the Department has in the H-1B, H-2A and H-2B programs.[80] By clearly stating the employer's legal obligations in one provision of the regulations, the Department has determined this approach will make it easier for employers to find, understand, and comply with the program requirements. Further, the proposed changes in section 656.13 would enhance protections for U.S. workers, including protection of U.S. worker wages, and promote consideration of U.S. workers for job opportunities, notably by expanding contact and consideration requirements in layoff situations and preventing employers from laying off U.S. workers to create the perception of available vacancies and filing an Application for Permanent Employment Certification with the intention of ( printed page 73943) replacing laid off U.S. workers with aliens. See proposed 656.13(e) and (f).
For proposed section 656.13, employers will be required to read and agree to comply with essential program requirements and worker protections for both U.S. and aliens when submitting an application under part 656, which will improve program integrity. Under penalty of perjury, employers will attest that they have read and reviewed every page of the Application for Permanent Employment Certification, including all applicable appendices and supporting documentation, and to the best of their knowledge the information contained therein is true and accurate. In addition, employers will assure they understand that knowingly and/or willfully furnishing materially false information in the preparation of this form and any supplement thereto or to aid, abet, or counsel another to do so is a federal offense punishable by fines, imprisonment, or both under 18 U.S.C. 2, 1001, 1546, 1621. The clearer requirements will also enhance the Department's program integrity efforts, as clearer requirements provide a more straightforward path for enforcement of program requirements.
Specifically, the Department is proposing to take existing required attestations, currently codified at section 656.10(c), and replace and relocate them to this new proposed section 656.13, while further replacing the language of the existing attestations to strengthen program integrity. The Department proposes to replace and relocate regulatory content explaining the nature and effect of attestations from current section 656.10(c) to the introductory language of proposed section 656.13. In addition, the Department is proposing to relocate other existing obligations from current sections 656.10, 656.12, 656.17, and 656.20 to proposed section 656.13, again further revising them to create clear employer attestations in efforts to clarify and/or update compliance requirements. Finally, the Department also proposes new attestations to reflect new requirements through this proposed rule. The proposals in this rule strengthen U.S. worker protections and bolster program integrity by holding employers who choose to participate in the PERM program accountable to the Department, and to their responsibilities to both U.S. workers and aliens, with regard to the employer's filing of the Application for Permanent Employment Certification and participation in the PERM program.
Proposed new section 656.13(a) continues to require an employer seeking to permanently employ an alien to attest that the job opportunity is a bona fide, full-time, permanent position and adds that the terms and conditions of employment must comply with all applicable laws and regulations, consistent with current sections 656.10(c)(7) and (1). This proposed revision continues current sections 656.10(c)(5), (8), and (9) attestations, requiring the employer to attest that the job opportunity has been and is open to any qualified U.S. worker, any U.S. worker who applied was rejected for lawful, job-related reasons, and the job opportunity does not involve unlawful discrimination. The Department proposes to expand this attestation so that the job opportunities that are the subject of Applications for Permanent Employment Certification remain open to the employer's laid off-employees in the place(s) of employment in the same or similar occupation throughout the period in which the employer is establishing its sponsorship of an alien for permanent employment in the United States—between filing the Application for Permanent Employment Certification and the date the Form I-140 petition is approved by USCIS or the date of determination, if the Application for Permanent Employment Certification or Form I-140 petition is denied or withdrawn, whichever is later, which would form the basis for the alien's application for a visa and admission to the United States as a permanent resident.[81] In addition, the provision adds language explicitly requiring employers to comply with Title VII of the Civil Rights Act of 1964, which prohibits workplace discrimination in the terms, conditions, or privileges of employment because of race, color, religion, sex, or national origin. Nothing in Title VII, “makes it illegal to discriminate on the basis of citizenship or alienage.” Espinoza v. Farah Mfg. Co., 414 U.S. 86, 95 (1973). The provision also adds language requiring compliance with the Antidiscrimination Provision of the Immigration and Nationality Act, 8 U.S.C. 1324b, which prohibits discrimination because of citizenship status against protected individuals and, for employers not covered by Title VII, because of national origin. That law states that it is an unfair immigration-related employment practice to discriminate against a citizen or national of the United States based on their citizenship status. 8 U.S.C. 1324b(a)(1), (3).
Separately, and under the Department's own authority in 8 U.S.C. 1182(a)(5)(A)(i)(II), proposed section 656.13(a)(5) requires employers to offer U.S. workers terms, conditions, and privileges of employment no less favorable than those offered to the alien. Compliance with the PERM requirements is a floor; it does not establish compliance with Title VII or 8 U.S.C. 1324b, and a departure from the PERM requirements may be relevant evidence in an enforcement action or private suit under those laws. This provision will prohibit an employer from rejecting any qualified U.S. worker over a prospective alien based on any cost justifications or basis, including differences in any term, condition, or privilege of employment, and compensation and fringe benefits between any qualified U.S. worker and a prospective alien. This proposed provision is necessary to ensure the integrity of the PERM program by prohibiting situations where the job offer is designed to match the skills of a particular alien. Unduly restrictive job postings have a chilling effect on the number of qualified U.S. workers who respond to job vacancy postings, and the Department will prohibit this practice with this proposal. These proposals strengthen U.S. worker protections and bolster program integrity by holding U.S. employers who participate in the program accountable to the Department and U.S. workers beyond the processing of the Application for Permanent Employment Certification.
Under proposed section 656.13(b), the Department continues the attestation required under current section 656.10(c)(6) to require the employer to attest that the job opportunity is not vacant due to a strike or employees being locked out in the course of a labor dispute involving a work stoppage. The proposal, however, expands the strike provision of the current regulation to clarify that it covers “all places of employment” included on the Application for Permanent Employment Certification, including third-party employers' locations. Through this proposed revised provision, the Department will improve program integrity and strengthen protections for U.S. workers.
To protect the integrity of the PERM labor certification program, deter fraud, and comply with the Department's statutory obligation to protect the wages of U.S. workers, the Department is proposing in section 656.13(c) to revise and clarify the already-required attestations in current sections ( printed page 73944) 656.10(c)(1) through (4). For example, current section 656.10(c)(1) requires the employer to attest that it has offered, and will continue to offer, a wage that equals or exceeds the PWD. Proposed section 656.13(c) would more clearly state the employer's attestation that it has offered, and will continue to offer, a wage that equals or exceeds the highest of the Federal, State or local minimum wage or the PWD issued to the employer. The proposed paragraph clarifies that the offered wage requirement includes consideration of Federal, State, and local minimum wage rates, which is an existing requirement—for compliance with local, State, and Federal laws—under current section 656.10(c)(7). In addition, added content about the potential for an updated PWD reflects the potential for OFLC to obtain a new PWD, for example, during supervised recruitment. Further, this proposed provision adds new language stating that the offered wage cannot result in less favorable treatment of current or prospective U.S. workers in any terms, conditions, and privileges of employment, including compensation and fringe benefits, as compared with the alien ( i.e., must not be lower than the wage the employer pays, and the wage the employer advertises, for similarly situated positions). A violation of this certification is a serious matter that will result in denial and may result in revocation, debarment, and referral under 656.31 and 656.32. The Department solicits comments on this proposed provision, including how this attestation could be worded more clearly to ensure that wage compensation and other benefits for U.S. workers similarly employed are not adversely affected by the prospective employment of the alien(s) in the job opportunity. The proposed attestation continues to specify that the offered wage cannot be based on commissions, bonuses or other incentives, unless the employer guarantees an offered wage earned on a weekly, bi-weekly, or monthly basis that equals or exceeds the prevailing wage. The Department proposes expanding the attestation to accommodate the possibility of the Department issuing a new or revised PWD ( e.g., post-filing supervised recruitment) and, if so, the employer would be required to update its wage offer, unless notified otherwise by the Department.
The Department also proposes to continue to require the employer to attest that it has enough funds available to pay the offered wage for work performed in the job opportunity at the time of Application for Permanent Employment Certification submission. The Department proposes to revise this attestation for clarity and to include the citation to DHS's requirements regarding the employer's ability to pay at 8 CFR 204.5(g)(2), as USCIS will assess the employer's compliance during I-140 Petition review. These revisions would better apprise employers of this requirement, while avoiding duplicative regulatory provisions. In addition, citing to DHS's regulations would reduce the potential for confusion about the assessment to be performed when the employer files the I-140 Petition.
Under proposed section 656.13(d), the employer must attest that it has conducted, and will continue to conduct, recruitment of U.S. workers in good faith and in the same manner it normally recruits and considers U.S. workers in the same job opportunities that it does not request PERM labor certification, while also ensuring that its recruitment meets the standards set forth in proposed section 656.17. Through this proposed provision, the Department would require that the employer recruit U.S. workers in good faith and follow the same methods they would typically use for similar job opportunities where they do not seek PERM labor certifications for aliens, including when those methods they typically use for similar job opportunities exceed the requirements in proposed section 656.17. Where, how, and for how long an employer advertises a job opportunity in connection with the PERM labor certification program must be at least equal to the employer's practices for similar job opportunities unrelated to a PERM labor certification. Similarly, the employer's application process for candidates who apply for the employer's job opportunity that is the subject of the PERM application, must not disadvantage U.S. workers or be less available to U.S. workers than the employer's standard for same job opportunities that are unrelated to a request for PERM labor certification. Additionally, if required, interviews must be conducted by phone or arranged through other means ( e.g., virtual meetings, cloud calls, or other platforms or applications that facilitate communication without involving physical presence) to minimize costs for U.S. workers and facilitate the process should the applicant, for example, be unable to travel or be physically present at the employer's location for an interview. Further, the employer's representative who interviews or considers U.S. workers for the job offered to the alien must be a U.S. worker and one who normally interviews or considers, on behalf of the employer, applicants for job opportunities such as that offered the alien which do not involve labor certifications. Lastly, employers are prohibited from giving preferential treatment to aliens over U.S. workers concerning any requirements for applications and interviews related to the PERM application process.
These attestations are necessary because there have been several reports of employers creating separate recruitment processes for PERM applications that deter U.S. workers from applying for job opportunities.[82] In one instance, the Department of Justice (DOJ) found that an employer did not advertise positions it sought to fill through the PERM program on its external job website, even though its standard practice was to post other job positions on this website.[83] The employer also required all applicants for PERM-related positions to mail paper applications, even though the company permitted electronic applications for other positions.[84] These provisions ( printed page 73945) would clarify expectations for employer conduct during the PERM application recruitment process.
Currently, if the employer has a layoff in the AIE within six months of filing the Application for Permanent Employment Certification, the employer must document that it has notified and considered all of the employer's potentially qualified laid off U.S. workers for the job opportunity to be offered in an Application for Permanent Employment Certification. See current section 656.17(k). The proposed rule would convert the existing layoff obligation into an employer attestation and relocate the provision to the newly proposed section 656.13(e) with revisions. As proposed, section 656.13(e)(1) would address layoffs prior to the employer's filing of an Application for Permanent Employment Certification, while section 656.13(e)(2) would address post-filing layoffs.
The Department's proposals regarding layoffs and consideration of laid-off U.S. workers are intended to provide U.S. workers additional protections against improper hiring conduct by employers [85] and prevent employers from laying off U.S. worker(s) and creating vacancies which are then filled with aliens using the PERM process.[86]
As proposed, an employer would be required to attest that it has not laid off any U.S. worker(s) in the same or similar occupation that is the subject of the Application for Permanent Employment Certification in the area of intended employment of the place(s) of employment within 2 years prior to filing the application. The Department proposes to change the current term to describe similar occupations from “occupation for which certification is sought or in a related occupation” in current section 656.17(k) to “same or similar occupation” in proposed section 656.13(e), for readability. The Department does not intend for this proposed revision to change the employers' consideration of their laid-off employees post-filing of the Application for Permanent Employment Certification and reminds employers that their obligation is to consider all potentially qualified laid-off U.S. workers for job opportunities that are the subject of a pending or certified Application for Permanent Employment Certification. The Department invites comments on this proposed change and ways to improve clarity.
If the employer could not make this attestation ( i.e., if it had engaged in such a layoff), the employer would be prohibited from filing the Application for Permanent Employment Certification, under the proposed rule.
The proposed 2-year timeframe during which layoffs would be prohibited supports the Department's goals in this rulemaking. Specifically, this proposal would enhance safeguards for laid-off U.S. workers and increase protection against employment discrimination for an extended period of time, while preventing misuse of the PERM labor certification program.
The Department proposes a 2-year period because the current 180-day layoff timeframe prior to the employer filing an Application for Permanent Employment Certification may not adequately capture the relationship between an employer's layoffs and its subsequent efforts to obtain permanent labor certification. Workforce planning, recruitment, the PERM process, and related business decisions can unfold over an extended period, allowing a shorter restriction to be avoided simply by delaying the filing of an application. A 2-year period would provide a more meaningful safeguard against such circumvention while also allowing sufficient time for employers to rehire or retrain recently laid-off U.S. workers if demand for the same or similar occupations returns. This proposal seeks to ensure employers do not circumvent program requirements nor abuse the system for seeking to hire alien workers by conducting layoffs that are timed with respect to application or petition timeframes to avoid scrutiny or further having to justify the hiring of alien workers despite the employer experiencing layoffs. The Department seeks comment on whether 2 years is the appropriate period and whether a shorter or longer period would better accomplish these objectives.
The Department also believes the proposed period could encourage employers to invest in retraining existing workers as workforce needs change and could increase opportunities to rehire laid-off U.S. workers when demand for an affected occupation returns. For example, without access to the PERM labor certification program for 2 years, employers would be more likely to invest in training their existing workforce in new technology or other changes, rather than replacing them with aliens who received that training elsewhere. For layoffs that occur due to changed market conditions, this proposal could result in increased re-hiring of laid-off U.S. workers, if an employer's need for the occupation that was affected by layoffs returns.
In addition, simultaneously laying off U.S. workers and recruiting in the same or similar occupation(s) of the PERM application (or doing so in close proximity) is inherently problematic for the statutorily required determinations the Department must make in order to issue a PERM labor certification. An employer's layoff of U.S. workers who are available and willing to work at the place(s) of employment and who, until the layoff, were performing work for the employer in the occupation, or a similar occupation, at the place(s) of employment would preclude the Department from making the statutorily required determinations necessary to issue a PERM labor certification. In addition to furthering U.S. worker protections and program integrity, the proposed 2-year pre-filing anti-layoff provision aligns with the Department's goal of streamlining processing by preventing the filing of an Application for Permanent Employment Certification where the employer's own actions have increased U.S. worker availability for the job opportunity that is the subject of the Application for Permanent Employment Certification.
Proposed section 656.13(e)(2) addresses layoff situations after an employer files their Application for Permanent Employment Certification. As proposed, this provision would apply to an employer's layoff of any U.S. workers in the same or similar occupation that is the subject of the ( printed page 73946) Application for Permanent Employment Certification in the place(s) of employment—including the placement of the alien with another employer, subject to proposed section 656.13(f)—after the filing of the application through the date on which the Form I-140 petition is approved by USCIS or the date of determination, if the Application for Permanent Employment Certification or Form I-140 petition is denied or withdrawn, whichever is later. If any such layoff occurs, the employer would be required to notify the NPC (and USCIS in the case of a pending Form I-140 petition) not later than 5 business days after such layoff(s) occurs. This new notification requirement after such layoff(s) occurs ensures program integrity by informing and allowing OFLC and USCIS, if applicable, to take appropriate action with the employer's Application for Permanent Employment Certification, and/or the employer's pending Form I-140 petition, respectively. The Department notes that the notification required by this provision will trigger the INA 204(b) consultation process whereby DOL will determine the adequacy of the employer's compliance with the post-certification obligation to consider laid off U.S. workers, and inform USCIS of the continued validity, or revocation of the underlying labor certification based on DOL's assessment of the employer's compliance.
In addition, the proposal would require the employer to offer the job opportunity that is the subject of the Application for Permanent Employment Certification to the laid-off U.S. worker(s) and document in an updated recruitment report whether the U.S. worker(s) refused the job opportunity, the employer rejected the U.S. worker(s) for the job opportunity for lawful, job-related reasons, or the employer hired the U.S. worker(s).
Under this proposed rule, the employer must seek to re-hire laid off U.S. worker(s) by offering the job opportunity to any potentially qualified laid off U.S. worker(s) before continuing with a PERM application. Failure to fill the job opportunity that is the subject of the Application for Permanent Employment Certification by re-hiring a laid-off U.S. worker who is qualified and available could raise an evidentiary inference of citizenship status discrimination, national origin discrimination or discrimination in any other class listed in anti-discrimination provision at proposed section 656.13(a)(4). Through this proposed requirement, the Department would protect against U.S. worker displacement and ensure that an employer's laid off U.S. workers have access to the job opportunity while the Application for Permanent Employment Certification and Form I-140 petition are pending, before the employer uses the PERM labor certification to permanently employ an alien.
The Department seeks stakeholder comments regarding layoffs and what constitutes reasonable time periods and requirements for employers filing Applications for Permanent Employment Certifications before, during, and after layoffs.
This proposed provision would also protect against U.S. worker displacement and ensure that an employer's laid off U.S. workers have access to the job opportunity while the Application for Permanent Employment Certification and Form I-140 petition are pending, before the alien uses the PERM labor certification and I-140 petition to apply for permanent residence.
Further, the Department proposes to require an employer who has laid off any U.S. worker(s) in the same or similar occupation that is the subject of the Application for Permanent Employment Certification in the area of intended employment of the place(s) of employment during the period set forth in proposed section 656.13(e)—including the placement of the alien with another employer, subject to proposed section 656.13(f)—to notify OFLC (and USCIS in the case of a pending Form I-140 petition), in writing or by any other method the Department might specify in a FRN (or as specified by USCIS in the case of a pending Form I-140 petition), not later than five business days after such layoff(s) occurs. This new notification requirement after such layoff(s) occurred ensures program integrity by informing and allowing OFLC and USCIS, if applicable, to take appropriate action with the employer's Application for Permanent Employment Certification, and/or pending Form I-140 petition, respectively.
The Department also proposes a new attestation in proposed section 656.13(f) that would require the employer filing the Application for Permanent Employment Certification to attest that they have not and will not place the alien with another employer where there are indicia of an employment relationship between the alien and the other employer, and the other employer has laid off U.S. workers who are employed in the same or similar occupation within the 2 years prior to filing of the application through the date the Form I-140 petition is approved by USCIS or the date of determination, if the Application for Permanent Employment Certification or Form I-140 petition is denied or withdrawn, whichever is later. Although the Department recognizes that the alien's assignments may change after filing, the Department proposes to require the employer to identify on the application any third-party entities and sites to which the alien is assigned at the time of filing. This collection will improve the information available to the Department regarding third-party assignments and compliance with proposed section 656.13(f). This proposed provision would improve the integrity of the PERM program by ensuring U.S. workers will not be displaced by aliens, whether the aliens work directly for the employer or are employed by a secondary employer, such as a contractor.
For consistency across programs, the Department is proposing to use the criteria set forth in the Department's H-1B program regulations at 20 CFR 655.738(d)(2) to assess indicia of an employment relationship between the other employer and the alien. Under 20 CFR 655.738(d)(2), the relationship between the alien and the other employer need not constitute an “employment” relationship, but other relevant indicia of an employment relationship may be considered, including: which employer has the right to control when, where, and how the alien performs the job; who furnishes the tools, materials, and equipment; where the work is performed, who sets the hours of work and the duration of the job; and whether the work performed is part of the other employer's regular business. The Department is considering whether to propose the implementation of a contract requirement between employers, which could align with the contract requirements in the H-1B program. Although the Department is not proposing a contract requirement in this rule, the Department welcomes comments on this consideration.
This proposed rule also codifies current requirements that employers and their agents, attorneys, and/or employees have not offered, and will not offer, for sale, barter or purchase Applications for Permanent Employment Certification, including applications certified by the Department, and have not sought or received, and will not seek to receive, payment of any kind from the worker(s) for any activity related to obtaining a PERM labor certification. The Department is proposing to relocate the language contained in current section 656.12, while also seeking to make some clarifying changes. See proposed section ( printed page 73947) 656.13(g). This provision includes payment of the employer's attorneys' fees, whether as an incentive or inducement to filing, or as a reimbursement for costs incurred in preparing or filing an Application for Permanent Employment Certification, except when the work benefits the person or entity making the payment, based on an established business relationship with the employer. This provision serves to prevent fraud and abuse by those who unlawfully take advantage of the PERM labor certification program. The proposed rule, however, would continue to allow aliens to pay their own costs in connection with a PERM labor certification, including attorneys' fees, except where the same attorney represents both the alien and the employer, such costs must be paid by the employer. Under the proposed rule, payment includes, but is not limited to, monetary payments; wage concessions, including deductions from wages, salary, or benefits; kickbacks, bribes, or tributes; in kind payments; and free labor. Adding this provision to section 656.13 would serve as an additional guard rail to deter program abuse and bolster the integrity of the program. See proposed section 656.13(g).
The Department proposes to require employers to attest they will comply with applicable Federal, State, and local employment-related laws and regulations, including, but not limited to, employment-related health and safety laws, and all applicable provisions of the Fair Labor Standards Act, 29 U.S.C. 201, et seq. See proposed section 656.13(h). The Department proposes this attestation to put employers on notice that compliance with these laws is a condition of certification and to strengthen coordination of enforcement among Federal agencies with authority to ensure compliance with program requirements and combat fraud and abuse. The Department also requires employers to attest that they and their agents and attorneys are prohibited from holding or confiscating workers' passports, visas, or other immigration documents pursuant to 18 U.S.C. 1592(a). This proposal will provide protection for aliens' rights from unscrupulous employers, such as those who take away aliens' passports with the intent to trap and coerce the worker and undermine the integrity of the program by deterring those workers from coming forward to report labor and employment-related issues to the Department and the government at large.
Proposed section 656.13(i) would require employers to attest to compliance with record retention requirements. Employers would continue to be required to retain all documents and records demonstrating compliance with part 656 for a period of 5 years, which the Department proposes to count from the date on which the Immigrant Petition is approved or denied or withdrawn. If the employer does not proceed to file an Immigrant Petition, it would be required to retain all records associated with the Application for Permanent Employment Certification for a period of 5 years from the expiration of the certification or denial or withdrawal. See proposed section 656.10(h).
Proposed section 656.13(j) adds a new attestation, which would require employers to attest that they will fully cooperate with any audit, supervised recruitment, compliance review, evaluation, or inspection conducted by DOL related to compliance with part 656, or by other government agencies investigating PERM practices. This proposal will deter fraud and abuse and improve the integrity of the PERM program because employers are put on notice of the Department's expectations and understand their obligations and responsibilities in the event of an audit examination or investigation.
Consistent with current practice, failure to attest to compliance with any of the required assurances and obligations would result in a denial of the Application for Permanent Employment Certification and a false certification would subject the employer to audit, supervised recruitment, debarment, referral, and criminal penalties under 18 U.S.C. 1001.
5. Proposed New 20 CFR 656.14, Notice of Filing
The NOF, required by the Act, is intended to make U.S. workers employed by the sponsoring employer aware that their employer is pursuing an Application for Permanent Employment Certification for a particular job opportunity, and enable them to provide information directly to the Department if any of the material terms or conditions of employment being offered to the alien are different from or adversely affect those offered or provided to U.S. workers in similar positions, or if they have any other evidence or information bearing on the prospective employment of an alien in the job opportunity at the place(s) of employment that the Department should consider. U.S. workers can also provide information to the Department of any qualified and available U.S. workers who have not been considered for the job opportunity, such as during a layoff that may occur at any time prior to the filing of the application through approval of the Form I-140 petition by USCIS.[87]
First, the Department proposes relocating the current regulatory text in section 656.10(d) to a new section at 656.14, with additional modifications to reorganize the section for clarity. The Department also proposes revising the standard for providing the NOF requirements to enhance transparency, strengthen program integrity, and acknowledge the widespread use of electronic tools by employers to disseminate other notices of employment-related guarantees or conditions ( e.g., Fair Labor Standards Act minimum wage requirements, Equal Employment Opportunity protections), especially due to remote or other hybrid telework environments.
The Department is proposing that after the language currently contained in sections 656.10(d)(i) and (ii) is relocated to the newly added section 656.14's paragraphs (a)(1) and (2), conforming changes be made. Specifically, the Department is proposing revisions that would clarify employers' obligations for the NOF when the employer seeks to apply for a PERM labor certification to permanently employ an alien in the United States. Proposed new section 656.14(a) would still require, if requested by the CO, employers to submit documentation showing that the NOF was posted. The proposed revisions would eliminate regulatory text listing the sections to which the NOF requirement applies ( i.e., 656.15, 656.16, 656.17, 656.18, and 656.21), in favor of regulatory text that indicates that the NOF requirement applies to all applications under part 656, consistent with statute and other proposed regulatory changes. The proposed move to section 656.14 would streamline the current regulatory text by making it clear that the NOF is a general requirement; accordingly, the new regulatory text would eliminate the need for updates in the future should there be additional changes to the organization of part 656.
The Department makes minor revisions in proposed section 656.14(a)(1) to specify that the NOF, when given to a bargaining representative(s), must be in writing, either electronically or by mail. The employer must also provide a copy of the Application for Permanent Employment Certification to the ( printed page 73948) bargaining representative. Proposed section 656.14(a)(1) clarifies that employers must maintain documentation of where and when the employer's NOF was sent to the bargaining representative(s). These proposed revisions strengthen the NOF requirements to bolster the integrity of the PERM labor certification program and better align with similar notice requirements in the temporary labor certification programs.
In proposed 656.14(a)(1), the Department proposes to change the circumstances in which notification of bargaining representative(s) is required. Under the current rule, an employer must notify a bargaining representative, if there is a bargaining representative for the employer's employees in the occupational classification for which certification of the job opportunity is sought “in the employer's location(s) in the area of intended employment.” As proposed an employer would be required to notify the bargaining representative(s) for the employer's employees located at the place(s) of employment” listed on the Application for Permanent Employment Certification. This change—to require notice to bargaining representative(s) at the place(s) of employment—would ensure the bargaining representative(s) of similarly employed U.S. workers at all place(s) of employment listed on the Application for Permanent Employment Certification must receive notice and resolves potential misinterpretation of the singular term “the area of intended employment” in the current provision. This approach better ensures the employer's employees receive notice of the filing of the Application for Permanent Employment Certification, even if those place(s) of employment are in more than one AIE. As discussed in relation to the definition of “place(s) of employment” in proposed section 656.3, workforce mobility has increased over the past two decades and the place(s) of employment listed on an Application for Permanent Employment Certification may encompass more than one AIE. The proposed change would ensure that all bargaining representatives representing employees in the occupational classification at the place(s) of employment receive direct notice of the filing of the Application for Permanent Employment Certification and can assess impact to U.S. workers. In addition, the proposed change would increase consistency of notification to the employer's employees at the place(s) of employment, whether or not there is a bargaining representative, as both the current and proposed rules require physical or electronic posting of the NOF for employees at the location where work will be performed. The Department does not consider this proposed change to impact the employer's burden, as any increase in notice to bargaining representative(s) under proposed section 656.14(a)(1) would be offset by the corresponding decrease in posted notices under proposed section 656.14(a)(2). Even if some employers would experience some increased burden due to having to perform this notification and maintain applicable records of performance, the benefits maintaining the documentation for the notices the employer provides to any applicable bargaining representatives outweigh that increased burden. The Department also makes revisions in proposed section 656.14(a)(2) for situations where there is no bargaining representative(s) for any of the employer's employees located at the place(s) of employment. Currently, the employer must post the NOF physically at the place of employment, but the regulation does not specify the required number of posting locations required. Proposed section 656.14(a)(2) specifies that employers must post the NOF “in at least 2 conspicuous locations to all employees located at the place(s) of employment in which the work will be performed by the alien.” Posting the NOF physically in at least two conspicuous locations at all places of employment involved, as defined in proposed section 656.3, would allow individuals who work or traverse through different locations more opportunity to see the posting and would enhance the visibility of the NOF to employees. Furthermore, requiring posting at all places of employment better executes the function of the notice—which is to inform people that the filing is taking place and solicit information bearing on the filing. Restricting it to one location would inherently deprive some of the employer's employees of notice.
In addition, current section 656.10(d)(1)(ii) requires the NOF to be posted physically and, if applicable, “. . . in any and all in-house media, whether electronic or printed, in accordance with the normal procedures used for the recruitment of similar positions in the employer's organization.” In contrast, proposed section 656.14(a)(2) would require electronic posting of the NOF as an alternative to physical posting—not as an additional requirement—under certain conditions. For employers who maintain internal or external website(s) that are “known and readily accessible to all employees, and customarily used by the employer for notices to employees about terms and conditions of employment,” the Department proposes that prominently displaying the NOF on those websites, in lieu of physical postings, would satisfy the statutory notice requirement under proposed 656.14(a)(2). The Department asserts that posting the NOF electronically would be particularly effective for businesses with employees who frequently telework or travel, and who may not see the NOF if it is only posted physically in the workplace.
The Department also proposes, in new section 656.14(g), changing the timing and duration of the NOF posting. Currently, the NOF must be posted for at least 10 consecutive business days during the 30- to 180-day period before the employer files the Application for Permanent Employment Certification.[88] The Department proposes to eliminate the strict duration requirement from current section 656.10(d)(1)(ii) and timeframe requirement from current section 656.10(d)(3)(iv). Under proposed section 656.14(g), the NOF would be posted during the 10 business days before the employer files the Application for Permanent Employment Certification and continue to be displayed through the date on which the Form I-140 petition is approved by USCIS or the date of determination, if the Application for Permanent Employment Certification or Form I-140 petition is denied or withdrawn, whichever is later. The Department has determined that increasing the duration of the NOF's display and requiring display contemporaneous with Application for Permanent Employment Certification filing and OFLC and USCIS processing would provide U.S. workers with different work schedules, who may be on leave, who may be impacted by layoffs, or may be hired during the Application for Permanent Employment Certification or Form I-140 petition process more opportunity to see the NOF and provide relevant information to OFLC and, if applicable, USCIS. This longer timeframe allows the Department to receive information it may not have previously received, which in turn, better informs the CO's decision-making and enhances program integrity. This improves transparency and protects U.S. workers by informing U.S. workers and other interested parties of the job opportunity offered in the Application for Permanent Employment Certification and the employer's obligations under part 656. In addition, although the statute is silent on the length of time ( printed page 73949) that the NOF should be available to U.S. workers, these proposed changes better align with the statute's minimum standard that “no certification may be made [by the Secretary] unless the [employer applying] for certification has, at the time of filing the application, provided notice of the filing.” [89]
Proposed section 656.14(a)(2) would also revise the description of how and where the NOF must be posted for clarity and to simplify regulatory language. For example, the Department adds “[t]he notice must be of sufficient size” to existing language that states the NOF must be “clearly visible, and unobstructed while posted and must be posted in conspicuous places.” In addition, the Department proposes to update the description of appropriate locations to post a NOF from “locations in the immediate vicinity of the wage and hour notices required by 29 CFR 516.4 or occupational safety and health notices required by 29 CFR 1903.2(a)” to “conspicuous places where the employees of the employer would normally expect to see such employment-related notices,” while retaining that employees should be able to “readily read the posted notice on their way to or from their place of employment.” The Department also proposes to retain the current NOF documentation requirements and clarify that the documentation must identify when the notice was posted, in addition to where it was posted. These proposed revisions would ensure that NOFs are displayed appropriately, where and how employees will see them and the employer's documentation will better verify compliance with all NOF posting requirements, including timing and duration.
The Department also proposes adding a new provision at section 656.14(b), which extends the NOF requirements to third-party worksites, if applicable. For employers planning to place an alien under an Application for Permanent Employment Certification at a third-party worksite, regardless of whether the place(s) of employment is(are) owned or operated by the employer or by some other person or entity, NOF posting at the third-party worksite would be required. The Department asserts that this proposed provision would enhance transparency and conform with the Department's goal of modernizing the PERM labor certification program by recognizing common employment scenarios that involve third parties and contractors. Further, requiring posting of the NOF at the third-party worksite(s) would enhance program integrity, so that U.S. workers at the third-party worksite are also aware of the filing of an Application for Permanent Employment Certification and can provide both DOL and USCIS with any relevant information about the application. This could be particularly important in the event the other employer engages in layoffs where the alien has or will be placed. To enable the Department to verify compliance with this requirement, the Department proposes to require employers to disclose third-party worksites applicable to the Application for Permanent Employment Certification at the time of filing.
The Department proposes to relocate the language included in current section 656.10(d)(2) and (3) to proposed section 656.14(c), with minor technical revisions to eliminate an unnecessary regulatory cross reference and to make a conforming change for consistency.
The Department is proposing to relocate current section 656.10(d)(3) to a proposed new section 656.14(d), and make further minor technical revisions, such as replacing “Certifying Officer” with “OFLC Administrator” to clarify responsibilities, as these terms are defined in section 656.3.
The Department is proposing to relocate the content of current section 656.10(d)(4), which lists required NOF content requirements, to proposed new section 656.14(e) with minor revisions to update the language and section references. In addition, the Department proposes to eliminate current sections 656.10(d)(5) and (d)(6), which separately address NOF content requirements for college or university teacher job opportunities and sheepherder job opportunities. The Department proposes to apply proposed section 656.14(e) to all NOFs, regardless of the job opportunity that is the subject of the Application for Permanent Employment Certification.
This proposed section would continue NOF content requirements for Applications for Permanent Employment Certification currently subject to processing under current section 656.17. Currently, employers filing Applications for Permanent Employment Certification in compliance with the requirements of section 656.17 must include the information required for advertisements in current section 656.17(f), the offered wage, and the notice and solicitation of evidence that effectuate the purpose of the NOF. Similarly, proposed section 656.14(e) would require the notice to contain the information required for advertisements in proposed section 656.17(e)—which, as proposed, would include a requirement to state the offered wage— and the notice and solicitation of evidence language of proposed 656.14(d).
The proposed NOF content provision, designed to implement a single standard for all NOFs, regardless of the occupation, would result in one change for employers filing under section 656.18. Currently, such employers are not required to include the offered wage in their NOFs; neither current section 656.18(d)(3) (notice and solicitation of evidence bearing on the application) nor current section 656.18(b)(3) (advertising content requirements) require it. Under the proposed rule, reflecting the Department's efforts to simplify the regulations and processing, employers filing under proposed 656.18 would be subject to the same advertisement content requirements as other job opportunities at proposed 656.17(e) and, therefore, the same NOF content requirements in proposed 656.14(e).
Regarding current section 656.10(d)(6), the Department proposes to eliminate the regulatory text, consistent with the proposed elimination of Schedule A and sheepherder procedures at sections 656.15 and 656.16.[90]
The Department invites comments to inform how to appropriately ensure the broadest visibility possible of NOFs for job opportunities, such as remote or domestic service job opportunities, where the place of employment is the employer or employee's residence and other job opportunities where the employer's similarly employed employees may not see a physical posting of a NOF, unless the proposed expanded timeframe for NOF posting is adopted ( e.g., long-haul trucking). As proposed, physical posting of the NOF at the place of employment would be permitted for an employer who does not maintain internal or external website(s) that are “known and readily accessible to all employees, and customarily used by the employer for notices to employees about terms and conditions of employment.” For remote job opportunities physical posting of the NOF at the place of employment—a residence where no other employees work—would not provide the notice and solicitation of information from the employer's employees that Congress intended. The Department has similar ( printed page 73950) concerns with NOF postings for other job opportunities that involve residential places of employment ( e.g., domestic service) or business places of employment that similarly employed employees may not visit regularly or often ( e.g., long-haul trucking). Should electronic posting of the NOF be required for such job opportunities in all cases? If electronic NOF posting is required of an employer who does not use an electronic means for notices to employees about terms and conditions of employment, what electronic methods ( e.g., websites, emails, text messages) and/or posting locations should be required for the NOF? For example, should the Department require an electronic posting on one or more of the employer's internal and external websites, consistent with the pre-filing recruitment requirements in proposed section 656.17(b)(2)? Based on feedback received, the Department will consider adding an additional requirement in the final rule to address this issue.
In addition, as discussed in connection with the proposed elimination of section 656.10(e)(1), the Department is considering ways to better solicit information bearing on an Application for Permanent Employment Certification from the public. For example, the Department could post a copy of the NOF, which contains information about the job offered and solicits information from interested parties, on a publicly accessible web page that OFLC manages for broad visibility. Leveraging the NOF as a vehicle for soliciting information bearing on an Application for Permanent Employment Certification would not add burden for the employer, apart from a de minimis burden to upload an already-prepared document in FLAG, and would be more effective at reaching interested parties than retaining current section 656.10(e)(1).
The Department solicits comments about how NOF visibility—and solicitation of information beyond the place(s) of employment and related word-of-mouth dissemination—could be expanded, without additional burden to the employer or significant impact to application processing efficiency, and will consider adding additional requirements in the final rule based on the feedback it receives from commenters.
6. 20 CFR 656.15, Reserved
As explained in the preamble discussion for current section 656.5 of subpart B, the Department has determined not to retain Schedule A in this proposed rule. Therefore, the Department is proposing to remove the provisions discussing filing procedures, documentation, and determinations discussed in current sections 656.15(a) through (f) for Schedule A occupations and reserve this section.
7. 20 CFR 656.16, Reserved
The current regulation designates section 656.16 for requirements for employers who seek to permanently employ sheepherders under part 656. Under this rule, the Department proposes to eliminate the alternative procedures for sheepherders. Under the proposed rule, all Applications for Permanent Employment Certification for sheepherders would be submitted to OFLC following the filing procedures in proposed section 656.10 after the employer completed pre-filing recruitment requirements in proposed 656.17 and satisfying all other normal PERM labor certification requirements.
Employers who seek to permanently employ an alien as a sheepherder have long been authorized to file an uncertified Application for Permanent Employment Certification directly with DHS, along with a PWD, provided that the alien could document legal employment as a nonimmigrant sheepherder in the United States for at least 33 of the preceding 36 months.[91] The Department's current approach to PERM labor certification for sheepherder positions developed from Congressional legislation authorizing special handling of a certain number of skilled sheepherders for permanent employment during the early 1950s.[92] However, this legislation expired and Congress has not enacted further legislation providing an alternative path for employers seeking to permanently employ aliens as sheepherders.[93] As there is no statutory requirement that the Department continue to except certain sheepherders from the normal test of the U.S. labor market, consistent with the Department's proposal to eliminate exceptions to pre-filing recruitment requirements ( e.g., for job opportunities currently defined as Schedule A), the Department proposes to eliminate the alternative procedure section of the current rule for sheepherders at section 656.16. The Department proposes to review and process sheepherder applications under normal procedures ( e.g., proposed sections 656.10 and 656.17).
The Department proposes to delete this section in its entirety and to reserve this section for future use.
8. Proposed 20 CFR 656.17, Pre-Filing Recruitment Requirements
The Department is proposing to revise current section 656.17 and its section title (from “Basic Labor Certification Process” to “Pre-filing Recruitment Procedures”), in part, due to the proposed reorganization of part 656. The Department proposes to relocate the content of current paragraphs (a) and (c) of section 656.17 (“Filing applications” and “Filing date”) to proposed section 656.10; the content of current paragraph (k) of section 656.17 (“Layoffs”) to proposed section 656.13; and the content of current paragraphs (b), (h), (i), (j), and (l) of section 656.17 (“Processing,” “Job duties and requirements,” “Actual minimum requirements,” “Conditions of employment,” and “Alien influence and control over the job opportunity”) to proposed section 656.19. The Department also proposes to revise section 656.17 to remove obsolete language ( e.g., current paragraph (d) “Refiling procedures”) and to conform with other proposals contained in this proposed rule, such as the elimination of alternative procedures for sheepherders and occupations currently covered by Schedule A. The resulting proposed section 656.17 is designed to focus on pre-filing recruitment requirements. Moreover, under the proposed rule and as previously discussed, the pre-filing recruitment requirements proposed in section 656.17 would apply to all Applications for Permanent Employment Certification, unless the job opportunity was eligible for—and the employer opted to comply with—the optional pre- ( printed page 73951) filing recruitment requirements of proposed section 656.18.
Consistent with the current regulation, proposed section 656.17 would continue to require employers seeking a PERM labor certification to engage in pre-filing recruitment activities, conduct a good faith test of the labor market for qualified U.S. workers, and retain documentation to demonstrate compliance with program requirements. However, the Department proposes to eliminate the current distinction between pre-filing recruitment requirements for professional and non-professional occupations in section 656.17; the requirements of proposed section 656.17 would apply equally to professional and non-professional occupations. As discussed below, the Department proposes significant revisions to modernize the pre-filing recruitment steps required under section 656.17 that better reflect the widespread use of online or digital recruitment practices, ensure that recruitment efforts reach the broadest pool of qualified and available U.S. workers, and ensure the employers' labor market tests are conducted in good faith and consistent with their current hiring practices to reach prospective applicants for similar job opportunities outside the PERM labor certification process. The proposed approach will support recruitment of both professional and non-professional occupations, enabling the Department to further its goal of simplifying part 656.
Employer Obligations
Consistent with current section 656.17(e), proposed section 656.17(a) requires employers to attest, under penalty of perjury, that they conducted the pre-filing recruitment outlined in section 656.17, including compliance with all advertising requirements contained in proposed sections 656.17(b) through (e). Under the proposed rule, only employers filing Applications for Permanent Employment Certification for college or university teachers, professional athletes, or professional sports team coaches may opt not to conduct pre-filing recruitment under section 656.17; however, if they do not comply with pre-filing recruitment requirements of proposed section 656.17, they must comply with the pre-filing recruitment requirements of proposed section 656.18.
Both currently and under the proposed rule, the employer must maintain documentation, including copies of all advertisements and resumes or applications from prospective U.S. applicants considered for the job opportunity, to support the attestations made in the Application for Permanent Employment Certification and recruitment report. Under this proposed rule, the employer would be required to submit the recruitment report (including resumes and applications of all U.S. workers who applied for the job opportunity) and documentation to prove compliance with recruitment requirements at the time of filing the Application for Permanent Employment Certification. See proposed sections 656.10(a)(3) and (4).
Required Recruitment
Under the current rule, employers are generally required to recruit during the 30-day to 180-day period before filing the Application for Permanent Employment Certification. Recruitment for nonprofessional occupations consists of placing a job order with the SWA for a period of 30 days and print advertisements on two different Sundays in a newspaper of general circulation in the AIE most appropriate to the occupation and the workers likely to apply for the job opportunity. Recruitment for professional occupations similarly consists of placing a SWA job order and two print advertisements—one of which may be a professional journal that is normally used for job opportunities requiring an advanced degree and experience—but also requires three additional steps. Employers self-select the three additional steps to take from a menu of ten (10) recruitment options. The list of recruitment options for professional occupations are intended to provide employers flexibility to select methods appropriate to the job opportunity that is the subject of the Application for Permanent Employment Certification. The menu of options includes: job fairs; the employer's website; job search websites other than the employer's; on-campus recruiting; trade or professional organization newsletters or journals; private employment firms; employee referral programs with incentives; campus placement offices; local and ethnic newspapers; and radio and television advertisements.
