The Department recognizes that print advertisements are no longer a meaningful method for recruitment, which impacts the informational value of the two print advertisements currently required for the PERM labor market test for both nonprofessional and professional job opportunities.[94] As some newspapers are shutting down and others are replaced by digital sources, the avenues for recruiting through news sources also migrate from print to online.[95] Online newspapers and social media sites are surpassing print newspapers for news and information,[96] including for job seekers, with job seekers using online or digital platforms over traditional print media.[97] In addition, many newspapers have reduced or eliminated print circulation.[98] This trend has continued, for example, with the Atlanta Journal-Constitution issuing its final print edition on December 30, 2025.[99] According to news reports, in recent years, print newspapers declined at rapid rates,[100] including local and ethnic print news sources,[101] resulting in news deserts throughout the country and the increased prevalence of areas ( printed page 73952) without local newspapers.[102]
The loss of print editions directly affects employers' abilities to place advertisements for job opportunities in print newspapers to comply with current part 656 requirements for both professional and nonprofessional occupations, and ultimately impacts the numbers of U.S. workers exposed to job opportunities advertised in print news sources.
Based on information, stakeholder feedback, and anecdotal data from previous rulemakings for the H-2A and H-2B programs, print ads in newspapers have yielded very few, if any, applications from able, willing, and qualified U.S. workers. Further, the Department's experience in conducting audit examinations, has shown that very few U.S. workers have applied to job opportunities in response to print advertisements that employers have placed.[103]
To modernize compliance requirements and to attract more U.S. workers to available job opportunities, this proposed rule seeks to replace mandatory print newspaper advertising with updated electronic methods for both nonprofessional and professional job opportunities. The modern methods of recruitment proposed in this rule ensure that recruitment for job opportunities that are the subject of Applications for Permanent Employment Certification is conducted in the manner that is at least the same as the employer's recruitment practices for job opportunities in the same or substantially comparable occupations that it does not seek PERM labor certification and most likely to reach the broadest pool of qualified U.S. workers in the current labor market.
In addition, the Department proposes to replace the current menu of options for additional recruitment for professional occupations with a standardized list of modern methods of recruitment, which would be applicable to both professional and nonprofessional occupations. As discussed below, the flexibility of the menu approach has not had the intended effect of ensuring appropriate recruitment for professional occupations. In addition, nonprofessional recruitment currently uses similar approaches to professional recruitment, including online advertising on job search websites and social media and recruitment through trade schools.[104]
The Department has reviewed the additional recruitment options employers have disclosed on Applications for Permanent Employment Certification for professional occupations and how employers have actually recruited using those options. In promulgating the current menu of additional recruitment options under the 2004 PERM Final Rule, the Department reasoned, at that time, that the methods selected were based on what program experience had shown to be real-world methods normally used by businesses to recruit workers, and that employers should be afforded flexibility to select the recruitment steps that were consistent with the employer's standard recruiting procedures.[105]
Based on a review of employer applications granted PERM labor certification for professional occupations ( i.e., requiring a degree) during FY 2021-FY 2025, the Department is proposing that the current approach—a menu of additional recruitment options for professional occupations—be eliminated because it is not serving its intended purpose.[106] Out of 409,368 PERM labor certifications granted during the most recent 5-year period, 94.2 percent of PERM applications for professional occupations were certified for positions within just the following 5 SOC major groups: SOC 15 (Computer and Mathematical) accounted for 65.4 percent of labor demand; SOC 17 (Architectural and Engineering) accounted for 10.1 percent of labor demand; SOC 13 (Business and Finance Operations) accounted for 8.9 percent of labor demand; SOC 11 (Management) accounted for 6.7 percent of labor demand; and SOC 29 (Healthcare Practitioners and Technicians) accounted for 3.2 percent of labor demand.[107]
First, the data reviewed shows that across all these professional occupations, the majority of employers selected online advertising through a job search website (95.4 percent) and half selected a website maintained by the employer (49.5 percent) as two of their three additional recruitment options. These recruitment options, along with the rapidly increasing use of social media platforms ( e.g., Instagram, LinkedIn, and others),[108] are considered among the top trends in recruiting young professionals seeking initial employment and career advancement.
Second, the data indicate that, in most instances, the ability for employers to self-select which additional recruitment options they want to conduct may lead to choices that do not necessarily serve to reach the broadest possible pool of potentially qualified U.S. workers. For example, despite requiring at least a post-secondary degree, more than 18.5 percent of employers across these occupational groups chose radio and television advertisements at a far higher rate as compared to using job fairs (0.2 percent), on-campus recruiting (0.5 percent), or a campus placement office (9.6 percent), which are more common methods of building talent pipelines and recruiting qualified professionals by allowing direct interaction with recruiters, industry peers, and hiring managers. In addition, with approximately 40 percent of college and university students participating in formal internships before graduation, the value of on-campus recruitment is not limited to entry-level job opportunities.[109] Additionally, approximately 76 percent of employers chose local and ethnic newspapers to ( printed page 73953) advertise their professional job opportunities, which the Department noted in the 2004 Final Rule should be used “to the extent they are appropriate for the job opportunity.” [110] As noted above, job fairs, on-campus recruiting or a campus placement office would appear to be more appropriate for these professional job opportunities, yet employers overwhelmingly used local and ethnic newspapers instead. Program data suggest that employers are not using the flexibility afforded through the menu approach to engage in recruitment activities most appropriate to the job opportunities offered, contrary to the Department's intent.
In the proposed rule, the Department would eliminate mandatory print advertisements for both professional and nonprofessional job opportunities and the current menu of additional recruitment options for professional occupations. Instead, for both professional and nonprofessional job opportunities, the proposed rule would require employers to comply with a standardized, mandatory list of modernized recruitment activities, which leverage online recruitment and the employer's own approach to recruit for similar job opportunities that are not the subject of an Application for Permanent Employment Certification.
The proposed rule seeks to improve and modernize the PERM labor certification labor market test by replacing outdated and ineffective means of recruitment with modern and more effective forms of recruitment, while also retaining some forms of recruitment that remain standard and productive, to advertise job opportunities as broadly as possible in order to attract as many U.S. workers as possible. In addition, the proposed rule aims at leveraging employers' current approaches to recruitment outside the PERM labor certification program. The proposed rule would require employers to recruit U.S. workers for PERM labor certification job opportunities using the same methods they use when seeking to fill a vacancy in a non-PERM labor certification recruitment setting. The recruitment steps proposed in this paragraph would ensure that U.S. workers are informed of the job opportunity and are provided the opportunity to apply and be considered for the job opportunity prior to an alien being offered permanent employment through a PERM labor certification, which would allow the Department to make the certification required by the INA. The mandatory pre-filing recruitment activities required of all employers under proposed section 656.17 are outlined below.
Placement of the Job Order— Proposed section 656.17(b)(1) would require the employer to place a job order with the SWA serving the place(s) of employment, consistent with the current rule. The Department proposes a revision to clarify that if the job opportunity covers more than one place of employment and the SWAs serving the places of employment are distinct, the employer would be required to place a job order with more than one SWA. Further, the proposed rule uses the term “place of employment” rather than “area of intended employment” to add specificity for the location(s) that determine placement of the job order with the particular SWA(s).
Placement of Advertisement on Employer's Website(s) —As proposed in section 656.17(b)(2), if an employer maintains one or more websites for its business operations, it must place an advertisement for the job opportunity that is the subject of the Application for Permanent Employment Certification on those websites. If the employer customarily uses its website(s) for its business operations to recruit for job opportunities that are not the subject of an Application for Permanent Employment Certification, the advertisement for the job opportunity that is the subject of the Application for Permanent Employment Certification must be placed in the same manner ( e.g., same web page) the employer customarily uses to recruit for job opportunities. If the employer does not customarily use its website(s) for its business operations to recruit for job opportunities, the employer must post the job opportunity that is the subject of the Application for Permanent Employment Certification on all such website(s) in a conspicuous location ( e.g., the employer's contact page). The objective of the advertisement is to apprise as many U.S. workers of the job opportunity as possible and, therefore, the advertisement must be placed in a conspicuous location on each website the employer maintains for its business operations. Under the proposed rule, the employer is required to use all websites it maintains for business purposes to ensure the broadest dissemination of the job opportunity.
This proposal is intended to better apprise U.S. workers of the job opportunity and also make it more difficult for employers who are not recruiting in good faith to conceal job opportunities from U.S. workers, including those who are employed by the employer but might be interested in applying for the offered position, or U.S. workers who have been laid off, for example.
Placement of Advertisement on Job Search Website(s) —Proposed section 656.17(b)(3) would require the employer to place an advertisement for the job opportunity on at least one job search website. This proposal specifies that compliance with paragraph (b)(3) requires placement of the advertisement on a job search website that is not the employer's own website ( i.e., the employer's business website in proposed paragraph (b)(2)) or the SWA's website ( i.e., job order posting in proposed paragraph (b)(1)).
The proposed rule requires employers to post advertisements on one or more job search website(s) using the same methods and website locations as the employer customarily uses for recruitment of its other job opportunities that are unrelated to requests for PERM labor certification. The intent is to ensure that while recruiting for a PERM labor certification job opportunity, the employer, at a minimum, uses all of the job search website methods it typically uses, and in the same manner that it typically uses, when recruiting outside of the PERM labor certification context to make jobs known to U.S. workers. In the event an employer does not customarily post advertisements on job search websites for job opportunities that are the same as the job offered in the Application for Permanent Employment Certification, compliance with proposed 656.17(b)(3) would require the employer to post an advertisement on one job search website that is appropriate for recruitment of the job opportunity.
For an employer whose business is job postings, providing job searches, or job matching ( e.g., an employer whose business includes a job search engine, job website, job board, job portal, or job aggregator, etc.; and/or an employer whose businesses performs tasks such as headhunting, staffing, or connecting employers and job seekers through job platforms, etc.) or, otherwise, the employer specializes in advertisement of job opportunities, compliance with proposed section 656.17 would require the employer to advertise on its own job search website(s) for proposed paragraph (b)(2) and, in addition, to advertise using a third-party commercial job search website for the proposed paragraph (b)(3).
Social Media Platform(s) —Proposed section 656.17(b)(4) would require an employer who customarily uses one or more social media platforms to advertise ( printed page 73954) job opportunities in the same occupational classification to similarly do so during pre-filing recruitment for PERM labor certification. Under the proposed rule, the employer must use the same method(s) and location(s) and post this advertisement in a similar manner as it would for job opportunities unrelated to requests for PERM labor certification. The intent of this requirement is to ensure that while recruiting for a PERM labor certification, the employer, at a minimum, uses all of the methods it commonly uses when recruiting outside of the PERM labor certification context to make jobs known to U.S. workers.
If an employer does not customarily use a social media platform to advertise job opportunities in the same occupational classification, proposed paragraph (b)(4) does not require an advertisement on a social media platform. In this way, proposed paragraph (b)(4) contrasts with proposed paragraphs (b)(3) and (b)(5), which would require an employer who does not normally advertise job opportunities in the same occupational classification using the method described to do so during PERM labor certification pre-filing recruitment.
Job Fairs or On-Campus Recruitment —Proposed section 656.17(b)(5) would require employers to advertise the job opportunity through at least one job fair, on-campus recruitment event, or college, university, or trade school placement office while the job order placed with the SWA, under proposed paragraph (b)(1), is actively circulating. Even if the employer does not normally recruit for the same or similar job opportunities through any one of these methods, compliance with proposed paragraph (b)(5) would require the employer to do so at least once, through one of the listed methods.
For employers whose recruitment methods for the same or similar job opportunity outside the PERM labor certification context includes recruitment through job fairs, on-campus recruitment events, and/or college, university, or trade school placement office, the employer would be required to recruit for the PERM-related job opportunity in the same way it recruits for non-PERM job opportunities. The Department recognizes that employers may customarily recruit through job fairs, on-campus recruiting events, and/or college, university, or trade school placement offices that may not be geographically close to the place(s) of employment because those job fairs are expected to attract qualified candidates or those colleges, universities, or trade schools offer programs of study that align with the job opportunity offered and whose students or alumni are likely to be good candidates for the employer's job opportunities. The Department does not intend for employers to artificially limit their recruitment under proposed paragraph (b)(5) based on geography. Rather, employers who customarily recruit through job fairs, on-campus recruitment events, and/or campus placement offices would use the same location(s) and manner for job opportunities that are the subject of Applications for Permanent Employment Certification.
The Department welcomes comments on the proposed pre-filing recruitment methods listed above, as well as additional ideas or methods of recruitment that could be used in the PERM program. Specifically, the Department requests comments on whether to include a requirement to contact all U.S. workers currently employed at the place(s) of employment, similar to the requirement in the H-2B program under temporary final rules (TFRs) for fiscal years 2023, 2024, and 2025.[111] For this requirement, employers would be required to contact U.S. workers currently employed at the place(s) of employment to inform them of the job opportunity and request their assistance in recruiting qualified U.S. workers who may be seeking employment. As explained in the H-2B TFR and relevant for recruiting U.S. workers for permanent employment under this NPRM, the Department believes this method of positive recruitment is a reasonable and cost-effective way of broadening dissemination of available job opportunities and increasing the likelihood that qualified U.S. workers will apply.[112] Moreover, encouraging existing employees to provide referrals is generally recognized as an effective means for an employer to identify prospective applicants who would be well matched to the available job opportunity.[113] The Department also invites comments on how innovations in technology will impact the relevant forms of recruitment and whether there are alternative or additional methods of broadly disseminating job opportunities that could more effectively test the U.S. labor market while reducing unnecessary employer burden, including approaches that would facilitate and consolidate the publication of job opportunities. Commenters are encouraged to identify mechanisms that could increase the visibility and accessibility of PERM job opportunities to U.S. workers, improve the Department's ability to ensure compliance and/or assess the effectiveness of recruitment, or reduce reliance on multiple employer-directed recruitment activities.
Length of Active Recruitment
Section 656.17(c) of the proposed rule requires active recruitment for each method in proposed paragraphs (b)(1) through (b)(4) of this section to be performed for at least 90 calendar days. However, if during the prior 12 months, the employer has routinely advertised for the same or similar job opportunities unrelated to requests for PERM labor certification for more than 90 calendar days, then the employer must advertise for the PERM labor certification job opportunity for the longer period of time it routinely used in the prior 12 months. To comply with this paragraph, the employer must use whichever period of time is longer—90 calendar days or the employer's customary length of recruitment for the same or similar job opportunities in the prior 12-month period.
For example, where the employer normally posts a job opportunity for 120 calendar days, the employer must similarly post the job opportunity that is the subject of the Application for Permanent Employment Certification for 120 calendar days. The intent is for the employer to allow as much active recruitment time in connection with PERM labor certification recruitment as the employer allows on active ( printed page 73955) recruitment for non-PERM labor certification recruitment. The proposed text requires that the job opportunity for which the employer seeks a PERM labor certification is as broadly and widely advertised as possible and in accordance with the employer's own customary business recruitment standards, in order to reach as many potentially qualified U.S. applicants as possible. The Department thinks that the proposed 90-day period for active recruitment of U.S. workers is both reasonable and appropriate because this proposed minimum timeframe is consistent with the amount of time unemployed American workers are actively searching for work and therefore a sufficient period of time to test the U.S. labor market. According to the October 2026 Employment Situation Summary Report, the BLS estimated that the average number of weeks unemployed persons were actively looking for work in September 2026 was 25.4 weeks ( i.e., approximately 6 months), while the median duration was 12.1 weeks ( i.e., approximately 3 months) and, when excluding the long-term unemployed ( i.e., persons actively seeking work 27 weeks or longer), nearly 1.1 million unemployed persons were actively looking for work for 15 to 26 weeks.[114] When examining the most recent estimates of unemployed American workers actively searching for work within the prime working-ages of 25 to 54 years, this proposed timeframe is likewise reasonable and appropriate. For October 2026, BLS also estimated that the median number of weeks unemployed persons were actively looking for work was 13.2 weeks for those 25 to 34 years old; 15.4 weeks for those 35 to 44 years old; and 14.6 weeks for those 45 to 54 years old.[115]
Additionally, the proposed 90-day advertising standard would provide a minimum level of consistency in recruitment efforts and ensure qualified U.S. workers have ample opportunity to apply and be considered for employment in the job opportunity. Because current regulations do not require any minimum timeframe, the amount of time employers make online advertisements available through their own website and other job search websites varies significantly, and often times these announcements are available for less time than the current 30-day job order placed with the SWA. For example, among the 200,124 applications granted PERM labor certification for professional occupations during FY 2021-FY 2025 that chose to advertise job opportunities through a website maintained by the employer, approximately 25 percent (50,361 applications) advertised for less than 15 calendar days, 34 percent (67,825 applications) advertised for 15 to 30 calendar days, and 41 percent (81,938 applications) advertised for 30 calendar days or more. Among the 387,518 applications where the employer chose to use an online job search website, approximately 62 percent (241,880 applications) advertised for less than 15 calendar days, 29 percent (113,165 applications) advertised for 15 to 30 calendar days, and 8 percent (32,464 applications) advertised for 30 calendar days or more.[116] Thus, the Department's most recent program experience demonstrates a clear need to propose a minimum regulatory standard that can achieve greater consistency in the length of time prospective qualified U.S. workers have to apply and be considered for employment in these job opportunities.
As discussed in detail below under section 656.17(f), the employer must document the length of its recruitment efforts for the Application for Permanent Employment Certification and, as directed by the CO, provide documentation of the length of the employer's recruitment efforts for Application for Permanent Employment Certification with proof of the length of the employer's normal or typical recruitment standard for the same or similar job opportunities.
Completion of Active Recruitment
Section 656.17(d) of the proposed rule would require the employer to complete the recruitment activities of proposed paragraphs (b)(1) through (b)(4), between 30 to 60 calendar days before the employer files the Application for Permanent Employment Certification. This provision protects U.S. workers by ensuring the recruitment for the job opportunity does not become stale prior to the Application for Permanent Employment Certification filing, and ensures that U.S. workers who may have availability for the job opportunity are made aware of the job in proximity to the filing of the Application for Permanent Employment Certification, rather than many months ahead of time, potentially prior to when currently available U.S. workers have begun their job search. In contrast, the current rule permits employers to complete recruitment as much as 5 months in advance of filing.
Advertising Content Requirements
Proposed section 656.17(e) largely continues current advertising content requirements, with revisions to ensure that U.S. workers are better apprised of the job opportunities offered. In addition, under this proposed rule, the Department would expand the scope of the advertising content requirements. The current rule has been interpreted as limiting advertising content requirements to the mandatory print advertisements.[117] In contrast, proposed 656.17(e) would apply the advertising content requirements to all recruitment conducted for compliance with 656.17.
Through the years after the implementation of the current rule, employers began to include technical jargon and shortened terms in PERM-related newspaper advertisements. More often than not, advertisements used in recruitment for PERM labor certification have confused potential job applicants, rather than sufficiently apprising U.S. workers about the actual job duties, qualifications, and skills required. In addition, over time, the advertisements placed for compliance with the current rule have been recognized by applicants as PERM-specific advertisements and in many cases, U.S. workers no longer apply because they believe such ads signify the lack of a bona fide job opportunity.[118] This perspective from U.S. workers suggests that some employers are not making PERM-related job opportunities truly open to qualified U.S. workers, a problem this proposed rule addresses. Further, the proposed rule would require consistency throughout the recruitment process by requiring employers to solicit and recruit for U.S. workers using specific content intended to apprise U.S. workers of the job requirements, wages, locations and other pertinent details of the available job opportunities.
While proposed section 656.17(e)(1) would continue to prohibit advertising from containing terms and conditions ( printed page 73956) that are less favorable to U.S. workers than those offered to the alien, it adds that the terms and conditions offered in the advertising also, at a minimum, must comply with assurances set forth in proposed section 656.13. The proposed rule would ensure that U.S. workers are not disfavored in the recruitment process or dissuaded from applying for job opportunities that are subject to Applications for Permanent Employment Certification due to misleading or inaccurate information or prohibited terms.
The list of required advertising content in proposed section 656.17(e)(2), again, largely continues current requirements. However, the proposed list includes intentional revisions to better ensure that U.S. workers are apprised of the pertinent details of the available job opportunities and available U.S. workers can apply for the position. For example, rather than continuing to require advertisements to “[n]ame the employer” and “[d]irect applicants to report or send resumes, as appropriate for the occupation, to the employer,” the proposed rule requires advertisements to contain “[t]he employer's name and contact information.” The revised advertising content requirements proposed in section 656.17(e) are intended to both provide the necessary information for U.S. workers to determine whether they are interested in and qualified for the job opportunity and better match non-PERM recruitment situations.
Finally, proposed section 656.17(e)(3) would permit employers to recruit for multiple positions simultaneously using the same advertisement as long as that single advertisement complies with all the pre-filing recruitment time, placement, and duration requirements for each job opportunity; and includes sufficient information to inform U.S. workers of each job opportunity.
As proposed, all of the employer's advertisements for recruitment under this proposed section must include the following: the employer's name and contact information; a description of the job opportunity sufficient to apprise U.S. workers of the services or labor to be performed and the actual minimum qualifications or requirements to perform the services or labor sought; the place(s) of employment or geographic area of employment, including a statement as to whether the position offers remote work, full-time telework, or a hybrid combination of telework and on-site work, with enough specificity to apprise applicants of any travel requirements and where applicants will likely have to reside to perform the services or labor; the wage offer, or if there are multiple wage offers, the range of applicable wage offers (each of which must equal or exceed the highest of the prevailing wage or the Federal, State, or local minimum wage); a statement that overtime is available to the worker and the wage offer(s) for working any overtime hours (as applicable); and a statement that the job is a full-time position and, if the employer has more than one job opening, the total number of job openings the employer intends to fill.
The Department also proposes a new provision at section 656.17(e)(3) to provide some flexibility for employers who may be able to combine multiple positions in a single advertisement, while complying with the content requirements in proposed section 656.17(e). Permitting this flexibility would be financially efficient for employers, while ensuring the employer's advertisements contain content necessary to appropriately apprise U.S. workers of the job opportunities offered.
Recruitment Report
Section 656.17(f) of the proposed rule discusses the recruitment report the employer must prepare and maintain to document its recruitment efforts under part 656 and the results. A notable change from the current regulation is that under the proposed rule, the employer would submit the recruitment report (including the resumes and applications of all U.S. workers who applied for the job opportunity) concurrently with the Application for Permanent Employment Certification. The employer's submission of the recruitment report with the Application for Permanent Employment Certification will allow the Department's review of the recruitment report for each Application for Permanent Employment Certification, rather than solely reviewing recruitment reports during audit examinations or when submitted in connection with supervised recruitment, as is the case under the current regulation. In other words, under the proposed regulation, employers will be required to submit a recruitment report for each application and the Department will be able to review the report for every single application to assess employers' pre-filing recruitment activities and consideration of U.S. workers. As discussed for proposed sections 656.10(a)(3) and (4), the Department would also require the employer to submit the supporting evidence for the recruitment report—copies of all advertising and U.S. worker applicants' resumes or applications for the job opportunity. While the CO may review only the recruitment report in all cases, the employer's full documentation of compliance with recruitment and consideration of U.S. workers would be available for the CO to review, without additional correspondence. Also, as noted in connection with proposed sections 656.10(a)(3) and (4), employers would not incur additional new burdens as a result of this requirement because they are already required to prepare and maintain recruitment reports for each Application for Permanent Employment Certification and to provide the recruitment report and supporting documentation to the CO, upon request ( e.g., audit examination).
The Department proposes to require more specific content in the recruitment report under the proposed rule than the current rule mandates. Current section 656.17(g)(1) includes general language that requires the recruitment report to “describe[e] the recruitment steps undertaken and the results achieved, the number of hires, and, if applicable, the number of U.S. workers rejected, categorized by the lawful job-related reasons for such rejections.” This approach provides recruitment results to the CO in the form of data, which does not adequately reflect the success—or failure—of specific recruitment activities and does not adequately reflect the employer's consideration of U.S. workers who apply for the job opportunity that is the subject of the Application for Permanent Employment Certification. The Department has determined that the current recruitment report content does not provide sufficient information for the CO to form a clear picture of the labor market test ( e.g., which recruitment activities successfully reach U.S. workers, how the employer considered U.S. applicants, why the employer did not offer the job opportunity to each U.S. worker who applied, or that the employer did offer the job opportunity to U.S. workers who applied, but they declined) or to verify information in recruitment reports, as appropriate, for program integrity.
The recruitment report under proposed 656.17(f)(1) would include specific factual information to provide a fuller picture of the employer's recruitment activities and results. As proposed, the recruitment report submitted with the Application for Permanent Employment Certification would identify the name of each recruitment activity and the date(s) of advertisement; identify the uniform resource locator (URL) or other location of each advertisement, and, for each advertisement placed with a website, ( printed page 73957) platform, publisher, or the SWA, the platform or publisher generated confirmation number, receipt, or order identifier and the posting and expiration dates it generated; state the name and contact information and date of application of each U.S. worker (including any the employer laid off) who applied for or was referred for the job opportunity up to the date the employer finalizes its recruitment report for submission with the Application for Permanent Employment Certification, categorized by the advertising method through which the U.S. worker applied, indicating whether the U.S. worker met the actual minimum requirements, with the disposition of each U.S. worker specified, including whether the U.S. worker received a job offer and, if so, whether they accepted or declined; and, if applicable, for each U.S. worker who applied but was not hired, explain the lawful job-related reason(s) for not hiring that U.S. worker. The recruitment report would also identify the name and title of each employee of the employer who reviewed, screened, or interviewed each of the applicants listed.
As provided by the INA in section 212(a)(5)(A)(i) and (ii) and both the current and proposed rule in section 656.24(b)(2), the Department will not issue a PERM labor certification to an employer if able, willing, and qualified U.S. worker(s) are available for the job opportunity that is—or would be—the subject of the Application for Permanent Employment Certification. To demonstrate eligibility for a PERM labor certification, the employer must document its reasons for not hiring each U.S. applicant who applied for the job opportunity in a recruitment report. In the event U.S. workers apply for the job opportunity and satisfy the minimum qualifications, as described on the Application for Permanent Employment Certification, or for a college or university teacher position or positions offered to aliens who have exceptional ability in the sciences or the arts, who are at least as qualified as the alien, the employer must consider each of those U.S. worker applicants. Unless the employer identifies lawful, job-related reason(s) for not hiring each qualified U.S. worker, the Department considers the U.S. worker(s) available and will not issue a PERM certification. The employer may offer the job opportunity to such U.S. workers to determine whether the U.S. worker(s) are willing and available and if no qualified U.S. workers accept the job offer, the employer could continue to pursue issuance of a PERM labor certification. However, if a qualified U.S. worker accepts the job offer, the Department would not issue a PERM labor certification, as the job opportunity can be filled by a U.S. worker; permanent employment of an alien is not necessary. In addition, the proposed recruitment report content would provide the CO with information necessary to assess whether the employer has satisfied its burden of proof with respect to this statutory and regulatory requirement.
Adding the proposed specificity to recruitment report content requirements would improve clarity for employers and the consistency of recruitment information submitted to the NPC. It will also allow the Department to make a more informed determination about the availability of U.S. workers when carrying out its statutory obligation to determine that there are not sufficient able, willing, and qualified U.S. workers available for the job opportunity. In addition, the proposal to require recruitment reports to identify which U.S. workers applied for the job opportunity through which recruitment activities would better enable the Department to update recruitment requirements to balance burden against the effectiveness of advertising activities in the future.
Duty to Update Recruitment Report— Proposed section 656.17(f)(2), consistent with the proposed layoff attestation in section 656.13(e), would require the employer to update its recruitment report after initially submitting it with the Application for Permanent Employment Certification in the event of a layoff. A recruitment report update would be required whenever the employer experiences a layoff of any U.S. worker(s) in the same or similar occupation as the Application for Permanent Employment Certification in the place(s) of intended employment, until the employer withdraws the Application for Permanent Employment Certification or related Form I-140 petition, the CO denies PERM labor certification or USCIS denies the related Form I-140 petition, or USCIS approves the related Form I-140 petition. The employer would prepare an updated recruitment report each time it experiences a layoff. Each time, the employer would sign and date the updated recruitment report. The recruitment report updates would not be submitted to the CO, unless requested. This proposal supports program integrity and protects U.S. worker access to job opportunities before they are permanently filed with an alien by enabling the CO to confirm compliance with required consideration of laid off U.S. workers while the Application for Permanent Employment Certification and Form I-140 petition are pending.
Similar to proposed section 656.17(f)(1), proposed section 656.17(f)(2) specifies the content to be required for recruitment report updates. The employer would be required to update their recruitment report with (i) a detailed explanation of the method(s) used and date(s) on which the employer notified all potentially qualified laid off U.S. workers for the job opportunity, who had been employed in the same or similar occupation that is the subject of the Application for Permanent Employment Certification in the place(s) of intended employment, and (ii) the name and contact information of each U.S. worker notified, identifying each one who applied to the job opportunity up to the date of the preparation of the recruitment report, categorized by the notification method through which they applied for the job opportunity, and specifying the disposition of each U.S. worker and whether the job opportunity was offered to the U.S. worker and whether the U.S. worker accepted or declined.
Duty to Maintain Recruitment Documentation —The Department is proposing to relocate the requirement to retain documentation to prove compliance with section 656.17 recruitment requirements from current section 656.17(e)(1) to proposed revised section 656.17(g). The proposed rule would continue to require employers to retain documentation associated with their pre-filing recruitment for the Application for Permanent Employment Certifications and add the required retention of documentation associated with their ongoing, post-filing consideration of any laid off workers, if applicable.
As is current practice, the CO may request the employer's documentation to verify compliance with timing, content, and activity requirements, as well as recruitment results. To demonstrate compliance, the employer must document each advertisement's date(s), content ( e.g., proof of publication or screen shot of the ad placed on a website), and location(s) ( e.g., physical location and/or website address) and each notification to laid off U.S. workers.
Finally, the Department proposes a new section 656.17(h) that would provide some flexibility for the OFLC Administrator to provide further clarifying guidance regarding recruitment requirements, methods, and practices that do not rise to the level of requiring rulemaking. The Department considers this to be an important option for the OFLC Administrator to address ( printed page 73958) questions or resolve confusion that may arise as recruitment methods continue to evolve.
9. 20 CFR 656.18, Optional Pre-Filing Recruitment Requirements for College and University Teachers and Professional Athletes and Professional Sports Team Coaches
The Department proposes to retain the optional competitive recruitment and selection procedures currently available to an employer seeking to permanently hire a college or university teacher at section 656.18, with revisions. Consistent with the current regulation, the employer would be permitted to conduct recruitment for a job opportunity involving a college or university teacher using the normal pre-filing recruitment procedures set forth in section 656.17. Alternatively, if the employer selected the alien using a competitive recruitment process, the employer would have the option of following the optional pre-filing recruitment procedures set forth in section 656.18.
The Department proposes expanding the optional pre-filing recruitment alternative available in proposed section 656.18 to include professional athlete or professional sports team coach job opportunities and rename proposed section 656.18 to reflect the changed content. The current regulations do not address pre-filing recruitment or documentation requirements for such job opportunities. As a result, while employers seeking to permanently employ professional athletes and professional sports team coaches must engage in pre-filing recruitment, they have not been required to comply with the standard pre-filing recruitment requirements of current section 656.17. Consistent with the Department's goals of clarifying program requirements and maintaining program integrity, the Department proposes to specify recruitment and documentation requirements for Applications for Permanent Employment Certification involving professional athletes and professional sports team coaches in this proposed rule, while maintaining the current flexibility for alternative recruitment practices appropriate to these job opportunities. The Department proposes to permit an employer seeking to permanently employ an alien as a professional athlete or professional sports team coach to either comply with the normal pre-filing recruitment procedures set forth in proposed section 656.17, or the alternative process in proposed section 656.18. The Department invites comments regarding the proposal to permit such employers to demonstrate a competitive recruitment and selection process under proposed section 656.18, rather than requiring compliance with proposed section 656.17. In addition, the Department invites comments that provide information about the means and methods of recruitment used for professional athletes and professional sports team coaches ( e.g., private executive recruiters, talent scouts, multi-session tryouts or interview panels), which may not be captured in the standard pre-filing recruitment requirements of 656.17. The Department invites the public to provide input that can better inform the pre-filing recruitment standards and documentation requirements to ensure potentially qualified and available U.S. workers are afforded a fair chance to apply for professional athlete and professional sports team coaching positions.
To use the optional recruitment and documentation procedures at section 656.18, both currently and under the proposed rule, the employer must be able to document that the alien was selected for the job opportunity using a competitive recruitment and selection process. Also consistent with the current regulation, this proposed rule would require the employer to prepare and retain documentation showing compliance with the requirements of the optional pre-filing recruitment procedures for submission to the CO, upon request. However, under the proposed rule, such employers also would submit the recruitment report and all evidence of compliance with the advertising requirements and consideration of U.S. workers (including resumes and applications of all U.S. workers who applied for the job opportunity) proposed in section 656.18 when filing their Application for Permanent Employment Certification— a change for all employers, as discussed in proposed section 656.10.
The documentation necessary to show compliance using a competitive recruitment and selection process for a college or university teacher job opportunity under proposed 656.18 would include the following list, which the Department retains from the current rule with minor conforming modifications: (1) a written statement, signed and dated by an official who has actual hiring authority from the employer, providing a detailed description of the recruitment procedures undertaken; (2) a final report or other documentation from the organizational or administrative body making the recommendation or selection of the alien at the completion of the competitive recruitment and selection process; (3) evidence of all other recruitment sources utilized; (4) a copy of at least one print or electronic advertisement for the job opportunity placed in a national professional journal, giving the name and the date(s) of publication; and which states the job title, duties, and requirements; and (5) a written statement attesting to the degree(s) of the alien's educational or professional qualifications and academic achievements. For a professional athlete or professional sports team coach job opportunity, the Department would require an employer using the optional recruitment procedures in proposed section 656.18 to document that the alien was selected for the job opportunity using a competitive recruitment and selection process and retain, at minimum, the first three documentation requirements listed above. The Department invites comments on additional documentation requirements that would be appropriate to require for professional athletes or professional sports team coaches akin to the additional requirements for college and university teachers, listed as (4) and (5) above.
In paragraph (b), the Department proposes to require employers who use the competitive recruitment and selection option under proposed section 656.18 to both retain the documentation listed in paragraph (a) and submit it to the CO when filing the Application for Permanent Employment Certification. The Department notes that attestations regarding the competitive recruitment and selection process are collected in Appendix D of the Application for Permanent Employment Certification.
While the recruitment and documentation requirements for employers who use these optional pre-filing recruitment procedures are consistent with current regulations, the proposed rule would change the timing and content of recruitment report submissions required of employers using the optional pre-filing recruitment requirements of proposed section 656.18. As addressed in proposed section 656.10, the Department proposes a consistent filing requirements and consistent recruitment report content requirements for all Applications for Permanent Employment Certification. The Department proposes to eliminate current section 656.18(c), which requires an employer to submit the Application for Permanent Employment Certification for a college or university teacher within 18 months of the alien's selection through a competitive recruitment and selection process. Instead, these applications would be ( printed page 73959) subject to the same filing requirements as all other Applications for Permanent Employment Certification, including proposed section 656.10(b), which requires application submission within 30 calendar days after completion of pre-filing recruitment requirements. This proposed change is discussed in section 656.10.
In addition, employers who engage in pre-filing recruitment under proposed section 656.18 would be required to prepare a recruitment report in compliance with proposed 656.17(f). As discussed in proposed section 656.17, the Department proposes to require additional specificity in employers' recruitment reports to better enable the CO to assess the employer's test of the U.S. labor market and, as appropriate, verify information. For employers preparing a recruitment report following optional pre-filing recruitment under proposed section 656.18, the Department proposes to expand recruitment report content requirements from only the “total number” of applicants for the job opportunity and the specific lawful job-related reasons why the alien is more qualified than each U.S. worker who applied to the additional specificity that would be required under proposed section 656.17(f)—the name of each recruitment activity and date(s) of advertisement and the name and contact information of each U.S. worker who applied, categorized by the advertising method through which they applied, and specifying each applicant's disposition and whether the job opportunity was offered to them and whether they accepted or declined.
The Department highlights that, consistent with the Department's efforts to modernize recruitment requirements, proposed section 656.18(a)(4) would permit either print or electronic advertisements. The Department invites comments about whether to retain the option for employers to place the one required advertisement in a national professional journal in print.
In addition, consistent with organizational changes proposed throughout this rule, the Department proposes to consolidate current sections 656.18(a)(1)(i) and 656.18(a)(1)(ii), which correspond to recruitment report content, with similar language in proposed section 656.17(f). Finally, the Department proposes deleting current section 656.18(d) to avoid redundancy with similar language in proposed section 656.18(a). These minor revisions are consistent with the Department's overall goal of simplifying and clarifying part 656.
10. Proposed 20 CFR 656.19, Processing of an Application
As previously discussed in connection with proposed section 656.10, the Department proposes to move the current content of section 656.19 (“Live-in household domestic service workers”) to proposed section 656.10(e), so that all filing requirements are located in section 656.10 of this proposed rule. Then, the Department proposes to repurpose current section 656.19 to address the processing of Applications for Permanent Employment Certification submitted to OFLC and retitle section 656.19 as “Processing of an application.”
Consistent with the Department's proposed reorganization of part 656, the Department proposes to relocate provisions from other areas of the current regulation that address the review and processing of Applications for Permanent Employment Certification submitted to OFLC to proposed section 656.19. Specifically, the Department proposes to move content about the CO's assessment of the job opportunity described in the Application for Permanent Employment Certification from current sections 656.17(h) (“Job duties and requirements”) and 656.17(j)(2) (“Conditions of employment”) to proposed section 656.19(d). Similarly, text currently found at section 656.17(i) (“Actual minimum requirements”) would be relocated to proposed section 656.19(e). These proposals would streamline the regulations, while improving clarity for employers regarding how applications are assigned for review and promoting more effective and fair processing by OFLC.
Proposed section 656.19 would address the CO's assignment of Applications for Permanent Employment Certification for review, rules for correspondence between the NPC and employers during processing, including requirements for responding, and the CO's assessment of the job opportunity offered in the Application for Permanent Employment Certification. The provisions proposed in this section would apply to all employers who must obtain a PERM labor certification before filing a Form I-140 petition with USCIS, including sheepherders, professional athletes and professional sports team coaches, and job opportunities currently designated as Schedule A (including aliens with exceptional ability).
In proposed paragraph (a), the Department considered codifying its application assignment process but decided against including these details in the proposed regulatory text. The NPC currently processes applications by assigning Applications for Permanent Employment Certification for staff review in the order received, using application filing dates. Specifically, the CO or other designated staff assigns for review all of the Applications for Permanent Employment Certification received in a certain month and year, before moving to assign applications filed in the next month and year. After being assigned, each designated staff organizes their monthly workload and initiates review of each application for compliance with PERM labor certification program requirements. After initial review, the CO or designated staff may initiate other processing steps, such as an audit examination under section 656.20 or supervised recruitment under section 656.21, or issue a final determination, approving or denying the application, pursuant to section 656.24. Processing times for any particular Application for Permanent Employment Certification received and assigned for review within that processing month may vary, depending on the unique circumstances of each application, contents of each application, and the applicable processing steps. While the Department intends to continue this practice for the foreseeable future, new technology or program enhancements may create necessary modifications. Any future changes, including minor tweaks to processing or order, would require the Department to propose another rule to codify its revised practices. Accordingly, the Department does not find it efficient or necessary to codify its internal review process.
Proposed paragraph (b) contains the correspondence rules that would apply to both the CO and the employer while an Application for Permanent Employment Certification is in process. For any notice or request the CO sends to an employer that requires a response from the employer, the CO will use electronic delivery, unless the employer was permitted to file by mail, as set forth in proposed section 656.10(c). For employers permitted to file by mail, the CO will send any notice or request via traditional methods to assure next-day delivery. Similarly, employers would be required to submit their responses electronically by the due date, except that an employer permitted to file by mail under proposed section 656.10(c), may use traditional methods to assure next-day delivery. This proposed section would implement the current practice regarding due dates in the H-2A program, under which if a response ( printed page 73960) due date falls on a Sunday or Federal holiday, the employer's deadline would be the next business day, rather than the current practice regarding due dates in the H-2B and CW-1 programs, under which if a response due date falls on a Saturday, Sunday, or Federal holiday, the employer's deadline would be the next business day. See20 CFR 655.140(b), 655.11(f), and 655.430(b), respectively. Mandatory electronic filing, with limited exceptions, and for those permitted to file by mail, access to delivery services on Saturday to postmark a response on the due date, the Department proposes to adopt the H-2A program's approach to due dates. This paragraph provides clarity for employers and supports the Department's efforts to process Applications for Permanent Employment Certification efficiently. These rules apply to all correspondence between the CO and the employer during processing, including any CO-issued RFIs outside audit procedures or supervised recruitment procedures. However, the proposed rule reiterates these correspondence rules in proposed section 656.20 (Audit) ( see proposed § 656.20(e)) and proposed section 656.21 (Supervised Recruitment) to provide further clarity to employers subject to audit or supervised recruitment. See proposed sections 656.20(e) and 656.21(e). The Department invites comments about whether proposed section 656.19(b) provides sufficient notice of correspondence rules or if those rules should also be included in part 656 in sections 656.20 and 656.21. Providing sufficient notice of correspondence is important because failure to respond to an RFI, audit notice, supervised recruitment notice, or any other notice or request that requires a response, or responding after the due date may result in a denial.
Proposed paragraph (c) discusses response dates for correspondence between the CO and employer. For Applications for Permanent Employment Certification received electronically, the date that the response is submitted is considered the response date. For the time of submission, the Department will use the Eastern time zone of the United States. For Applications for Permanent Employment Certification received by mail in accordance with section 656.10(c)(2) or (3), the date that the response is submitted is the postmark date of the employer's response. This approach is consistent with the Department's filing date proposal, as discussed in proposed section 656.10(f)(2), and current practice in the Department's temporary labor certification program regulations for response submission to the NPC.[119] This applies to any notice or request sent to the employer during Application for Permanent Employment Certification processing.
Proposed paragraph (d) contains content relocated from current sections 656.17(h) (“Job duties and requirements”) and 656.17(j)(2) (“Conditions of employment”), while proposed paragraph (e) contains the content relocated from current section 656.17(i) (“Actual minimum requirements”). The Department intends to retain the CO's current standard of review and documentation requirements for assessing the job duties and requirements specified on the Application for Permanent Employment Certification. Modifications to the current regulatory text in proposed sections 656.19(d) and 656.19(e) are intended to improve clarity, not to change the existing standard of review. For example, the proposed section 656.19(d)(1) retains the requirement that the job requirements specified in the Application for Permanent Employment Certification must be those normally required for the occupation and must not exceed the SVP level assigned to the occupation as shown in the O*NET Job Zones, unless adequately documented as arising from business necessity. The Department proposes to revise the provision to identify the application as “ Application for Permanent Employment Certification” and add “(SVP)” and “Occupational Network” for clarity. Similarly, in proposed sections 656.19(d)(2)(ii)(A) and (B), the Department proposes to add “effectively” to modify “communicate in English” to better convey the standard applied to foreign language requirements carried over from current section 656.17(h)(2)(ii). Further, the Department proposes to add “executed contracts or agreements” in proposed section 656.19(d)(2)(ii)(A) to clarify the type of documentation that may be required to show plans to market products or deliver services in a foreign country. The Department also proposes to add “clients, contractors, or employees” in proposed section 656.19(d)(2)(ii)(B) to replace “customers” and clarify with whom an alien may need to communicate. Lastly, the Department proposes to require an additional explanation as to why the responsibilities of the position for which permanent labor certification is sought necessitates regular contact and communication with these individuals.
Again, the Department proposes these modifications for clarity and better organizational flow, not to change the existing standard of review.
11. Proposed 20 CFR 656.20, Audit
The PERM labor certification audit process is a pre-decisional integrity measure that enables the CO to request and review documentation the employer is required to maintain, currently under section 656.10(f), to verify compliance with program requirements and form attestations before issuing a PERM labor certification. This proposed rule seeks to modify and clarify the audit examination process for employers under part 656.
Discretion
Consistent with the current regulation, the proposed rule allows the CO to select an Application for Permanent Employment Certification for audit review prior to the issuance of a final determination. The current rule lists two reasons in section 656.20(a) why the CO might select an application for audit—random selection or for quality control purposes. These examples could be misunderstood as limiting the CO's discretion, which was not the Department's intent. The proposed rule revises this language to clarify that the CO has the discretion to choose an Application for Permanent Employment Certification for audit for any reason(s) the CO might deem necessary to ensure compliance with the laws and regulations governing the PERM labor certification process. This discretion is necessary so that the CO can effectively use the audit tool to verify compliance and to bolster the integrity of the program. The CO may—or may not—select an application for audit based on any variety of internal and/or external factors, such as filing trends, specific industries or types of employers, complaints received from members of the public or referrals from other government agencies, program needs, etc., but ultimately the proposed rule clarifies that the CO has the necessary discretion to choose an Application for Permanent Employment Certification at any time prior to the issuance of a final determination. Under the proposed rule, the Department seeks to ensure the CO's ability to select applications for audit is clear, as the CO's discretion with this integrity tool strengthens the overall integrity of the program. ( printed page 73961)
Audit Notice
Proposed 656.20(b) continues current practice of requiring the CO to issue a written notice to the employer ( i.e., Notice of Audit Examination), after selecting an Application for Permanent Employment Certification for audit examination. In addition, proposed paragraph (b) codifies the CO's current practice of sending a copy of the audit notice to the employer's agent or attorney, if applicable, to ensure proper notice. The proposed audit notice will contain similar content to current audit notices, such as informing the employer of the selection of its application for audit review, identifying the documentation the employer is required to provide in response to the audit, specifying a due date by which the employer must respond to the CO for the audit, and advising the employer of the consequences of noncompliance with the audit notice.
The Department proposes revising the audit response due date in section 656.20(b)(2) to support processing efficiency. Currently, section 656.20(a)(2) requires the CO to specify the same due date in all audit notices—30 calendar days from the audit notice issuance date. Proposed section 656.20(b)(2) would permit the CO to specify a date “no more than 30 calendar days” from the audit notice issuance date as the due date for the employer's response. The Department notes that employers attest at the time of filing the Application for Permanent Employment Certification that they have prepared documentation demonstrating program compliance. Compliant employers will therefore be ready to submit a response to the audit notice quickly. This proposal permits the CO to require a faster audit response than 30 days without harm to the employer, particularly as the Department proposes to retain the option for an employer to request a one-time extension of up to 30 calendar days, if needed, in proposed section 656.20(c).
In addition, the Department proposes revising the audit notice's content to better advise employers of the potential consequences for failure to comply with the audit process. Current section 656.20(a)(3) has been relocated to proposed section 656.20(b)(3), and continues to require that the audit notice advise the employer that failure to respond by the due date will result in denial and ineligibility for review by BALCA under section 656.26. Separately—and not included in required audit notice content subparagraphs—current 656.20(b) addresses substantial failure to respond to the audit notice, stating that it may result in denial or supervised recruitment. The current provision fails to ensure that the employer is notified of all potential consequences of noncompliance with audit examination ( e.g., debarment) in the audit notice. The Department proposes to relocate the content of current section 656.20(b) to proposed section 656.20(b)(4), with revisions for readability and to include all potential consequences of failure to fully comply with the audit process. Proposed section 656.20(b)(4) improves notice to employers by ensuring the audit notice advises of all potential consequences of noncompliance with the CO's audit examination: denial of the Application for Permanent Employment Certification under section 656.24; imposition of supervised recruitment under section 656.21 for the current application or future application filings for up to two years; or debarment under section 656.31(f) from filing any labor certification applications or labor condition applications or receiving any approvals from OFLC for up to three years. These consequences are not new. The Department is proposing to both simplify the language in the audit notice and make it more complete.
One-Time Extension Request
In proposed section 656.20(c), the Department proposes to continue to permit an employer to request an extension of a response deadline once during audit examination. The Department proposes one change to the current one-time extension request provision. Currently, the one-time extension request provision in paragraph (c) refers only to the initial Notice of Audit Examination due date (current subparagraph 656.20(a)(2)). It does not reference requests for supplemental information and/or documentation the CO may make under current paragraph 656.20(d). The Department proposes to remove this limitation and permit an employer to request an extension of any one response deadline during audit examination. This proposed change is consistent with a similar provision in section 656.21 for supervised recruitment processing, which permits one extension request to any one response deadline during supervised recruitment. In addition, this proposed change acknowledges that a response to a supplemental audit information and/or documentation request may require more time to prepare and submit to the CO than the employer's initial response, which would largely encompass information and/or documents the employer attested to having prepared before filing the Application for Permanent Employment Certification.
Continuing current procedure, the employer must request an extension in writing, before the due date of the response, and the CO at their discretion, may grant one extension of up to 30 additional calendar days for the employer to respond. Where granted, the CO issues a written notice to the employer that specifies the new date by which the employer must respond with the requested information and/or documentation. This proposal clarifies in section 656.20(c) that this is a “one-time extension,” and the extension will not automatically be granted but rather will be granted at the CO's discretion. Only one extension request during audit examination will be approved by the CO.
Actions During Processing
The Department proposes to revise current section 656.20(d) by adding a heading (“Actions during processing”), updating content in paragraph (d)(1) for clarity and to reflect the proposed response deadline of up to 30 days, and retaining paragraph (d)(2) without change. Existing section 656.20 paragraphs (d) and (d)(1) state that before making a final determination the CO may “request supplemental information and/or documentation,” while current paragraph (d)(2) states that the CO “may require the employer to conduct supervised recruitment under 656.21.” In proposed paragraph (d), the Department continues its current practice, where, if the CO determines further information is needed to verify program compliance, the CO may request supplemental information from the employer and/or impose supervised recruitment requirements. The CO may request supplemental information more than once during audit examination, each time providing up to 30 calendar days for response. This provision is intended to ensure that employers are aware the CO may request supplemental information to have full information before rendering a final determination on the Application for Permanent Employment Certification. The Department also clarifies that the CO will not consider any information the employer submits that is beyond the approved deadline(s) for the audit response(s), that is not in response to a CO's request, or that is not provided in the manner specified by the CO's request for supplemental information.
These proposed regulatory changes will add more clarity to the process by clearly specifying when and how supplemental information must be ( printed page 73962) provided to the CO to ensure employers submit the necessary information when requested by the CO and that requesting and submitting supplemental information is done as efficiently as possible to avoid unnecessary delays in the audit examination.
Correspondence in Audit
Furthering technological efficiency measures and updates, and modernization efforts developed and implemented over time for OFLC's programs, the Department is now proposing to codify in section 656.20(e) that the CO will issue all notices or requests electronically to the employer—and the employer's agent or attorney, if applicable—during audit using the electronic mail (email) address the employer designates in the Application for Permanent Employment Certification. There is an exception for employers permitted to file by mail, as set forth in § 656.10(c). For these employers, the CO will send any notice or request via traditional methods to assure next day delivery. This proposal increases transparency and supports the Department's modernization goals.
The Department proposes in paragraph (e) to also require employers to communicate electronically, except where the employer has been granted permission to file by mail in section 656.10(c), in which case the employer must send responses to the CO using a method that assures next-day delivery. As discussed above, the Department is proposing to adopt the current practice regarding response due dates in the H-2A program—employer responses must be sent by the due date specified by the CO, or by the next business day if the due date falls on a Sunday or Federal holiday. This proposal furthers the Department's goals to modernize and streamline processes to support processing efficiency.
Failure To Comply With Audit
In proposed section 656.20(f), the Department proposes to clarify the actions the CO may take as the result of an audit examination, and address the potential for debarment based on noncompliance with audit examination requirements. Under the current and proposed rules, the CO may: order the employer to conduct supervised recruitment under 656.21 before the CO issues a final determination on the current Application for Permanent Employment Certification; deny the Application for Permanent Employment Certification under section 656.24; deny the Application for Permanent Employment Certification under section 656.24 and order supervised recruitment under 656.21 for the employer's future filings for up to 2 years; and/or deny the Application for Permanent Employment Certification under section 656.24 and pursue debarment against the employer, agent, and/or attorney for up to 3 years under 656.31(f). Although debarment is not a new possible consequence of noncompliance with audit examination, it is not mentioned in current section 656.20 of the current rule; rather, it appears in current section 656.31(f)(1)(iv). Moreover, proposed paragraph (f) better clarifies that failure to comply with audit requirements will lead to denial under section 656.24 and potential debarment under section 656.31, while an inadequate audit response will be denied under section 656.24. The CO may also determine it is appropriate to impose supervised recruitment for future applications for up to two years, in addition to denial. The Department proposes to change language referring to “a substantial failure” to provide required documents to “failed to comply with audit requirements ordered by the CO” in the audit examination. This proposed change simplifies the standard of review with a more objective test.
During audit examination, the employer must fully respond to the audit, including any supplemental RFI issued to the employer, and must return the response to the CO in the manner specified by the audit notice. Failure to provide the information requested will result in a denial because the CO will lack the information it requires to properly assess the application for compliance. The proposed changes ensure that employers are fully aware of the consequences of not complying with audit examinations.
Potential Referrals
The Department proposes a new section 656.20(g) to inform employers that the CO will refer information provided in response to an audit to other enforcement agencies where the findings indicate a possible violation of law within an agency's jurisdiction, and will provide such information upon an agency's request. Such referrals are in addition to internal referrals provided for elsewhere in part 656 and serve as an additional means of deterring fraud and suspicious activity. Under this proposed rule, the CO will refer audit findings and underlying documentation for an employer, or an attorney or agent, to any enforcement agency, including the DOL-OIG, DHS, DOS, EEOC, and DOJ. This includes referring any findings that an employer discouraged an eligible U.S. worker from applying, or failed to hire, discharged or otherwise discriminated against an eligible U.S. worker to DOJ, Civil Rights Division, Immigrant and Employee Rights Section and/or the U.S. Equal Employment Opportunity Commission. Proposed section 656.20(g) serves as an additional means of deterring fraud and suspicious activity and notifies the public fully of the Department's intention to cooperate with fellow agencies to the maximum extent possible to protect the integrity of the permanent labor certification program.
12. Proposed 20 CFR 656.21, Supervised Recruitment
The Department proposes clarifications to the supervised recruitment provisions in section 656.21. OFLC uses supervised recruitment as a means of enhancing program integrity by directing the employer through specific recruitment steps under part 656. Through this integrity measure, OFLC ensures the adequacy of the employer's test of the labor market for U.S. workers and ensures the employer's consideration of U.S. applicants for the available job opportunity.
When the CO places an employer into supervised recruitment, the CO guides the employer through an additional recruitment process. Consistent with the current rule, under the proposed rule, the CO may impose supervised recruitment on an employer for a single Application for Permanent Employment Certification or for all of the employer's future-filed Application(s) for Permanent Employment Certification for a period of up to 2 years if the CO determines the employer failed to fully comply with audit examination requirements. Once in supervised recruitment, the employer must demonstrate its compliance with the additional recruitment requirements specified by the CO, in addition to compliance with all other requirements of part 656, in order to receive a PERM labor certification.
Discretion
The proposed rule revises language in section 656.21(a) to more clearly state the CO's discretion to place an employer in supervised recruitment. Proposed section 656.21(a) replaces “[w]here the Certifying Officer determines it appropriate” with “[t]he CO may, at their discretion, require supervised recruitment.” Under both the current and proposed rule, the CO has discretion to place an employer in supervised recruitment; however, the proposed rule more clearly states this discretion. In addition, proposed section ( printed page 73963) 656.21(a) replaces “for the pending application or future applications pursuant to § 656.20(b),” which could be interpreted as requiring the pending application to undergo audit before supervised recruitment, with “on any Application for Permanent Employment Certification pending a final determination or for all of an employer's future Applications for Permanent Employment Certification for up to two years pursuant to § 656.20(f).” By more clearly providing the CO maximum discretion to induct an employer into supervised recruitment at any phase of processing an Application for Permanent Employment Certification, the Department provides employers with clearer notice regarding the potential for supervised recruitment and supports the CO's efforts to quickly and efficiently address trends and concerns regarding program integrity. Furthermore, the proposed revision in paragraph (a) adds the specific period during which the CO may order supervised recruitment for an employer's future filings to add clarity to proposed section 656.21(a). The period stated in proposed 656.21(a) is not new.
