Current section 656.20(f) specifies the potential period for which the CO may order supervised recruitment for an employer's future Applications for Permanent Employment Certification as up to two years. This proposal would provide important information relating to supervised recruitment in one place.
By clarifying the CO's discretion regarding supervised recruitment, the Department seeks to both streamline processing and safeguard U.S. workers by ensuring a robust test of the labor market.
Supervised Recruitment Notice
The Department proposes to repurpose section 656.21(b) to formalize the “Notice of Supervised Recruitment” procedure, with revisions. To increase the consistency of the audit and supervised recruitment procedures and improve clarity for employers subject to supervised recruitment procedures, the Department proposes to repurpose section 656.21(b) so that it codifies current practice, through which the CO issues a written notice to the employer, currently called “Notice of Supervised Recruitment,” with a copy to the employer's attorney or agent, if the employer has such a representative.
In proposed section 656.21(b)(1), the Department proposes new content outlining the type of information and instructions to be provided to the employer in the Notice of Supervised Recruitment. As proposed, the notice will specify the procedures the employer must follow to conduct supervised recruitment, including “the required method(s) and content for advertising the job opportunity to prospective U.S. applicants ( e.g., whether to have applicants respond directly to the NPC), timeframes for compliance with the supervised recruitment process, and state that supervised recruitment will be incorporated as bona fide conditions and terms of the Application(s) for Permanent Employment Certification.” To simplify proposed section 656.21 and acknowledge that advertising methods and contents are assessed by the CO on case-by-case basis, the Department does not propose to retain the list of required advertisement content in current section 656.21(b)(2) that currently applies to newspaper advertisements. Further, this approach provides the CO with flexibility to customize instructions for each job opportunity to the employer in the Notice of Supervised Recruitment, including advertisement content, to ensure the supervised recruitment efforts are appropriately guided to solicit referrals from the broadest pool of potentially qualified and available U.S. workers. Among the greater flexibilities afforded the CO under the proposed rule to customize recruitment and advertising content, as appropriate, during supervised recruitment, proposed section 656.21(b) would permit—but not require—the CO to require the employer's advertisements to direct applicants to send resumes or applications for the job opportunity to the NPC, rather than the employer.[120] The current rule requires applicants to apply only to the CO and the CO to refer those applicants to the employer. With this proposed change, the Department intends to balance the competing goals of supervising labor market testing and using advertisements to test U.S. worker availability for PERM job opportunities that blend in with non-PERM advertisements.
In addition, the Department proposes to add new content in proposed section 656.21(b)(2) that would implement the same consequences for failure to provide required documents by the due date(s) as currently applies during audit examination. While in audit examination processing, if the employer fails to provide required documentation by the due date, the CO will deny the application, with the denial ineligible for review by BALCA under section 656.26. See current section 656.20(a)(3) and proposed section 656.20(b)(3). If adopted in the final rule, proposed section 656.21(b)(2) would implement this consequence in Supervised Recruitment processing and would require that the Notice of Supervised Recruitment include notification of the consequence.
Requirements
To accommodate the new content in paragraph (b), which discusses the Notice of Supervised Recruitment, current section 656.21(b) has been redesignated in the proposed rule as section 656.21(c), with additional substantive changes. Currently, supervised recruitment requires placement of print advertisements in all cases, as well as any other measures the CO may add. See current sections 656.21(b) and (d). In addition, the current rule specifies advertisement content, requires the employer to submit a draft advertisement for CO review within 30 calendar days of the supervised recruitment notice date, and requires communication between the CO and employer regarding the timing of advertisements in paragraphs (b) and (c).
Consistent with Department's proposed modernization of pre-filing recruitment requirements in this rule, proposed section 656.21(c) eliminates print advertisements as mandatory for all supervised recruitment, while retaining flexibility for the CO to order appropriate recruitment activity under the CO's guidance, which could include print advertisements, when appropriate. Because supervised recruitment may require the employer to repeat a pre-filing recruitment activity under supervision—for example, reposting an advertisement on the employer's website for a longer period of time or changing the location of the posting on the website, or the CO can require an additional advertisement on a website other than the employer's or the SWA's during supervised recruitment—the Department proposes language in paragraph (c) to clarify that the supervised recruitment process is in addition to the pre-filing recruitment requirements in section 656.17 or 656.18.
Proposed paragraph (c) further discusses the various potential additional requirements that could constitute supervised recruitment; however, the CO would not be limited to the list in proposed paragraph (c). The Department intends for the CO to use discretion to determine the appropriate supervised recruitment steps to ensure the proper recruitment of U.S. workers. ( printed page 73964)
Paragraph (c) continues to allow the CO to control the supervised recruitment process, for example, by: requiring submission of a draft advertisement for review and approval prior to placement; identifying source(s) and specifying timeframe(s) for advertisements, including an extended period for advertisements or job orders; and requiring the employer to notify the CO when advertisement(s) are placed. In addition, the proposal continues to permit the CO to require the employer to retain proof of publication for submission to the CO, upon request, and to require the employer to submit a recruitment report to the CO after completing supervised recruitment.
As discussed above, the Department proposes to eliminate advertisement content requirements in regulatory text. Instead, under this proposed rule, the CO will specify required advertisement content in the Notice of Supervised Recruitment to maintain flexibility to require the content necessary to apprise U.S. workers of the job opportunity. Moreover, the CO may require the employer to submit a draft advertisement so that the CO can review and approve the content before placement.
Similarly, the Department proposes to eliminate the separate recruitment report content provision currently in section 656.21(e). Rather, proposed 656.21(c)(6), leverages the recruitment report provision at proposed section 656.17(f). Applying the proposed recruitment report content in section 656.17(f) to recruitment reports submitted in supervised recruitment would simplify part 656 and bring consistency to recruitment report content requirements.
The Department also proposes to add clarifying language in proposed section 656.21(c)(7), as discussed in connection with proposed section 656.10(a)(2), to put employers subject to supervised recruitment on notice that the CO may require them to update the NOF they provided or posted before filing the Application for Permanent Employment Certification with revised content, consistent with content ordered for supervised recruitment advertisements.
Supplemental Information Request
The Department proposes to relocate content from current section 656.21(f) to proposed section 656.21(d), with revisions, to address requests for documentation or information during supervised recruitment. Proposed section 656.21(d) has been revised for consistency with current practice in supervised recruitment and the similar provision for audit examination in proposed section 656.20(d). Proposed section 656.21(d) specifies that the CO may issue one or more requests to the employer for supplemental information and/or documentation during the supervised recruitment process. The CO will continue to issue requests for supplemental information to the employer in writing and will specify the date(s), of up to 30 calendar days, by which information is due to the CO. The employer must respond to the Notice of Supervised Recruitment and any request(s) from the CO during supervised recruitment, by the date(s) specified, in the manner specified, and with the documentation and information requested of the employer.
Correspondence During Supervised Recruitment
Consistent with proposals for correspondence during processing, the Department proposes in section 656.21(e) to require the CO to send notices and requests to the employer with a copy to the employer's attorney or agent, if the employer has such a representative, and the employer to respond electronically, unless the employer was permitted to file by mail as set forth in § 656.10(c). For employers permitted to file by mail, the CO would be required to send any notice or request and the employer to respond via traditional methods to assure next day delivery. Consistent with other proposals in this rule and current practice in the H-2A program, the proposed rule would require employer responses during supervised recruitment to be sent by the due date specified by the CO, or by the next business day if the due date falls on a Sunday or Federal holiday. This proposal improves transparency about the CO's correspondence during supervised recruitment, in addition to furthering the Department's goals to modernize and streamline processes to support processing efficiency.
Failure To Comply With Supervised Recruitment
The Department proposes revising section 656.21(f) to clarify the consequences of failure to comply with the supervised recruitment process or requirements. Current section 656.21(f) simply states that the CO will deny the application if the employer fails to “supply the CO with the required documentation or information within 30 days of the date of the request.” However, the CO also must deny certification if the employer submits the required documentation or information, but the substance of that documentation or information does not support certification. For example, the CO will deny the application if the employer submitted its recruitment report, but the recruitment report does not contain required content, such as lawful, job-related reasons for rejecting the U.S. workers who applied. The additional recruitment during supervised recruitment, when ordered, is part of the CO's assessment of U.S. worker availability and prevention of adverse impact to U.S. workers similarly employed. An employer's failure to perform the additional recruitment required, in compliance with the CO's instructions, precludes certification. Proposed section 656.21(f) clarifies that failure to comply with any requirements ordered by the CO during supervised recruitment—including, but not limited to, due dates—will result in denial.
In addition, the Department proposes revising section 656.21(f) to add that debarment of the employer and/or its attorney or agent is a possible consequence of noncompliance with supervised recruitment orders. This is not a new possible consequence. Both the current rule and proposed rule include a pattern or practice of failure to comply in the supervised recruitment process as a basis for debarment in section 656.31(f). Adding the proposed language in section 656.21(f) is consistent with changes proposed in section 656.20 (Audit) and improves notice of the consequences of noncompliance, which include taking appropriate measures to ensure the integrity of the PERM labor certification program under section 656.31(f).
Potential Referrals
The Department proposes to redesignate the provision for the one-time extension request option from current section 656.21(g) to proposed section 656.21(h) and repurpose section 656.21(g) to notify the public that the CO will provide supervised recruitment findings or underlying documentation to any enforcement agency, including the DOL-OIG, DHS, DOS, EEOC, and DOJ. Additionally, the CO will refer findings that an employer, attorney, or agent, “discouraged an eligible U.S. worker from applying, or failed to hire, discharged or otherwise discriminated against an eligible U.S. worker” to DOJ, Civil Rights Division, Immigrant and Employee Rights Section and/or the U.S. Equal Employment Opportunity Commission. The text in proposed section 656.21(g) aligns with a similar provision in proposed section 656.20 (Audit). These proposed provisions serve as an additional means of deterring fraud and suspicious activity and notify the public of the ( printed page 73965) Department's intention to cooperate with fellow agencies to the maximum extent possible to protect the integrity of the PERM labor certification program.
One-Time Extension Request
The Department proposes to relocate current section 656.21(g) to proposed section 656.21(h), with revisions for clarity and consistency with a similar provision in proposed section 656.20(c) for audit examination. The proposed rule continues to permit an employer to request one extension to any one response deadline during the supervised recruitment process. Consistent with current practice, where an employer requests an extension, it must do so in writing, before the due date of the supervised recruitment response, and the CO at his or her discretion, may grant one extension of up to 30 additional calendar days for the employer to respond. Where granted, the CO will issue a written notice to the employer that specifies the new date by which the employer must respond with the requested documentation. This proposal clarifies in paragraph (h) that this is a “one-time extension” and the extension is at the CO's discretion. Only one extension request during the supervised recruitment process will be approved by the CO.
13. Proposed 20 CFR 656.24, Labor Certification Determinations
Consistent with the Department's proposals throughout this rulemaking to reorganize part 656 and simplify language to clarify program requirements and procedures, the Department proposes revisions to the section addressing PERM labor certification determinations at section 656.24. The proposed changes in this section are largely organizational and for conformity with the overall proposed rule. For example, proposed section 656.24 contains paragraph headings to more clearly identify topics and simplifies procedural matters related to issuing final determinations to certify or deny an Application for Permanent Employment Certification. The Department also proposes relocating the content of current section 656.24(f), which discusses supervised recruitment, to proposed section 656.21(f) and the content of current section 656.24(g), which discusses reconsideration, to proposed section 656.24(d)(2). Below, the Department summarizes proposed section 656.24 and highlights proposed changes.
The Department retains its explanation of the OFLC Administrator's role and delegation of authority to COs to issue determinations for Applications for Permanent Employment Certification in proposed section 656.24(a) (Determinations), with conforming changes. The Department is proposing to revise the current language in section 656.24(a), to clarify that the OFLC Administrator's authority to review and issue determinations for Applications for Permanent Employment Certification is not limited to “special or unique problems.” The proposed language clarifies that authority resides with the OFLC Administrator, who may, in their discretion, delegate that authority to COs. The proposed provision states that, “the OFLC Administrator has sole discretion to certify, deny, or suspend processing of Applications for Permanent Employment Certification under 656.31(b) or for other appropriate reasons or to delegate authority to the CO to do so.” See proposed section 656.24(a). The Department emphasizes that the proposed language is not intended to change current practice. While the OFLC Administrator has the authority to review applications and issue final determinations, the COs at NPCs will continue to issue final determinations in most cases. The Department is revising this provision to add clarity, as well as align this language with several other provisions which discuss authority and delegation.
Proposed section 656.24(b) continues to address the three criteria for certification of an Application for Permanent Employment Certification. In addition to conforming modifications, the Department proposes revisions intended to increase clarity and consistency within part 656 and across the Department's regulations. With only minor clarifying changes, proposed subparagraph (b)(1) continues to require the employer to have met all the requirements of part 656 as a condition of certification. Proposed subparagraphs (b)(2) and (b)(3) continue to address the statutorily required determinations—that there are insufficient U.S. workers able, willing, qualified, and available to fill the employer's job opportunity and that the employment of the alien will not have an adverse effect upon the wages and working conditions of U.S. workers similarly employed—with revisions.
In proposed subparagraph (b)(2), the Department adds language regarding the CO's assessment of U.S. worker availability using information gathered from the U.S. labor market test, as provided in the employer's recruitment report. As modified, proposed subparagraph (b)(2) is consistent with similar provisions in the H-2A and H-2B programs at sections 20 CFR 655.51(b) and 20 CFR 655.161(b), respectively. It is the employer's responsibility to ensure that it submits a recruitment report that clearly articulates lawful, job-related reason(s) for not hiring any U.S. worker who applies for the job opportunity that is the subject of the Application for Permanent Employment Certification. Proposed section 656.24(b)(2) would better notify employers how the CO will assess U.S. worker availability in the event the employer's recruitment report fails to clearly state reason(s) that are lawful, job-related reasons for not hiring a U.S. worker. The CO will “count as able and qualified” any U.S. worker who was referred or applied for the job opportunity and for whom the employer does not identify a lawful, job-related reason for rejection in the recruitment report. This language supports efficient and faster processing, as the CO can reach a determination based on the information stated in the recruitment report, without additional requests and delays.
For most Applications for Permanent Employment Certification, current section 656.24(b)(2)(i) governs the CO's assessment of an employer's recruitment results. The Department proposes no substantive change to the standard of review for recruitment reports subject to assessment under section 656.24(b)(2)(i). Consistent with current practice, a U.S. worker is considered able and qualified for the job opportunity if the worker, by education, training, experience, or a combination thereof, is able to perform the duties involved in the normally accepted manner, as customarily performed by other U.S. workers similarly employed, or can acquire the skills necessary to perform the duties involved during a reasonable period of on-the-job training. This assessment may be referred to as a “minimally qualified” assessment. The Department proposes to retain this subparagraph with one minor revision to identify the CO as the decisionmaker.
For Applications for Permanent Employment Certification involving a college or university teacher job opportunity, the CO currently applies the alternative assessment of U.S. worker availability found at current section 656.24(b)(2)(ii), which is a limited exception to the “minimally qualified” assessment. This alternative assessment reflects Congress' October 20, 1976 amendments to the INA at section 212(a)(5)(A)(ii), which implemented an “equally qualified” assessment for these job ( printed page 73966) opportunities.[121] Under this proposed rule, the CO would continue to apply this alternative assessment to count as available, any U.S. worker who applies or is referred to a college or university teacher job opportunity and who is “at least as qualified” ( i.e., “equally qualified”) as the alien selected.
The Department proposes to expand current section 656.24(b)(2)(ii) to include job opportunities involving aliens who have exceptional ability in the sciences or the arts. This proposal further implements Congress' October 20, 1976 amendments to the INA at section 212(a)(5)(A)(ii), which also excepted aliens who have exceptional ability in the sciences and arts from the generally applicable “minimally qualified” assessment of U.S. worker availability and, instead, required application of the “equally qualified” assessment.[122] Although Congress extended the “equally qualified” assessment standard to PERM labor certifications for aliens who have exceptional ability in the sciences or the arts at the same time as college and university teachers, it was not necessary to address it in part 656 as long as job opportunities involving aliens who have exceptional ability in the sciences or the arts were included in Schedule A, Group II, and exempt from engaging in a labor market test.[123] With the proposed elimination of Schedule A, the Department expects to receive Applications for Permanent Employment Certification from employers seeking to permanently employ aliens who have exceptional ability in the sciences or the arts and will be required to assess U.S. worker availability using the statutory standard at INA section 212(a)(5)(A)(ii).[124] Such employers would be required to engage in the pre-filing recruitment activities proposed in section 656.17(a), and submit a recruitment report to OFLC when filing the Application for Permanent Employment Certification, as provided in proposed section 656.10(a)(1). Accordingly, proposed section 656.24(b)(2)(ii) clarifies that any U.S. worker who is “at least as qualified” ( i.e., “equally qualified”) as the alien who has exceptional ability in the sciences or the arts would be considered available.
The Department proposes minor conforming changes in proposed subparagraph (b)(3). In addition, the Department proposes a modification to state that, in reaching the determination regarding adverse effect, the CO will consider information about working conditions that are “normal to the occupation and industry,” rather than “prevailing . . . in the occupation.” The Department notes that current subparagraph (b)(3) contains the only instance in part 656 where the current rule suggests a “prevailing” standard for assessing a job opportunity or occupation. The proposal to use a “normal” standard for the occupation and industry, here, is consistent with the CO's assessment of the information the employer provides about the job opportunity and related occupation on the Application for Permanent Employment Certification and during processing.[125] In addition, using a “normal” standard better aligns with publicly available resources and information, which facilitates efficient review of Applications for Permanent Employment Certification.
Finally, the Department proposes to reorganize the content of current sections 656.24(c), (d), and (e) to focus final determination issuance procedures in proposed sections 656.24(c) (Approved certification) and 656.24(d) (Denied Certification). Proposed sections 656.24(c) and 656.24(d) reflect changes to conform with electronic filing and communication proposals throughout this rule and OFLC's current practice of electronically sending the certified Application for Permanent Employment Certification directly to USCIS upon approval. For employers permitted to file by mail under proposed section 656.10(c), the CO would continue to send certifications by mail and denials by means normally assuring next-day delivery, to ensure full access to reconsideration and administrative review options. In addition, paragraph (d) identifies “Notice of Denial” as the name of the notice issued when the CO denies certification, a proposal to add clarity, as discussed in section 656.26 below.
To add clarity for employers, the Department further proposes to revise the content relocated from current 656.24(e) to proposed section 656.24(d) for Notice of Denial content. Currently, section 656.24(g) provides the option for an employer to request reconsideration by the CO after receiving a denial, but this option is not listed in the denial notice content requirements. To ensure employers who receive a Notice of Denial are apprised of their full set of options after denial, proposed subparagraphs (d)(2) and (d)(4) ensure that the employer is informed that it has three options—it may request reconsideration by the CO under proposed 656.26(a), review by BALCA under proposed 656.26(b), or may not appeal and, instead, file a new Application for Permanent Employment Certification. Proposed subparagraph (d)(3) continues to apprise employers that if they wish to request reconsideration or review of the denial, they must do so within 30 calendar days of the denial date; after 30 calendar days have passed, the window to appeal the denial closes and the denial becomes final. These are not new options, but in the proposed rule they are more clearly stated. Further, the proposed rule would require these options to be explained in the Notice of Denial.
14. Proposed 20 CFR 656.26, Review of Denied Permanent Labor Certifications
Consistent with the Department's general proposal to reorganize part 656 to follow the ordinate flow of processing an Application for Permanent Employment Certification, the Department proposes to rename section 656.26's heading to read “Review of denied permanent labor certification” (currently “Board of Alien Labor Certification review of denials of labor certification”) and transfer the language currently contained in section 656.24(g), which addresses requests for reconsideration by the CO of an application's denial, under the proposed revised section 656.26(a). With this proposed change, section 656.26 would cover the options available to an employer who receives an application denial—requests for reconsideration by the CO and requests for review by BALCA. In addition, the Department proposes to redesignate current sections 656.26(a) through (c) as proposed sections 656.26(b) through (d) and to revise the existing regulatory language in section 656.26 to codify existing practices and provide clarity to employers, attorneys, agents, and BALCA regarding the reconsideration and appellate processes.
Requests for CO Reconsideration vs. Requests for BALCA Review
Currently, when the CO denies an Application for Permanent Employment ( printed page 73967) Certification, the CO must include certain language in the notice to the employer. Current section 656.24(e) requires the CO to “quote the request for review procedures at § 656.26(a) and (b).” The request for review procedures articulates how an employer may request review of the denial by BALCA. In the 2002 NPRM, Labor Certification for the Permanent Employment of Aliens in the United States; Implementation of New System (“2002 NPRM”),[126] the Department noted that the regulations at that time were silent on the possibility of motions for reconsideration by the CO after a final determination and that while, historically, COs “sometimes honored such motions” for reconsideration, they “generally treated them as requests for review and transmitted the matter to the” BALCA.[127] The 2002 NPRM proposed to preserve the ability for employers to submit motions for reconsideration, and the Department then amended the regulations to “specifically provide that while motions for reconsideration before the [CO] may be filed, [the CO] may, in his/her complete discretion, choose to treat the motion as a request for review.” [128] Thus, the Department adopted two avenues of review of a final determination in the current rule—either the employer requests review by BALCA, or it requests reconsideration by the CO.[129]
In practice, most employers, after an initial denial, choose to submit a request for reconsideration by the CO rather than submitting a request for review by BALCA. It became the general practice of OFLC to deem requests that did not specifically ask for BALCA review to be requests for reconsideration. After the denial of the request for reconsideration, the CO would automatically transmit the Application for Permanent Employment Certification to BALCA for review. This was done regardless of whether the employer requested review by BALCA after the request for reconsideration was denied. Those cases were then docketed at BALCA. This practice continued for many years. As a result, BALCA was inundated with cases—at one point reaching over 3,760 docketed cases in a fiscal year.[130]
There were a number of issues with this process as it evolved. First, many employers did not want their cases to be sent to BALCA for review after their requests for reconsideration were denied. OFLC automatically sending the applications for review by BALCA meant that not only was the case docketing an extra administrative burden to BALCA and to the CO, who created the administrative file for review, but it was also a burden to the employer who then had to wait for the case to be docketed only to then file a motion for it to be dismissed. Second, this waiting period could jeopardize an employer's attempts to file future PERM labor certification applications because, pursuant to current section 656.24(e)(6), an employer may not file a “new application in the same occupation for the same [alien] . . . while a request for review is pending with the [BALCA].” If an employer intended to file a new PERM labor certification application, it would again have to wait for the matter to be docketed, then file a dismissal and wait for the order to be issued.
For example, in Lindsborg Community Hospital Association, on April 10, 2012, the CO denied a request for reconsideration and forwarded the case to BALCA.[131] The employer attempted to withdraw the case on May 7, 2012, and the CO—because the case had already been sent to BALCA—sent the letter to BALCA as well.[132] Due to a docketing error of some kind, BALCA had not received the employer's withdrawal request as of September 17, 2012.[133] The employer explained that the pending appeal meant that it could not file a new Application for Permanent Employment Certification “and that this may have implications for the timing of the new application and the supporting prevailing wage determination.” [134] Had the employer been required to specifically request an appeal in the first instance, this lengthy delay between the denial on reconsideration (Apr. 10, 2012) and eventual dismissal from BALCA (Sept. 19, 2012) would have been eliminated. Also, BALCA noted in a footnote that the employer's motion for reconsideration had “said nothing about requesting BALCA Review” and that the regulation “does not state that the CO must automatically forward an Appeal File to BALCA if the CO denies reconsideration. Thus, the fact that the matter was treated as an appeal may not have been procedurally correct.” [135] The high number of appeals was extremely burdensome to BALCA and the CO—an unnecessary burden, especially in light of the fact that employers were not actively requesting such appeals. In addition, the Department's approach resulted in delays that could cause harm to employers seeking to file for permanent labor certifications.
The delays and administrative burdens on COs and BALCA led to an internal processing change that has been in effect since October 27, 2014.[136] As before, an employer may submit either a request for review by BALCA or a request for reconsideration by the CO, and the CO may still deem the request for BALCA review as a request for reconsideration.[137] Now, after the CO has determined to deny the request for reconsideration, the CO no longer automatically transmits that Application for Permanent Employment Certification to BALCA for review. Instead, the CO includes language in the reconsideration decision notice affirming the earlier denial that explains to the employer that the employer has the ability to request review by BALCA and has 30 calendar days from the date of the reconsideration decision notice to do so. This process significantly cut down the administrative burdens for the CO, BALCA, and employers because now only those decisions that employers wish to pursue are sent to BALCA. The table below shows the difference between the years when the CO was automatically sending cases to BALCA for review without employer's request as compared to when employers had to specifically request BALCA review after the CO denied request for reconsideration. The decrease in the number of cases docketed was significantly lower when employers had to request for BALCA review.
( printed page 73968)
Fiscal years | Cases CO automatically referred to BALCA | Fiscal years | Cases employers requested BALCA review
2011 | 2,989 | 2014 | 1,729
2012 | 3,766 | 2015 | 757
2013 | 3,471 | 2016 | 852
2017 | 326
Total Number of Cases Docketed | 10,226 | | 3,664
Source: Office of Administrative Law Judges.
It is likely that there are additional reasons for the declining docketed appeals; however, the data show a pattern of significant decrease of cases docketed when the CO no longer automatically sent cases to BALCA after denial on reconsideration. Currently, the CO only sends cases for BALCA review if an employer actively submits the request. The proposed section 656.26 would codify this current practice.
Although this processing change is not contradictory to the regulations in any way, it was not fully captured by the text of the current regulations. Notably, the older process by which the CO would transmit Applications for Permanent Employment Certification to BALCA immediately after denying the request for reconsideration was also not fully captured by the plain text of the current regulations. Regardless of which process would ultimately have been the chosen and lasting path by the Department, a change in regulatory text is needed to codify the current practice to provide clarity and transparency regarding this part of the permanent labor certification process.
Also, the fact that the processes are not actually encompassed in the regulations leads to procedural postures that are difficult to navigate. For example, if an employer requested reconsideration and the denial was affirmed, the employer was told that they could request review by BALCA within 30 calendar days; however as noted, there is no regulatory provision granting 30 calendar days to further appeal to BALCA—which itself is troublesome—but also, if the employer failed to appeal within that 30 calendar day timeframe, there was no regulatory provision to which either the CO or ALJs could cite that would allow for the dismissal or closure of an untimely filing.
With this NPRM, the Department proposes to amend sections 656.26 and 656.27 of the current regulations to codify the current practice that has been in place for over a decade and to articulate the full appellate process and provide transparency and clarity on program requirements and procedures of part 656.
Final Determinations
Currently, the term “Final Determination” is used for the initial denial of the PERM labor certification as well as for the notice sent after reconsideration is denied. This fact leads to, in many cases, having two “Final Determinations,” which is confusing to the regulated community. The Department proposes to call the notice after a PERM labor certification is denied the “Notice of Denial,” and call the notice issued after a request for reconsideration is denied a NORD. This proposed change in terminology clarifies the meaning of each of the CO's decisions and helps to distinguish which document the CO, an agent/attorney, employer, or even BALCA may be referring.
Section 656.26(a), Request for Reconsideration by the CO
In addition to proposing to relocate current section 656.24(g) to the proposed new section 656.26(a), the Department is seeking to change the reconsideration language in the provision at section 656.26(a). Most significantly, the Department proposes to include a subparagraph (5) in the proposed new section 656.26(a). Proposed subparagraph (a)(5) would explain that if reconsideration is requested, the CO will reconsider the application's denial, and then if the CO determines that the request for reconsideration should be denied, the CO will issue a NORD informing the employer of the determination. Proposed subparagraphs (a)(5)(i) through (vi) outline required NORD content. The proposed language states the notice will: state the reasons for the denial; quote the procedures for review by BALCA; advise that an employer has 30 calendar days from the date of the reconsideration determination to request review or the denial will become the final determination of the Secretary and the employer will have failed to exhaust administrative remedies; and advise that an employer may file a new application after the reconsideration is denied if a request for review is not filed with the Board, but otherwise a new application in the same occupation for the same alien cannot be pending while a request for review is pending with BALCA. The intent of this proposed subsection is to clarify, via both the regulatory text and the Notice of Reconsideration Denial, that an employer may, after the reconsideration is denied, make a formal appeal to BALCA within 30 calendar days, and to articulate the consequences for not doing so. As stated above, this proposed rule codifies this current practice and provides transparency and clarity in part 656.
The Department also proposes edits to the existing reconsideration regulatory language that would be relocated and contained in the new proposed section 656.26's paragraphs (a)(1) through (4). The new proposed paragraph (a)(1) states clearly that the employer may request reconsideration by the CO after the Notice of Denial is issued. The Department proposes to remove “For applications submitted after July 16, 2007” that would be included in proposed paragraph (a)(2), as the phrase is no longer necessary. Proposed paragraph (a)(4) asserts that an employer “must clearly state” with its request whether it is seeking review by BALCA or is making a request for reconsideration by the CO, and, if it does not do so, the CO will construe the request as a request for reconsideration by the CO.
In proposed paragraph (a)(4) the Department is proposing to remove the discretion for the CO to determine whether to treat the request as a reconsideration request or not and makes it very clear to employers that if they themselves are not clear as to their desired review process their request will be construed, by default, as requests for reconsideration. The Department believes its proposal to construe unclear requests as requests for reconsideration by the CO is largely more beneficial to the employer. Under this approach, the CO can review additional information submitted with the request for reconsideration (assuming it meets the documentary requirement of section ( printed page 73969) 656.26's paragraphs (a)(2)(i) and (ii)). A case sent straight to BALCA—a procedure that does not allow for the introduction of new evidence on appeal—must be persuasive using a more limited pool of information. In fact, BALCA has objected to the CO's decision in previous cases to send to BALCA “requests” that were not entirely clear, or consistent internally, as to what was being requested—reconsideration and/or review—precisely because doing so precluded the introduction of new evidence.[138]
Finally, an employer may also receive a faster answer if the CO is given the opportunity to review a denial on reconsideration rather than sending the denial to BALCA and waiting for a decision and, potentially, an order remanding the case. Should an employer wish to move straight to BALCA review, it must clearly state as such.
Section 656.26(b), Request for Review by BALCA
In light of the proposed explicit incorporation of the “request for reconsideration” into the regulations, corresponding edits would also be needed to section 656.26, including redesignating existing paragraph (a) to (b), (b) to (c), and (c) to (d) following the addition of the reconsideration provision at paragraph (a). In redesignated paragraph (b)(1), the Department proposes new regulatory text to explain that a request for review by BALCA may be filed if a request for reconsideration is denied. The Department proposes including cross citations to other parts of the regulations for clarity and symmetry. As such, this section now proposes that BALCA may review a PERM labor certification denial pursuant to proposed section 656.24(d), a request for reconsideration denial under proposed new section 656.26(a), as well as a PERM labor certification revocation, or debarment under section 656.32 or section 656.31(f).
The Department proposes edits to the language that would be contained in redesignated paragraph (b)(2) to incorporate the provision for “denial on reconsideration” and again provide a 30-calendar-day timeline to employers for filing appeals to BALCA for denials on reconsideration and the way they should do so. Paragraph (b)(2)(iv) still refers to a “final determination,” but this reference is not a defined term and instead refers to the last or final determination of the reviewing official before the appeal.
The Department is also proposing non-substantive edits to subparagraph (b)(4) to clarify that with respect to the review of a PERM labor certification denial or a denial of a reconsideration motion, only items that were before the CO when the CO made the decision may be considered, even if other documents are in the physical appeal file sent to BALCA. Because the CO will include the appeal request in the administrative file transmitted to BALCA, the file will contain paperwork submitted on appeal. However, pursuant to the regulations, BALCA is not permitted to consider this additional submitted evidence.
The Department also proposes amending the regulations at (c)(2) and (3) to remove the physical address of BALCA from the regulatory text primarily because that physical address is no longer accurate—but also because as the Department streamlines and modernizes its processes, it is best to leave out specific physical addresses from regulatory texts as those might change from time to time and the Department is able to provide easier and faster updates to the public about the physical addresses or locations, as well as general information regarding its offices, services and procedures, electronically, via its website or when requested by the methods it specifies for requesting information. Corresponding edits were also made to (d)(2) and (3) removing the physical address from the text. Finally, it should be noted that electronic submission to BALCA, as well as to the Associate Solicitor for Employment and Training Legal Services is highly encouraged.
15. Proposed 20 CFR 656.27 Consideration by and Decisions of BALCA
The Department proposes to revise existing section 656.27 by adding new provisions, repurposing and redesignating paragraphs, and changing regulatory language for clarity and conformity with other proposed revisions introduced through this proposed rule. The Department proposes to repurpose section 656.27(c) to add a new standard of review provision. This proposal would result in the redesignation of current paragraphs (c), (d), and (e) as proposed paragraphs (d), (e), and (f). Similarly, the Department proposes adding a new provision to provide BALCA with the authority to remand cases to the CO when the merits of the Application for Permanent Employment Certification have not yet been fully assessed, resulting in the redesignation of current paragraph (c)(3) as proposed paragraph (d)(4). Also, in proposed section 656.27(d) (current section 656.27(c)), the Department proposes to add review of denials on reconsideration to the list of adverse decisions BALCA may review and to clarify that such review will be limited to an on-the-record review. In proposed paragraphs (e) and (f) (current paragraphs (d) and (e)), the Department proposes to codify its existing practice of including the employer's attorney or agent, if applicable, in communication during appeal. Throughout proposed section 656.27, the Department also proposes minor conforming changes for consistency throughout the proposed rule. In addition, the Department is proposing to remove language from current section 656.27(e)(1) that requires “good cause” to be shown for hearing rescheduling requests. This proposed change is intended to provide BALCA with more control and flexibility in hearing scheduling and requests for rescheduling.
Standard of Review
In proposed paragraph (c), the Department seeks to incorporate the arbitrary and capricious standard of review into requests for review of denials of PERM labor certifications under proposed section 656.24(d), denials of requests for reconsideration under proposed section 656.26(a), or affirmations of PWDs under section 656.41. As the regulation is currently silent on the standard of review, this proposed change clarifies and ensures BALCA is conducting review under part 656 in a consistent manner. Other employment-based visa programs administered by OFLC require use of the arbitrary and capricious standard of review on appeal to BALCA.[139] This change further aligns the PERM program ( printed page 73970) with the other employment-based visa programs administered by OFLC.
Presently, BALCA explains that it uses a form of de novo review, “limited in scope by 20 CFR 656.27(c)” or in other words, as limited by the record.[140] However, BALCA has also said that “[t]he Board will not decide an appeal on grounds for denial not raised while the case was before the CO.” [141] It also stated that “our review of the preliminary decisions entrusted by regulation to the discretion of the CO is typically more limited, and we examine those decisions with an eye toward whether they are arbitrary, capricious, an abuse of discretion, or otherwise not in accordance with law.” [142]
The fact that BALCA seems to have combined different scopes of review—arbitrary and capricious for “preliminary decisions,” yet it conducts a modified “de novo” review that does not consider issues not already before the CO—demonstrates why clearly articulating one consistent standard of review in the regulations will help provide clarity to the regulated community, attorneys/agents and judges themselves. The Department proposes to adopt the standard of review “arbitrary and capricious, an abuse of discretion, or otherwise not in accordance with law” to make it consistent with the other employment-based visa programs. The Department does not believe that this will result in significant changes in adjudication because BALCA is limited in their review to the administrative record pursuant to current section 656.27(c) (proposed section 656.27(d)), and BALCA already does not decide an appeal based on new denial grounds or on new arguments not made before the CO.[143]
The Department proposes minor edits to section 656.27 paragraph (d) to account for the addition of review of denials on reconsideration by the CO as well as to note that on-the-record review is conducted on the basis of the record upon which the “CO's final determination” was made. Here “final determination” refers to the last determination by the reviewing official before it moves to BALCA—for example, the denial of the PERM labor certification, the denial of the reconsideration request, or the affirmation on the PWD.
Section 656.27(d)(2)-(3) Directing Certification and Remands
In the 2004 PERM Final Rule, the Department eliminated the possibility of remands from BALCA to the CO in the PERM program.[144] At that time, the Department believed that the cases that would have made it to BALCA would have been sufficiently developed such that remand would be unnecessary.[145] Furthermore, the Department, at the time, explained that the processing model for applications requesting PERM labor certification did “not contemplate the type of interchange between the employer and the [CO]” and “it is not apparent what the [CO] would do if a case were `remanded'.” [146]
Through years of actual administrative practice, however, the Department has discovered that there have been cases—though not frequent—with a record sufficient enough to determine that the CO was arbitrary and capricious in the denial, but still not enough to determine that certification is warranted. The purpose of the PERM labor certification program is to allow the Secretary to determine and certify that “there are not sufficient United States workers who are able, willing, qualified and available at the time of application for a visa and admission into the United States and at the place where the alien is to perform the work; . . . and the employment of the alien will not adversely affect the wage and working conditions of United States workers similarly employed.” [147] For certification to be issued in circumstances where the CO has not yet fully evaluated the merits of the case would violate the regulations and statute.[148]
A remand, rather than an order directing certification, in a situation where the CO has not fully reviewed an Application for Permanent Employment Certification and all supporting documentation would allow the CO to examine the merits of the application and to determine whether the application truly establishes that there are not sufficient U.S. workers able, willing, qualified and available for the position, and also that the alien's employment will not adversely affect the wages and working conditions of U.S. workers similarly employed.[149] BALCA has recognized this principle before and has actually remanded cases for further processing, even though remands are not currently provided for in the regulations. BALCA has stated, “[w]hile the Board generally eschews remands to allow further processing of an application, such remands are appropriate where an application was denied on preliminary grounds, and the merits of the application have not been addressed.” [150]
The Department agrees with BALCA and believes that remand may be the most appropriate way forward in certain cases; and therefore, is proposing to add a new provision, subparagraph (d)(3), to codify remands from BALCA for clarity and transparency purposes. The intent is for remands to be used sparingly and only in situations where, based on the facts and posture of the case, as well as the underlying reasons for denial, the CO had been unable to evaluate the underlying merits of the decision. If a case is sufficiently developed, then affirmance of the denial or reversal of the denial and an order directing certification would be appropriate. However, for example, if an employer failed to respond to an audit and did not submit appropriate documentation, a remand to allow for resubmission and further analysis for the CO would be inappropriate. In contrast, if the CO has listed all the legal reasons for denial after a thorough evaluation, but BALCA determines that the CO was arbitrary and capricious in their denial, certification rather than remand may be more appropriate as the CO already evaluated the merits of the application.
In addition, remand is an essential component of the Department's proposal in this NPRM regarding section 656.6 to better hold program violators accountable for their misconduct by preventing successors in interest to debarred persons or entities from participating in the program during the debarment period, without unnecessarily disrupting processing for proper filings. As discussed in proposed section 656.6, once the CO discovers an Application for Permanent Employment Certification has been filed by a successor in interest to a debarred person or entity, the CO would deny the application solely on that basis and without review. The Notice of Denial would offer the person or entity the option to appeal the CO's determination ( printed page 73971) of their successor in interest status under section 656.26. However, as the CO would not have reviewed the merits of the Application for Permanent Employment Certification or reached any determination apart from successor in interest status, if BALCA overturns the CO's determination regarding the successor in interest status, remand would be the appropriate procedural order to allow the application to move forward.
To that end, the Department is proposing to allow for remands in subparagraph (d)(3), and to correspondingly revise proposed subparagraph (d)(2) to make clear that a grant of certification may only be directed when the CO had the opportunity to review the merits of the application.
16. Proposed 20 CFR 656.30, Validity of Labor Certifications
The Department proposes revisions to current section 656.30 to further its reorganizational efforts of part 656, to remove language outside the Department's jurisdiction, and to clarify requirements concerning the granting of PERM labor certifications. Specifically, the Department proposes to eliminate the content of current paragraph (a)—“priority date” is a DHS, not DOL, topic—and, then, redesignate current paragraphs (b) and (c) as proposed paragraphs (a) and (b), with revisions discussed below. In addition, the Department proposes relocating current paragraph (d), regarding the standards for invalidating PERM labor certifications by DHS or DOS, to 656.32(c), with revisions discussed in the preamble for that section. Relocating current paragraph (d) and redesignating existing paragraph (c) as (b) in Section 656.30 would subsequently result in the removal of paragraphs (c) and (d) from Section 656.30.
In addition, the Department proposes to eliminate outdated language—including current section 656.30(e) in its entirety—to simplify the regulatory provision. This proposed change would result in the deletion of paragraph (e), which provides a no-longer-necessary process for requesting a duplicate paper permanent labor certification, as discussed below.
Finally, the Department proposes to change the title of proposed section 656.30 from “Validity of and invalidation of labor certifications” to “Validity of labor certifications,” consistent with the proposed content changes.
The current regulation at section 656.30(a) addresses filing dates in the context of how priority dates are established for Immigrant Petition purposes. However, the authority to designate priority dates for Immigrant Petitions resides with DHS, as USCIS is the agency that processes Immigrant Petitions, not DOL.[151] Although priority dates may be based on Application for Permanent Employment Certification filing dates, they are a concept outside the Department's statutory authority and bear no impact on the administration of the PERM program. DHS has long codified how priority dates are assigned in its regulations.[152] As part of its deregulatory initiatives, the Department is proposing to eliminate from its regulations language that is duplicative or outside its statutory or regulatory authorities. Removing language about priority dates from the Department's regulations will streamline PERM labor certification program regulations and ensure the scope of each department's authority remains focused on each agency's role, as determined by statute.[153] The Department proposes to retain content from current paragraph (a) about the Application for Permanent Employment Certification filing date, and seeks to relocate that content to proposed section 656.10(f), with clarifying revisions.
After relocating current paragraph (a) of section 656.30, the Department proposes to redesignate current paragraph (b) as proposed paragraph (a), with revisions. In turn, the proposed new paragraph (a) would continue to include the current 180-calendar day validity period for a PERM labor certification. Consistent with the current rule, this NPRM would require the employer to submit a PERM labor certification to USCIS with its Immigrant Petition ( i.e., Form I-140 petition) for the alien within 180 calendar days of the date the CO approves the Application for Permanent Employment Certification. If the employer does not submit an Immigrant Petition to USCIS using the approved Application for Permanent Employment Certification for the alien within 180 calendar days of approval, the PERM labor certification, as it is the case under the current rule, expires and cannot be used. However, the Department has become aware of the practice of PERM labor certifications being reused to subsequent Form I-140 petitions filed with USCIS for the same alien in violation of this prescribed validity period of 180 days, based on a mistaken interpretation of the provision. Therefore, the Department proposes a revision to the current regulatory language to explicitly prevent reuse of a PERM labor certification once it is used in support of the first or initial Form I-140 petition filing for the alien.
Specifically, some employers have been using their PERM labor certifications more than once, including filing after the 180-calendar day validity period has expired. For example, after receiving an Immigrant Petition denial from USCIS and unsuccessfully pursuing an appeal, some employers have filed, and USCIS has accepted, a subsequent Immigrant Petition using the same approved PERM labor certification. These employers assert to USCIS that current section 656.30(b) allows the approved PERM labor certification to remain valid indefinitely, so long as the employer first used it during the 180-calendar day validity period to file an Immigrant Petition with USCIS. Under this interpretation, a PERM labor certification could be several years out-of-date by the time an employer attempts to use it in support of a subsequent Immigrant Petition, which undermines the original PWD and labor market test.
The Department strongly disagrees with this interpretation of its PERM labor certification validity rule. The Department did not intend to permit use of the same PERM labor certification more than 180 calendar days after approval or in connection with subsequent Form I-140 petition filings for the alien. The Department asserts that the statutorily required determinations underlying a PERM labor certification are based on an assessment of the labor market for a certain job opportunity at a certain point in time and a new, updated assessment is necessary for each subsequent Immigrant Petition. The ability to use a PERM labor certification in perpetuity, so long as it was timely filed with a first Immigrant Petition is a fundamentally inconsistent interpretation of the Secretary's determinations, and contradicts statutory intent, and must be clarified to ensure the integrity of the PERM labor certification process. In addition, issuing this clarification and closing this perceived loophole will significantly reduce the continued costs of adjudicating and litigating subsequent filings with USCIS. Although the Department anticipates that this clarifying change would likely result in a marginal increase in filings in its PERM labor certification program, the Department has determined that this cost is necessary to serve its statutory ( printed page 73972) duty and preserve, above all, the integrity of the program.
Accordingly, the Department proposes to resolve this misinterpretation by revising the language that under the proposed reorganization of section 656.30 would be included in proposed new paragraph (a) to explicitly limit the validity of a PERM labor certification to the “initial Immigrant Petition.” With this proposed revision, the Department clarifies that a PERM labor certification may be used in connection with one Immigrant Petition, which must be submitted to USCIS within 180 days of the Application for Permanent Employment Certification' s approval. If USCIS adjudicates the initial Immigrant Petition and denies it—and after any appeals with USCIS have been exhausted and other due process rights afforded—the PERM labor certification is no longer valid for use when filing a new Immigrant Petition.
As explained in the beginning of this section, the Department also proposes redesignating this section 656.30's current paragraph (c) as paragraph (b). In addition to redesignating paragraph (c) as new paragraph (b), the Department proposes revising the regulatory language to clarify the scope of a PERM labor certification's validity that already exists, in large part, on the Office of Management and Budget (OMB)-approved form— Final Determination: Permanent Employment Certification Approval, and to conform with other proposals in this proposed rule. First, the Department proposes to remove language that has become outdated and language discussing Schedule A and sheepherder Applications for Permanent Employment Certification, which this NPRM proposes to eliminate. Second, the Department is proposing revised language to clarify that a PERM labor certification is valid only for the employer to whom the CO issued the approval, or its successors in interest. Proposed section 656.30(b) further clarifies that a PERM labor certification is only valid for the job opportunity, the alien, the place(s) of intended employment, and the employer named on the approved Application for Permanent Employment Certification, while recognizing that the employer may change its corporate form and should not lose access to the PERM labor certification due to a legitimate business transaction related to a change to its corporate structure.
Finally, the Department is proposing to eliminate current section 656.30(e), which outlines an outdated process for requesting duplicate PERM labor certifications from the OFLC CO. Through past information technology initiatives, the Department has eliminated the issuance of PERM labor certifications in paper form; now that PERM labor certifications are issued electronically through the OFLC FLAG system, employers, aliens, and their authorized attorneys or agents, as applicable, no longer need to request duplicate PERM labor certifications to submit to USCIS or DOS. Through a combination of existing data sharing agreements with USCIS and DOS and direct customer access to their authorized FLAG system accounts, OFLC makes all approved PERM labor certifications, as well as information related to any application, available real-time to USCIS and DOS. Once this change is implemented, the government agencies that collaborate with OFLC to administer the temporary and permanent labor certification programs will continue to have access to OFLC's FLAG system to obtain PERM labor certification records and verify data and information related to Applications for Permanent Employment Certification. Therefore, there is no longer a need to issue duplicate approved PERM labor certifications, when this process occurs electronically. Eliminating these paper government records serves to safeguard government resources, reduce burden on employers, and reduce costs to the public, the filing community, and the federal agencies processing these cases. This proposal also seeks to strengthen integrity measures by minimizing the number of original PERM labor certifications that are issued in paper-format. By eliminating this provision, the Department is not prohibiting the issuance of paper-based PERM labor certification records or duplicates to USCIS or other federal agencies, if and when necessary, but no longer needs to issue them directly to employers, aliens, or their authorized attorneys or agents, as applicable.
17. Proposed 20 CFR 656.31, Applications Involving Fraud, Willful Misrepresentation, or Violations of This Part
The Department is proposing to revise current section 656.31 to make non-substantive, clarifying changes that conform to other changes the Department is proposing in this NPRM to bring consistency regarding the terms widely used across part 656 regulations. In addition, the Department proposes to strengthen program integrity by expanding the scope under which debarment of the employer, attorney, or agent, or any combination thereof, will be applied.
First, the Department is proposing to shorten the section heading by deleting the term “Labor Certifications” and just simply having the heading read “Applications involving fraud, willful misrepresentation, or violations of this part.” This is a conforming, non-substantive change that would align with other changes the Department is making to ensure uniform use of terms throughout part 656 regulations.
The Department is further proposing clarifying changes throughout section 656.31 to uniformly indicate that the provisions of this section apply to “any,” meaning any single, Application for Permanent Employment Certification. This change would clarify previous instances where singular and plural references were used interchangeably. The Department is also proposing some clarifying changes such as the elimination of the term “National Processing Center” and shortening agency titles by including acronyms instead ( e.g., DHS, DOL, EEOC). The Department asserts that these changes will provide consistency and clarity across part 656 regulations, as more up-to-date and easier to identify terms will be used. In addition, references to other provisions that are relevant to the scope of this section would now be included, which could be beneficial in navigating part 656 regulations.
The Department is also seeking to codify OFLC's current practice regarding notification by proposing to update current sections 656.31(b)(1) and 656.31(c) to add language that would specify that a copy of any notice to suspend processing of applications for possible fraud or willful misrepresentation reasons, involving the employer who filed the Application for Permanent Employment Certification, would also be provided to the employer's agent or attorney, if appropriate. This proposed change would codify a practice that has allowed OFLC through the years to provide simultaneous notifications of this nature to not only employers, but also their agents and attorneys, when appropriate, for expediency, transparency and accountability purposes.
The Department is further codifying another existing OFLC practice as it proposes to revise section 656.31(d) to specify that it will resume processing of Application(s) for Permanent Employment Certification if the employer or their representative is acquitted of fraud or willful misrepresentation charges, or if criminal charges are withdrawn or otherwise did not result in a finding of fraud or willful misrepresentation. The existing regulatory language states that “the Certifying Officer shall decide each pending PERM labor certification ( printed page 73973) application related to that employer, attorney, or agent on the merits of the application.” Specifically, the proposed change would make it clear that the CO will resume processing the application at the stage where the suspension began, before reaching a determination on the merits of the application. The proposed change to section 656.31(d) reflects the Department's current processing procedures for handling cases involving potential fraud or willful misrepresentation in the PERM labor certification process.
In addition, the Department is proposing changes to section 656.31(f) to strengthen debarment. The Department proposes to expand the list of bases for debarment in current section 656.31(f)(1) to include two new proposed grounds for debarment. Proposed section 656.31(f)(1)(vii) would add a pattern or practice, by an attorney or agent, of preparing or submitting Applications for Permanent Employment Certification on behalf of unrelated employers that “contain materially the same job duties, requirements, or recruitment procedures where the CO has determined that those requirements were not the employers' actual minimum requirements or that the recruitment was not conducted in good faith” as a basis for debarment, while proposed section 656.31(f)(1)(viii) would establish “failure to pay the alien or a U.S. worker hired for the job opportunity the offered wage stated in the certified Application for Permanent Employment Certification” as a basis for debarment. The Department also proposes to add a new section 656.31(f)(3) that would more clearly establish the OFLC Administrator's independent authority to a finding of fraud or material misrepresentation and that such a finding does not preclude a related referral under proposed section 656.31(b).
Finally, the Department is proposing under section 656.31(f) that an employer, agent, or attorney, or any combination thereof, who is debarred by OFLC from the permanent labor certification program will also be debarred from all other foreign labor certification and labor condition application programs administered by the Department for the time period in the Notice of Debarment issued under section 656.31(f)(2), consistent with H-2B program regulations.[154] As the Department has noted in prior rulemaking in temporary labor certification programs, many employers, agents and attorneys participate in more than one foreign labor certification or labor condition application program administered by the Department.[155] However, under the current regulation, an employer, attorney, or agent that is debarred under the permanent labor certification program may continue to file applications and receive certifications or approvals under the Department's other foreign labor programs. By empowering OFLC to reject applications filed by, or on behalf of, a debarred employer, attorney, or agent, to refuse to issue certifications under the permanent labor certification program during the term of debarment, and to prevent filings or approvals during the debarment period in any of the Department's foreign labor certification or labor condition application programs, the Department would not only foster regulatory uniformity but would also enhance employer accountability and strengthen program integrity.
The Department invites comments about whether parties other than “employer, attorney, or agent” should be named in proposed sections 656.31(b) and (c). For example, an employer representative or point of contact may engage in fraud, willful misrepresentation, or violations while acting on the employer's behalf, but without the employer's knowledge. Thus, the Department seeks public input on whether “employer representative,” “employer point of contact,” or other parties be identified in sections 656.13(b) and (c) to ensure maximum integrity of the program from visa fraud or abuse.
The Department also is interested in receiving comments on additional reforms to the PERM debarment process including making the PERM debarment operative during the pendency of the administrative review process. The Department is also requesting comments on whether the maximum debarment period under 20 CFR 656.31(f) should be increased from 3 years to, for example, 5 years consistent with the debarment parameter at 20 CFR 655.73 for the H-2B program, or longer, such as to 10 years.
18. Proposed 20 CFR 656.32, Revocation and Invalidation of Permanent Labor Certifications
The Department is proposing various changes to section 656.32. One of the changes to proposed to section 656.32 involves adding a new paragraph (c) to contain content about “invalidation” that the Department proposes to relocate from current 656.30(d). This proposed change would also result in revision of the title of proposed section 656.32.
In proposed section 656.32(a), the Department is proposing to replace the phrase “Certifying Officer, in consultation with the Chief, Division of Foreign Labor Certification” with “OFLC Administrator” to establish the OFLC Administrator's role as adjudicator in revocation matters, while also updating terminology. In addition, the Department proposes to remove a sentence acknowledging DHS and DOS's authority to invalidate an approved PERM labor certification, as invalidation would now be addressed under the new paragraph (c) of this section and “revocation” and “invalidation” are different program integrity measures, performed by different agencies ( i.e., DOL vs DHS or DOS), based on each agency's own authority, as further discussed below.
Similarly, the Department proposes changes to section 656.32(b) to identify the OFLC Administrator as the adjudicator in revocation matters. In addition, the Department proposes to codify its current practice in section 656.32(b)(1) by stating that when the OFLC Administrator issues a Notice of Intent to Revoke (NOIR) in connection with an approved Application for Permanent Employment Certification, it will be sent to the employer and a copy, if appropriate, to the employer's agent or attorney. As a matter of longstanding practice, OFLC provides a copy of any notice during revocation proceedings to the employers' agents or attorneys, if applicable; codifying this practice merely captures a process that has been well established.
Although the Department is not modifying section 656.32(b)(2), the Department is reiterating that if the ( printed page 73974) employer or its representative does not submit rebuttal evidence within the 30-calendar-day period provided in the NOIR, the NOIR becomes the final decision of the Secretary. As a result, the Department would not issue a subsequent declaration or letter notifying the employer, if applicable, its attorney or agent, that the PERM labor certification has been, in fact, revoked; rather, the NOIR serves that purpose.
After relocating the content of current section 656.30(d) to proposed section 656.32(c), the Department proposes revisions for clarity and to simplify the regulation. The Department proposes revisions to remove a sentence about “revocation” from proposed paragraph (c). As noted above, “revocation” and “invalidation” are different program integrity measures performed by different agencies ( i.e., DOL vs DHS or DOS). DOL continues to have authority to revoke a PERM labor certification issued under part 656, if the certification was not justified, following the procedures outlined in proposed 656.32(b). DHS and DOS continue to have authority to invalidate a PERM labor certification, exercising their own authority to make “a determination[ ] in accordance with those agencies' procedures or by a court” of fraud or willful misrepresentation of a material fact involving the PERM labor certification application, as acknowledged in proposed section 656.32(c). As continued in proposed section 656.32(c), the Department's only role related to “invalidation” is to send written notice to DHS or DOS—and, as appropriate, a copy to the regional or national office of the DOL-OIG—if evidence of fraud or willful misrepresentation becomes known to the OFLC Administrator.
In proposed section 656.32, the Department is also proposing conforming changes to ensure uniform use of terms and language throughout part 656 regulations and to identify the OFLC Administrator as responsible for revocation and for sending notice to DHS or DOS, in the event of fraud or willful misrepresentation becoming known to the OFLC Administrator.
E. Discussion of Proposed Revisions to 20 CFR Part 656, Subpart D
The Department is proposing to amend subpart D by revising section 656.40; however, section 656.41 would remain unchanged.
1. Proposed 20 CFR 656.40, Determination of Prevailing Wage for Labor Certification Purposes
Employers who intend to file an Application for Permanent Employment Certification must obtain a PWD, as defined in proposed section 656.3, before filing that application, and before beginning to engage in pre-filing recruitment, unless the employer's application is subject to supervised recruitment under section 656.21. Current section 656.40 provides the standards and procedures for the PWD an employer must obtain from the Department before filing an Application for Permanent Employment Certification. However, at the time the current rule went into effect, PWDs were processed at State Workforce Agencies.[156] On January 1, 2010, PWD processing shifted to OFLC.[157] OFLC established internal procedures for processing Applications for Prevailing Wage Determination under section 656.40 and has updated its internal procedures in the years since, as appropriate, to support processing efficiency, allow for submission and consideration of additional information during processing, and adapt to an electronic processing environment. The Department has kept stakeholders apprised of these internal procedures through public guidance, including in Frequently Asked Questions posted on the Department's website.[158] The Department is taking this opportunity to codify its existing procedures to increase transparency and to ensure stakeholders are fully aware of these procedures and the requirements with which employers must comply to obtain the required PWD.[159] The Department also proposes to revise current section 656.40, consistent with its modernization and streamlining goals and to further improve efficiency in PWD processing.
Filing an Application for Prevailing Wage Determination
The Department proposes to eliminate the content of current section 656.40(a) and add new content in proposed section 656.40(a), using a framework similar to proposed section 656.10 (“Application filing requirements”). In proposed section 656.40(a)(1), the Department proposes to add specific details on what to file to request a PWD. Similarly, the Department proposes adding the location and method of filing in proposed section 656.40(a)(2).
Consistent with current practice, proposed paragraph (a)(1)(i) would require that an employer submit a completed Application for Prevailing Wage Determination, including its appendix and supporting documentation, if applicable, to the NPC. Proposed section 656.40(a)(1)(ii) codifies the current practice in which employers provide their legal name, trade name(s), valid FEIN, and valid business address with a physical location in the United States as part of their Application for Prevailing Wage Determination. Codifying these practices would provide additional clarification to employers about what information they must provide when submitting an Application for Prevailing Wage Determination to the NPC.
