Proposed paragraphs (a)(1)(iii) to (v) address additional documentation requirements in the event an employer seeks a PWD based on a wage source other than the Occupational Employment and Wage Statistics (OEWS) program.[160] The proposed rule continues to require employers to request a PWD based on a collective bargaining agreement (CBA) or professional sports league rules or regulations, when the job opportunity is subject to those wage sources. In the event the job opportunity is not subject to a CBA or professional sports league rules or regulations, the proposed rule continues to permit the employer to use Service Contract Act or Davis Bacon Act as the wage source, when applicable, or to request consideration of an employer-provided survey as an alternative to a PWD determined using the BLS OEWS program. Although the current rule does not state that documentation of the requested wage source must be submitted with the Application for Prevailing Wage Determination, the electronic filing system currently will not permit submission of an Application for Prevailing Wage Determination without additional documentation uploaded, if a source other than OEWS is requested. The Department proposes to codify its existing practice of requiring employers to provide documentation of their selected wage source, if applicable, for the CO's review concurrent with their filing of the Application for Prevailing Wage Determination. This practice supports efficient processing by reducing unnecessary requests for documentation necessary to issue a PWD.
The current rule also does not specify what documentation is necessary to enable the CO to assess whether the ( printed page 73975) employer's suggested wage source may be used to establish the prevailing wage, which the Department proposes to add in proposed subparagraphs (a)(1)(iii) to (v), based on current practice. This proposal to codify what documents to submit for the CO's review will increase transparency and better inform employers how to request an alternative wage source, if appropriate. In addition, it will avoid unnecessary requests for documentation, which slow processing.
For example, proposed subparagraphs (a)(1)(iii)(A) through (C), clarify that to request CO consideration of a CBA, employers must provide a: (1) copy of the relevant sections of the CBA, which must include the validity dates of the agreement; the position(s) and description of the job(s) covered; the wage section, including tables and explanations; and the signature page with effective dates of the agreement; (2) letter on letterhead from the employer or employer's representative that states the relevant portions of the agreement with effective dates; the title of the position in the agreement appropriate to the job duties, requirements, and location; and wage value appropriate to the job duties, requirements, and location; and (3) letter on letterhead from the CBA authorized representative that states the relevant portions of the agreement with effective dates; the title of the position in the agreement appropriate to the job duties, requirements, and location; and wage value appropriate to the job duties, requirements, and location. As is current practice, if the NPC determines the CBA is valid and covers the job opportunity for which the employer seeks a PWD, the NPC will use the CBA wage as the prevailing wage, even where the OEWS wage for the occupation in the AIE is higher. When the NPC determines, based on the documentation provided, that the CBA does not meet all the requirements, the NPC will continue its current practice of issuing an OEWS wage for the employer's job opportunity. As discussed in proposed section 656.18, in connection with documentation requirements for Applications for Permanent Employment Certification for professional athlete or professional sports team coach job opportunities, the Department recognizes that unions may vary in their responsiveness to an employer's request for the letter to be submitted to the NPC. The Department invites comments about whether and how an employer could provide alternative documentation that would not compromise program integrity, in the absence of a response from union.
The Department also proposes to add language in proposed subparagraph (a)(1)(iv) to address the documentation necessary to issue PWDs for professional athletes and professional sports team coaches whose wages are set by professional sports league rules or regulations, which is necessary due to the proposed elimination of current section 656.40(f). Proposed section 656.40(a)(1)(iv) clarifies that if an employer is requesting a PWD for a job opportunity for a professional athlete or professional sports team coach covered by professional sports league rules or regulations with wage provisions, the employer must concurrently submit with its Application for Prevailing Wage Determination a copy of the applicable league rules or regulations. Similar to the current regulation, this proposed language comports with section 212(p)(2) of the INA.[161] Further, as noted above, requiring a copy of the applicable league rules or regulations at the time of filing allows the Department to review the league rules or regulations when determining the prevailing wage, likely reducing the number of RFIs and further streamlining the PWD process.
Similarly, the Department proposes to relocate the content of current section 656.40(g)(2) to proposed section 656.40(a)(1)(v), with minor revisions to the existing language to codify its current practice for the CO's consideration of a survey other than OEWS. This proposed provision continues to require the employer to concurrently submit the actual wage survey with its Application for Prevailing Wage Determination. The current regulation lists requirements for a survey but does not explicitly state that copies of the actual survey pages are required. Currently, the NPC frequently contacts the employer to request the actual survey instrument through an RFI so that the NPC can determine if the survey meets the methodological requirements before issuing a PWD. To better streamline the process, improve efficiency, and align the regulation with the NPC's practice of reviewing the actual survey instrument alongside the submitted Application for Prevailing Wage Determination, the Department proposes to specify in its regulations that employers must concurrently submit a copy of the survey at the time of filing the application. Consistent with current policy and practice, the survey must contain specific information about the survey methodology, including sample size and source, sample selection procedures, survey job descriptions, job and wage leveling chart, and list of participants surveyed to allow a determination of whether the survey meets all the requirements of this section.[162] In situations where the survey instrument is incomplete, the Department will continue to send an RFI before issuing a PWD. Although the proposal does not change current practice nor create new burdens on the regulated community, by codifying this information in the regulation, the Department anticipates a reduction in the number of RFIs needed before issuing a PWD, thereby maintaining processing efficiency.
Proposed section 656.40(a)(2)(i) would mandate electronic filing for Applications for Prevailing Wage Determination, which is consistent with the Department's proposal to mandate electronic filing for Applications for Permanent Employment Certification in section 656.10(c). The Department also would provide the same limited exceptions, as are available under 656.10(c), to mandatory electronic filing for employers who are unable or limited in their ability to use or access electronic forms to submit an Application for Prevailing Wage Determination. This proposed provision is intended to maximize end-to-end electronic processing of Applications for Prevailing Wage Determination, which is an important technological objective of the Department.
Although electronic filing of applications using the FLAG System is not currently mandated, in the Department's experience, employers prefer to use electronic filing to request a PWD. Based on data for Applications for Prevailing Wage Determination processed during fiscal years 2023, 2024, and 2025, more than 99 percent of employer's Applications for Prevailing Wage Determination were submitted electronically to the NPC for processing using the FLAG System. Therefore, requiring electronic filing of Applications for Prevailing Wage Determination continues the current practice for nearly all employers. ( printed page 73976)
NPC processing
The Department proposes to relocate current section 656.40(b) (“Determinations”) to proposed section 656.40(d) and add new language in proposed section 656.40(b) to discuss the NPC review process for Applications for Prevailing Wage Determination. The processes described in this proposed section are consistent with the NPC's current practices and the Department is merely proposing to codify them for transparency and clarification. Proposed section 656.40(b)(1) explains that when NPC receives an employer's Application for Prevailing Wage Determination, the CO reviews it to ensure it complies with all applicable program requirements, including that all the necessary information pertaining to the employer's job opportunity is included in the Application for Prevailing Wage Determination. After reviewing the employer's application, the CO will decide whether to issue a RFI under proposed section 656.40(c) or issue a PWD under proposed section 656.40(d).
Proposed section 656.40(b)(2) would require the CO to electronically send notice(s) or request(s) that require a response to employers, unless the employer was permitted to file by mail as set forth in § 656.10(c). For employers permitted to file by mail, the CO would send the notice or request via traditional methods to assure next day delivery. Similarly, the employer's response to such a notice or request must be filed electronically, except that an employer permitted to file by mail as set forth in § 656.10(c) may use traditional methods to assure next day delivery. The proposed rule would require the employer to send the response by the due date or the next business day, if the due date falls on a Sunday or Federal holiday. Consistent with current practice, if the CO does not timely receive the employer's response by the due date, the CO will return the employer's application unprocessed. While the Department encourages electronic communication, this proposed paragraph would retain the option to issue and respond to notices and requests using traditional methods that ensure next-day delivery, which may be necessary in some cases.
Proposed section 656.40(b)(3) provides that an employer may file only one Application for Prevailing Wage Determination for the same job opportunity and AIE. This proposal promotes efficiency and is consistent with similar provisions in the temporary labor certification programs the Department administers.[163] This provision would prevent the Department from receiving and processing duplicate applications that result in a general unnecessary delay in processing. Submitting multiple Applications for Prevailing Wage Determination for the same job opportunity for a PWD in the same wage year is unnecessary, as a single PWD can be used for multiple Applications for Permanent Employment Certification that offer the same job opportunity in the same AIE(s). This provision also would reduce duplicative processing by preventing an employer from filing a new Application for Prevailing Wage Determination for the same job opportunity in the same AIE when the employer's appeal of a PWD for that job opportunity in the same AIE is pending. Preventing an employer from filing a subsequent Application for Prevailing Wage Determination also serves to enhance program integrity by deterring an employer that is seeking to manipulate the prevailing wage process from submitting numerous filings in the hope that one of the PWDs might be adjudicated in a manner more favorable to the employer. As proposed, the CO will return duplicative applications unprocessed. The Department does not intend for this proposal to prevent employers from submitting a new Application for Prevailing Wage Determination for the same job opportunity near the expiration of their current PWD in order to obtain an updated PWD for the new wage year with a new validity period.
Requests for Information
The Department proposes to relocate the content of current section 656.40(c) (“Validity period”) to proposed section 656.40(h), with revisions, as discussed below. The Department is proposing to add new language in proposed section 656.40(c) to codify the NPC's current RFI practice. Currently, the CO may issue one or more RFIs when the employer's Application for Prevailing Wage Determination is incomplete, contains errors or inaccuracies, or otherwise contains information that is not sufficient to issue a PWD. Consistent with current practice, under proposed section 656.40(c), the CO would send RFIs to the employer with a copy to the employer's attorney or agent, if applicable. As proposed, the CO could issue multiple RFIs to allow employers every opportunity to provide necessary information to ensure the employer's application is complete. This approach enables the CO to process the application based on the most accurate information possible to issue a prevailing wage that does not adversely affect the wages of U.S. workers.
In proposed section 656.40(c)(2), the Department outlines the content of an RFI, which largely continues current practice but introduces a change involving amendment of an employer's Application for Permanent Employment Certification to enable the NPC to issue a PWD. As proposed, the RFI would continue to state the reason the CO could not issue a PWD and offer the employer the opportunity to submit a response within 7 calendar days. In addition, the RFI would explain that if the employer submits a response, the CO will review the response, determine whether to issue a PWD, and, as necessary, make amendments to the Application for Prevailing Wage Determination so that the CO can issue a PWD. Currently, the CO does not treat the employer's response as authorizing amendments to the employer's Application for Prevailing Wage Determination that would enable the CO to issue a PWD. This change in practice would save both the Department and the employer's time, eliminating additional back-and-forth communication between the CO and the employer for the purpose of acquiring explicit permission to amend the employer's application so that a PWD can be issued. The Department intends this provision to enable the CO to identify information that would need to be corrected for the CO to issue a PWD ( e.g., conflicting information regarding travel). This provision would not permit employers to request modification of an Application for Prevailing Wage Determination. To correct information, the employer may request withdrawal of the Application for Prevailing Wage Determination under proposed section 656.40(i) and then file a new, corrected application. Furthermore, once a PWD is submitted in connection with, and becomes a part of an Application for Permanent Employment Certification, it will be subject to the “no modification” provision in 20 CFR 656.11(b).
As proposed, the RFI also would explain what will happen if the employer does not respond by the due date. If the employer does not send their response by the due date ( i.e., within 7 calendar days from the date the RFI was issued by the CO), the application will be deemed abandoned, the CO will return the application unprocessed to the employer without issuing a PWD, and the employer may file a new Application for Prevailing Wage Determination with the NPC. This proposal improves transparency, ( printed page 73977) supports processing efficiency, and is also consistent with other programs and regulations.[164] The Department seeks public comment regarding the number of days employers have to respond to RFI before the PWD is deemed abandoned.
Determinations
Consistent with the Department's proposed reorganization of section 656.40—to address what, when, and how to submit to the NPC to request a PWD in proposed paragraph (a) and processing information in proposed paragraphs (b) and (c), before addressing the CO's issuance of a PWD—the Department proposes to address determinations in proposed paragraph (d).
After relocating the content from current section 656.40(b), titled “Determinations” to proposed section 656.40(d), the Department proposes technical changes for consistency and conformity with other sections of this proposed rule, to improve clarity, and to update internal references. In addition, the Department proposes to update the term “DOL Occupational Employment Statistics Survey” to “Occupational Employment and Wage Statistics survey,” to reflect the current name.[165]
The Department proposes one substantive change in proposed section 656.40(d). In proposed paragraph (d)(3), the Department proposes to eliminate the exception in the current rule that allows a survey to provide the median wage if the arithmetic mean wage is not provided; as a result, all surveys must provide the arithmetic mean of wages of workers similarly employed in the AIE. Under this proposed rule, if an employer submits a survey that provides only the median wage, the survey would not meet the criteria to issue a PWD. Because the requested survey could not be used to issue a PWD, the CO would issue the PWD using an OEWS wage. The Department proposes this change to be consistent with the methodology of the OEWS survey. Also, based on the Department's experience, employers have generally submitted surveys with the arithmetic mean of the wages of all workers similarly employed in the AIE. As a result, the Department asserts that elimination of the option to provide a survey using a median wage will not have a significant practical effect because the NPC rarely receives surveys that only include a median wage. Moreover, the Department continues to think that setting the wage below the mean would have a depressive effect on wages, and therefore this proposed change is consistent with the Department's obligation to ensure that U.S. workers' wages are not adversely affected when setting a prevailing wage for workers employed in the PERM program.
PWD Validity Period
The Department proposes to relocate current section 656.40(c), entitled “Validity period” to proposed section 656.40(h), with revisions. Proposed section 656.40(h)(1) continues to require each PWD to have a specific validity period. The Department proposes a technical revision to clarify that the CO will specify the validity period of the PWD as 90 to 365 calendar days from the date of issuance, rather than 90 days to 1 year. In addition, added language clarifies that an employer cannot use a PWD for PERM labor certification purposes, while simultaneously requesting review of that PWD under section 656.41. As stated in proposed section 656.40(h)(1), “[a] request for review under section 656.41 renders the PWD invalid until the CO issues an affirmed or modified PWD.” After the review under section 656.41 is complete, the CO will issue an affirmed or modified PWD, which will specify the validity period for the PWD. Then, the PWD can be used to support an Application for Permanent Employment Certification during the validity period. A new proposed section 656.40(h)(3) would clarify the option available for an employer to use the same PWD for multiple, consistent Applications for Permanent Employment Certification. These proposed revisions clarify the Department's existing prevailing wage process and bring certainty to employers and stakeholders of the prevailing wage program.
In proposed section 656.40(h)(2), the Department proposes a substantive change to employers' use of a PWD for an Application for Permanent Employment Certification. Currently, if an employer either begins pre-filing recruitment or files the Application for Permanent Employment Certification within the PWD's validity period, they may use that PWD when filing the Application for Permanent Employment Certification. The Department proposes to eliminate the option for an employer to begin pre-filing recruitment before receiving a PWD for the job opportunity to be offered to U.S. workers during the labor market test. As proposed in subparagraph (h)(2), the employer must obtain a PWD before beginning pre-filing recruitment for the Application for Permanent Employment Certification and must begin pre-filing recruitment—whether in accordance with section 656.17, section 656.18, or section 656.21—within the validity period OFLC specified on the Application for Prevailing Wage Determination. This change would improve program integrity by ensuring pre-filing recruitment offers at least the prevailing wage rate the CO determines applies to the employer's job offer.
Similarly Employed
The Department proposes to relocate current section 656.40(d), titled “Similarly employed” to proposed section 656.40(g), without any revisions.
PWDs for Institutions of Higher Education, Affiliated or Related Nonprofit Entities, Nonprofit Research Organizations, or Governmental Research Organizations
Section 656.40(e) implements Congress' prevailing wage computation requirements for job opportunities at an institution of higher education (as defined in section 101(a) of the Higher Education Act of 1965 (20 U.S.C. 1001(a)(2000)), or an affiliated or related nonprofit entity, a nonprofit research organization, or a Governmental research organization.[166] In computing the prevailing wage for such job opportunities, the CO takes into account the wage levels of employees only at such institutions and organizations in the AIE. The Department defines affiliated or related nonprofit entity in current section 656.40(e)(1)(ii) using DHS' definition at 8 CFR 214.1(h)(19)(iii)(C), which DHS has updated since the current rule was ( printed page 73978) published.[167] The Department proposes to update section 656.40(e)(1)(ii) in the proposed rule to reflect DHS' current regulatory language. In addition, the Department proposes to include the DHS regulatory citation in proposed 656.40(e)(1) ( i.e.,8 CFR 214.2(h)(19)(iii)) for employers to use for reference. Also, the Department proposes one technical edit in this section by adding a hyphen in the word “pre-accreditation” in section 656.40(e)(1)(i)(E). Otherwise, the Department retains this section in the proposed rule without any other changes.
Professional Athletes and Professional Sports Team Coaches
The Department proposes to eliminate the content of current paragraph (f), titled “Professional athletes,” from section 656.40. As discussed in proposed section 656.3, the Department proposes to relocate the content of current section 656.40(f) that is definitional to proposed section 656.3. In addition, the Department's revisions throughout proposed section 656.40 appropriately address PWDs for professional athletes and professional sports team coaches, without retaining the content of paragraph (f) in section 656.40.
Surveys Other Than OEWS
The Department proposes to relocate current section 656.40(g), titled “Employer-provided wage information” to proposed section 656.40(f), with revisions. The revisions, which eliminate some content of subparagraph (1) and all of subparagraphs (4) and (5), are necessary to conform with the Department's proposals to require wage survey information to be submitted concurrently with the Application for Prevailing Wage Determination and to streamline the PWD process by eliminating unnecessary correspondence. In addition, the Department proposes to eliminate current subparagraph (2), as its content is now addressed in proposed section 656.40(a)(1)(v), and redesignate subparagraph (3) as subparagraph (2), without any substantive changes.
Withdrawal
The Department proposes to delete the content of current section 656.40(i) and repurpose section 656.40(i) to codify how an employer may request withdrawal of an Application for Prevailing Wage Determination. Proposed section 656.40(i) explains the NPC's current practice of allowing employers to withdraw a pending Application for Prevailing Wage Determination.[168] That is, if the CO has not issued a PWD for the job opportunity for which the employer submitted the Application for Prevailing Wage Determination, the employer may withdraw their application by submitting a request in writing, either electronically or via mail, to the NPC. The request must identify the unique application number and state the reason(s) for the withdrawal. This provision clarifies the existing prevailing wage process and practice with no substantive or procedural changes.
Other Eliminated Provisions
The Department proposes to eliminate the content of current section 656.40(h), titled “Submittal of supplemental information by employer.” Currently, employers must go through two stages of reviews when they disagree with a PWD. They must first submit a request for redetermination under current section 656.40(h) before they are permitted to submit a request for Center Director Review (CDR) under current section 656.41. The Department proposes to eliminate the first step of review so employers may directly submit a request for (CDR) under section 656.41. Deleting this section would reduce the regulatory burden on employers from having multiple review processes. In addition, it would streamline the process by which the Department receives and reviews employers' Applications for Prevailing Wage Determination by eliminating submission of additional information outside the RFI process proposed in section 656.40(c).
The Department also proposes to eliminate current content section 656.40(i), titled “Frequent users,” because the Department believes it is no longer relevant or necessary. The proposed removal of this paragraph is consistent with the Department's goal of simplifying its regulations. In addition, the proposed language added in section 656.40(h)(3) codifies current practice whereby an employer may use just one PWD for multiple job opportunities if the positions have the same job duties and requirements and are in the same AIE.
Similarly, the Department proposes to eliminate current section 656.40(j), titled “Fee prohibited.” This section is no longer relevant, as the responsibility to issue PWDs was transferred from the SWAs to OFLC on January 1, 2010.[169]
F. Discussion of Proposed New to 20 CFR Part 656, Subpart E
The Department is proposing to amend part 656 by adding a new subpart E, which would include one new provision in a new section 656.50, to add a severability clause to part 656.
1. Proposed 20 CFR 656.50, Severability
Proposed section 656.50 would add a severability provision to part 656. This new content would provide that if any provision of the Department's part 656 regulations is held to be invalid or unenforceable by its terms, or as applied to any person or circumstance, or stayed pending further agency action, the provision will be construed so as to continue to give the maximum effect to the provision permitted by law. As proposed, section 656.50 further provides that where such holding is one of total invalidity or unenforceability, the provision would be severable from the corresponding part and will not affect the remainder thereof.
Consistent with its reasoning when proposing to add such a provision within the H-2A regulations, the Department believes that adding a severability clause to part 656 is appropriate because each provision within part 656 is capable of operating independently from the others and to enhance the Department's capabilities to monitor compliance and conduct enforcement.[170] It is also important to the Department and the regulated community that the PERM and PWD regulations continue to operate consistent with the expectations of employers and workers, even if a portion of those regulations is held to be invalid or unenforceable.
The Department invites comments about the substance and scope of proposed section 656.39 and requests the public's views on other issues related to severability, such as whether the rule in general includes provisions that would not be amenable to severability; whether specific parts of the rule could operate independently; whether the benefits of the rule would continue to justify the costs should particular provisions be severed; or whether individual provisions are essential to the entire rule's workability. ( printed page 73979)
IV. Administrative Information
A. Executive Order 12866: Regulatory Planning and Review, Executive Order 13563: Improving Regulation and Regulatory Review, and Executive Order 14192: Unleashing Prosperity Through Deregulation
Under E.O. 12866, the Office of Information and Regulatory Affairs (OIRA) in OMB determines whether a regulatory action is significant and, therefore, subject to the requirements of the Executive Order and review by OMB. Regulatory Planning and Review, 58 FR 51735 (Oct. 4, 1993). Section 3(f) of E.O. 12866 defines a “significant regulatory action” as an action that is likely to result in a rule that may: (1) have an annual effect on the economy of $100 million or more, or adversely affect in a material way the economy, a sector of the economy, productivity, competition, jobs, the environment, public health or safety, or State, local, or Tribal governments or communities; (2) create a serious inconsistency or otherwise interfere with an action taken or planned by another agency; (3) materially alter the budgetary impact of entitlement, grants, user fees, or loan programs or the rights and obligations of recipients thereof; or (4) raise novel legal or policy issues arising out of legal mandates, the President's priorities, or the principles set forth in the Executive Order. OIRA has reviewed this rule and designated it a significant action under E.O. 12866.
E.O. 13563 directs agencies to, among other things, propose or adopt a regulation only upon a reasoned determination that its benefits justify its costs; the regulation is tailored to impose the least burden on society, consistent with achieving the regulatory objectives; and in choosing among alternative regulatory approaches, the agency has selected those approaches that maximize net benefits. Improving Regulation and Regulatory Review, 76 FR 3821, 3821 (Jan. 21, 2011), E.O. 13563 recognizes that some costs and benefits are difficult to quantify and provides that, where appropriate and permitted by law, agencies may consider and discuss qualitative values that are difficult or impossible to quantify, including equity, human dignity, fairness, and distributive impacts. Id.
Executive Order 14192, titled “Unleashing Prosperity Through Deregulation,” was issued on January 31, 2025. This executive order directs agencies to significantly reduce the private expenditures required to comply with Federal regulations and provides that “any new incremental costs associated with new regulations shall, to the extent permitted by law, be offset by the elimination of existing costs associated with at least 10 prior regulations.” This proposed rule is not an Executive Order 14192 regulatory action because it is being issued with respect to an immigration-related function of the United States. The rule's primary direct purpose is to implement or interpret the immigration laws of the United States (as described in INA § 101(a)(17), 8 U.S.C. 1101(a)(17)) or any other function performed by the U.S. Federal Government with respect to aliens. See OMB Memorandum M-25-20, “Guidance Implementing Section 3 of Executive Order 14192, titled `Unleashing Prosperity Through Deregulation'” (Mar. 26, 2025).
1. Need for Regulation
The Department has determined that this rulemaking is necessary to modernize the permanent labor certification (PERM) program so that it continues to fulfill the Secretary's statutory responsibilities under section 212(a)(5)(A) of the INA. The current regulatory framework has remained largely unchanged since 2005, despite significant changes in labor market conditions, recruitment practices, technology, and the manner in which employers recruit and hire workers.
Modernizing the PERM program is a regulatory action the Department must undertake to be able to adapt existing regulations governing the statutory permanent labor certification requirement to the current characteristics and demands of the labor market in the United States, address challenges encountered by the Department to more effectively administer the program while addressing employers' bona fide workforce needs who rely on the program to employ aliens in permanent positions that cannot be filled by qualified and available U.S. workers. For example, over the past two decades, employers have increasingly shifted away from print advertising toward electronic recruitment platforms, remote work has become commonplace across many industries, and workforce mobility and employer hiring practices have evolved considerably. At the same time, the Department has gained substantial operational experience administering the PERM program and identified areas where existing regulations no longer adequately reflect current labor market realities or provide sufficient clarity regarding employer obligations, as well as program administration, integrity and enforcement. These developments demonstrate the need to update the Department's regulations to ensure that the statutory PERM labor certification requirement remains an effective mechanism for determining whether qualified U.S. workers are available for permanent job opportunities and whether the employment of aliens would adversely affect the wages and working conditions of similarly employed U.S. workers.
The Department has also determined that rulemaking is necessary to strengthen protections for U.S. workers and improve integrity of the labor market test required by the INA. Decades of experience administering the PERM program has revealed opportunities to better ensure that employers recruit U.S. workers in good faith using recruitment methods that better reflect contemporary hiring practices and provide the broadest exposure possible of available job opportunities to prospective U.S. applicants. Moreover, the Department has identified the need to strengthen safeguards related to layoffs, clarify employer obligations during the recruitment and adjudication process, improve notice requirements, enhance oversight of third-party employment arrangements, and increase accountability for employers, attorneys, and agents participating in the program. In addition, following its review of public comments received in response to the Department's 2023 RFI concerning Schedule A, the Department concluded that it lacks a reliable, objective, and transparent methodology for maintaining or expanding Schedule A in a manner consistent with its statutory responsibilities. Accordingly, the Department has determined that regulatory changes are necessary to ensure that labor market testing more accurately reflects current labor market conditions and that all employers seeking permanent labor certification are subject to standards that are consistent, transparent, and aligned with the INA.
This rulemaking is also intended to modernize and streamline administration of the PERM program by codifying electronic filing and communication practices, clarifying regulatory standards, simplifying procedures, and eliminating outdated requirements that no longer provide meaningful regulatory value. The Department proposes to consolidate and reorganize regulatory provisions, modernize and streamline prevailing wage and application processing procedures, clarify integrity actions, as well as the appeals and review processes, and remove obsolete ( printed page 73980) requirements associated with paper processing and duplicative regulatory provisions. These changes are expected to improve administrative efficiency, reduce unnecessary burdens on employers and the Department, facilitate coordination with DHS, and improve the consistency and predictability of program administration.
Accordingly, the Department is proposing comprehensive revisions to 20 CFR part 656 that modernize recruitment requirements, strengthen protections for U.S. workers, enhance program integrity, improve processing efficiency, and update the regulatory framework governing permanent labor certifications to better reflect current labor market conditions and the Department's statutory responsibilities. Collectively, the proposed revisions are intended to preserve employers' ability to access employment-based immigration pathways when qualified U.S. workers are unavailable, while ensuring that the PERM labor certification process continues to provide meaningful protections for the U.S. workforce and supports the fair and effective administration of the Nation's employment-based immigration system.
2. Analysis Considerations
The Department estimated the costs and cost savings of this proposed rule relative to the existing baseline ( i.e., the current practices for complying, at a minimum, with the PERM program as currently codified at 20 CFR part 656, subparts A, B, C, and D).
In accordance with the regulatory analysis guidance articulated in OMB's Circular A-4 and consistent with the Department's practices in previous rulemakings, this regulatory analysis focuses on the likely consequences of the proposed rule ( i.e., costs, cost-savings, and benefits that accrue to entities affected). The analysis covers 10 years to ensure it captures measurable costs and transfer payments that accrue over time. The Department expresses all quantifiable impacts in 2025 dollars and uses discount rates of 3 and 7 percent, pursuant to Circular A-4 [171] published on October 9, 2003.
3. Subject-by-Subject Analysis
In this section, the Department describes all anticipated benefits, costs, and cost-savings of the proposed rule, including both quantified and unquantified impacts. Each impact is described, and quantifiable impacts are estimated while nonquantifiable impacts are discussed qualitatively.
i. Benefits Discussion
The Department expects the proposed rule to generate substantial benefits by modernizing the PERM labor certification process, strengthening protections for U.S. workers, improving the integrity and efficiency of the employment-based immigrant visa system, and reducing administrative inefficiencies for employers and the Federal Government. Collectively, the proposed changes would better align the PERM program with current labor market conditions, modern recruitment practices, and the Department's statutory obligations under INA section 212(a)(5)(A), and modernize the program so that it becomes more efficient and transparent.
A principal benefit of the proposed rule is the modernization of existing recruitment methods used to test the U.S. labor market for purposes of filing a PERM application. The current PERM framework relies heavily on print newspaper advertisements and recruitment methods established nearly two decades ago, which no longer reflect how employers recruit workers or how workers search for employment opportunities. The proposed rule replaces outdated print-focused recruitment requirements with modern electronic recruitment methods, including advertisements on employer websites, commercial job search websites, social media platforms, and SWA job orders, as well as job fairs or campus recruiting events. These proposed changes are expected to improve dissemination of job opportunities to U.S. workers and increase the likelihood of qualified and available U.S. workers becoming aware of and applying for PERM-related positions before employers seek to permanently employ aliens.
The proposal also would require employers to recruit U.S. workers using the same recruitment methods and timeframes they customarily use for comparable non-PERM positions. This approach is expected to improve the equality of employment opportunities by disincentivizing employers from departing from their ordinary PERM recruitment methods or efforts they normally employ, in ways that may discourage U.S. worker participation. By requiring recruitment practices that more closely resemble real-world hiring practices, the rule would strengthen the integrity of the labor market test and improve confidence that PERM recruitment is conducted in good faith.
The Department also expects significant benefits from the proposed extension of recruitment duration requirements. Under the proposed rule, most advertisements would remain active for at least 90 calendar days, or longer if the employer normally recruits for similar positions for a longer period. Longer recruitment periods are expected to improve labor market exposure for job opportunities and provide workers additional time to find out about the job offer, become familiar with the job requirements and apply for available positions. The Department believes this longer recruitment timeframe would better reflect actual labor market conditions and would align more closely with the amount of time unemployed workers spend searching for employment.
Another major benefit the Department believes would derive from the proposed rule is enhanced protection for U.S. workers impacted by layoffs not only throughout the PERM process but also while the employer pursues the visa petition before DHS. Specifically, the proposal would expand current layoff-related protections by prohibiting the filing of a PERM application within the 2 years after a relevant layoff and, in the event a relevant layoff occurs after filing a PERM application, requiring employers to consider laid-off U.S. workers during a significantly broader timeframe extending through adjudication of the associated Form I-140 petition or final case resolution. The Department expects these changes to reduce the risk of employers seeking permanent foreign labor while simultaneously displacing U.S. workers in the same or similar occupations. These provisions are also expected to improve employment opportunities for laid-off workers and better align labor certification determinations with actual labor market conditions at the time of adjudication of PERM applications and visa petitions.
The proposed rule also seeks to prohibit the placement of aliens at third-party worksites experiencing layoffs where indicia of an employment relationship exist between the alien and the third-party employer. This provision is expected to reduce the risk of indirect displacement of U.S. workers through staffing arrangements or contractor relationships and improve accountability and transparency in third-party employment arrangements.
The Department further expects benefits from increased transparency requirements related to layoffs and notice obligations. Employers would be required to notify DOL and, where applicable, USCIS, of layoffs occurring ( printed page 73981) during the relevant period and update recruitment reports accordingly. Expanded NOF requirements, including electronic notice procedures and notice obligations for third-party worksites, are expected to improve U.S. workers' awareness of pending labor certification applications and facilitate the reporting of fraud, misrepresentation, or the existence of qualified U.S. workers to the Department and USCIS.
The proposed elimination of Schedule A is also expected to produce important benefits. The Department determined that it currently lacks a reliable, objective, and transparent methodology to justify continuing categorical labor market test exemptions for occupations listed on Schedule A. Eliminating Schedule A would ensure that all occupations subject to PERM labor certification requirements undergo a labor market test unless exempted by statute. The Department expects this change to improve consistency with the INA's labor certification requirements and provide U.S. workers an opportunity to apply for all PERM-covered positions.
The proposed rule also contains substantial program integrity improvements. These changes include enhanced employer attestations, expanded audit and supervised recruitment authority, stronger debarment provisions, successor-in-interest liability standards, and independent accountability for attorneys and agents. The Department expects these changes to deter fraud and abuse, improve compliance with program requirements, and strengthen public confidence in the PERM labor certification process. Expanded authority to hold attorneys, agents, and successor entities accountable for misconduct is expected to reduce opportunities for program circumvention and abusive filing practices.
The proposal would also modernize and streamline the administration of the PERM program through mandatory electronic filing and expanded electronic communications. These changes are expected to reduce paperwork burdens, lower mailing and administrative costs, improve application completeness and data quality, reduce processing delays associated with deficient applications, and facilitate faster communication between OFLC and stakeholders. The vast majority of employers already use electronic filing systems, based on FY 2021-FY 2025 data, approximately 99.61 percent of PERM labor certification applications were filed electronically. Department expects the transition costs associated with mandatory electronic filing to be minimal for most employers.
In addition, the Department estimates that the proposed centralized electronic processing and electronic transmission of determinations to USCIS will improve interagency coordination, facilitate data sharing, reduce duplicative submissions, and improve fraud detection capabilities. The Department also expects operational efficiencies and better public experience with the program from codifying existing adjudicative practices, clarifying audit and appeal procedures, and reducing unnecessary BALCA docketing.
The proposed rule would further improve regulatory clarity and predictability by modernizing existing terminology, consolidating procedures, and clarifying definitions and standards throughout part 656. These changes are expected to improve the regulated community understanding of program requirements, reduce ambiguity and inadvertent noncompliance, improve adjudicative consistency, and reduce litigation risk.
Overall, the Department expects the proposed rule to generate substantial benefits by improving labor market testing, strengthening protections for U.S. workers, increasing transparency and accountability by all program participants, modernizing program administration, reducing inefficiencies, and enhancing the integrity of the PERM labor certification process. The proposals are expected to create a more effective, transparent, and administratively efficient system that better reflects contemporary labor market conditions while preserving access to employment-based immigration pathways for employers with legitimate workforce needs.
ii. Costs
In the following sections, the Department presents the costs estimated to be incurred by relevant stakeholders.
a. Quantifiable Costs
Rule Familiarization
To quantify rule familiarization costs, the Department employs a methodology that reflects the structure of the PERM filing market. PERM applications are primarily filed either by outside immigration law firms on behalf of employers or directly by employers themselves, although agents also may file PERM applications on behalf of employers. Accordingly, the Department estimates familiarization costs separately for two groups: (1) unique law firms that submitted PERM applications on behalf of employers, and (2) unique employers that filed PERM applications directly without outside legal counsel.
For law firms, the Department assumes that one attorney per firm is responsible for reviewing and understanding the regulatory changes associated with the rule. The Department values this time using the fully-loaded hourly compensation rate for Attorneys. For employers filing without outside counsel, the Department assumes that one Human Resources (HR) specialist per employer is responsible for rule familiarization and values this time using the fully loaded hourly compensation rate for HR Specialists.
The Department used the hourly compensation rates presented in Exhibit 1 to estimate rule familiarization costs. BLS's OEWS data show that the mean hourly wage of HR Specialists (SOC 13-1071) is $39.42 and the hourly rate of an Attorney is $89.35 (SOC 23-1011).[172] The Department applied a 42-percent benefits rate [173] and a 17-percent overhead rate,[174] resulting in a fully loaded hourly wage of $62.68 for HR Specialists and $142.07 for Attorneys.
Based on FY 2023-FY 2025 PERM certification data, the Department identified 5,433 unique law firms and 41,747 unique employers. Assuming one hour of familiarization time per attorney and per HR specialist, the law firm familiarization cost is estimated at $0.77 million, and the employer familiarization cost is estimated at $2.62 million for a combined first-year total of $3.38 million, once the rule is implemented. The annualized cost over a ten-year period is projected at $0.39 million at (3% discount rate) and $0.48 million (7% discount rate).
( printed page 73982)
Employer Signature on All Supporting Documents (§ 656.4)
The primary cost associated with the employer signature requirement is the incremental time and coordination burden imposed on employers who are represented by attorneys or agents and who are not currently in the practice of signing all documentation submitted to OFLC beyond the Application for Permanent Employment Certification and recruitment report. For these employers, the proposed rule adds a workflow step to the filing and processing cycle. Specifically, the need to obtain the employer's signature on audit responses, supplemental information submissions, and other materials before transmitting them to OFLC.
The Department notes that the additional signature burden is not uniform across the population of employers filing Applications for Permanent Employment Certification, it is concentrated among those whose applications are selected for audit examination or placed into supervised recruitment, as those are the principal processing stages at which employers would be required to sign and submit additional documentation beyond the application and recruitment report. For applications that proceed through standard adjudication without audit or supervised recruitment, the proposed signature requirement would apply only to the Application for Permanent Employment Certification and the recruitment report, both of which are already subject to employer signature requirements under current regulations and thus would impose no incremental burden on those employers.
The Department calculates this cost by multiplying the total number of employers (including Schedule A and Sheepherders) by 1 hour (on average) and the wage rate for an HR Specialist. Based on FY 2023-FY 2025 PERM certification data, there were approximately 26,277 unique employers that filed PERM applications per year, an average of 1,417 unique Schedule A employers per year,[175] and an average of 4 Sheepherder applications per year.[176] The annual cost is calculated as follows: (26,277 + 1,417 + 4) × $62.68 × 1 hour = $1,736,046. Over the ten-year period of analysis, the total undiscounted cost would be approximately $17.36 million.
The annualized cost over the ten-year span is projected at approximately $1.79 million (3% discount rate) and $1.86 million (7% discount rate).
Extended NOF Duration and Locations (§ 656.14)
The primary cost associated with the expanded NOF requirements is the additional administrative burden of maintaining the posting, whether physical or electronic, for a significantly longer duration than currently required. Under the proposed rule, the NOF must remain available throughout the entirety of OFLC's adjudication of the Application for Permanent Employment Certification and USCIS's adjudication of the associated Form I-140 petition, which can span months or, in some cases, years. For employers who post the NOF physically, this may require periodic monitoring to ensure the posting remains visible and unobstructed throughout the extended period, consistent with the proposed requirement that the notice be of sufficient size, clearly visible, and unobstructed while posted. For employers who post electronically through their existing internal or external websites, the incremental burden of maintaining the NOF through the conclusion of adjudication is expected to be minimal, as sustaining a web page posting requires little ongoing effort once the notice is initially published.
The Department assumes an average of 15 minutes of additional time per application to manage the extended posting requirements. The average number of certification applications for FY 2023-FY 2025 was approximately 108,786, and the average number of Schedule A applications filed with USCIS for FY 2023-FY 2025 was 23,835, for a total of 132,621. This yields an estimated annual incremental cost of approximately $2.08 million (132,621 × $62.68 × 0.25). Over the ten-year period of analysis, the total undiscounted cost would be approximately $20.78 million. The annualized cost over the ten-year span is projected at $2.14 million at a 3-percent discount rate and $2.23 million at a 7-percent discount rate.
New Attestations (§ 656.13)
The primary cost associated with the new and expanded attestations is the incremental time required for employers and their authorized attorneys or agents to review, understand, and execute the expanded set of attestations at the time of filing. To estimate the cost of the new and expanded attestation requirements, the Department multiplies the number of certification applications and Schedule A applications (132,621) [177] by ( printed page 73983) an average of 30 minutes of additional time per application for employers to review and execute the expanded attestations at the wage rate of $62.68 for a HR specialist. This yields an estimated annual incremental cost of approximately $4.16 million (132,621× 62.68 × 0.5). Over the ten-year period of analysis, the undiscounted total cost would be approximately $41.56 million. The annualized cost over the ten-year span is projected at $4.28 million at a 3-percent discount rate and $4.44 million at a 7-percent discount rate.
Schedule A Occupations Now Subject to Full PERM Recruitment (§ 656.5 & § 656.15)
The proposed elimination of Schedule A requires employers seeking to permanently employ aliens in occupations previously listed under Schedule A, including physical therapists, professional nurses, and workers who possess exceptional ability in the sciences or the arts, to conduct full pre-filing recruitment under proposed section 656.17 and submit an Application for Permanent Employment Certification to OFLC before filing a Form I-140 petition with USCIS. Under the current rule, employers sponsoring aliens in Schedule A occupations obtain a PWD from OFLC but bypass the PERM labor certification process entirely, submitting an uncertified Application for Permanent Employment Certification directly to USCIS without conducting any pre-filing recruitment. The proposed rule imposes entirely new recruitment costs on these employers that they have not previously incurred.
To estimate the population of certification applications affected by this provision, the Department relied on USCIS administrative data on Form I-140 petitions filed under Schedule A occupations for fiscal years 2023 through 2025. The number of Schedule A petitions filed annually is 20,557 in FY 2023, 27,428 in FY 2024, and 23,521 in FY 2025, with an average of approximately 23,835 petitions per year over the three-year sample.[178]
To estimate the incremental labor cost imposed on Schedule A employers by the full PERM pre-filing recruitment requirement, the Department estimates 60 minutes per application for pre-filing recruitment activities to the average annual volume of Schedule A petitions (23,835) and the loaded hourly compensation rate for HR specialists of $62.68, as presented in Exhibit 2. This yields an estimated annual incremental labor cost of approximately $1,493,978, calculated as 23,835 applications multiplied by 1 hour multiplied by $62.68 per hour. Over the ten-year period of analysis, the total undiscounted cost would be approximately $14.94 million. The annualized cost over the ten-year span is projected at $1.54 million at a 3-percent discount rate and $1.32 million at a 7-percent discount rate.
Notify OFLC of Pending Form I-140 Petition (§ 656.13(f))
The primary cost associated with the proposed notification requirement is the time employers would spend preparing and submitting the required notification. Under the proposed rule, employers would be required to notify the NPC, and USCIS in the case of a pending Form I-140 petition, no later than 5 business days after engaging in a qualifying layoff, including the placement of an alien with another employer that is engaging in layoffs.
To estimate the cost of this requirement, the Department estimates that employers would spend approximately 10 minutes (0.167 hours) preparing and submitting each notification. Consistent with the methodology used elsewhere in this analysis, the Department uses the average annual number of Form I-140 petitions for FY 2023 through FY 2025, 23,835 petitions, to estimate the number of notifications. Applying a loaded hourly compensation rate of $62.68 for human resources specialists, the Department estimates an annual cost to employers of approximately $248,991 (23,835 petitions × 0.167 hours × $62.68 per hour).
The Department estimates that this requirement would result in a recurring annual cost of approximately $248,991. Over the ten-year period of analysis, the total undiscounted cost would be approximately $2.49 million. The annualized cost over the ten-year span is projected at $0.26 million (at the 3-percent discount rate) and $0.27 million (at the 7-percent discount rate).
b. Nonquantifiable Costs
In this section, the Department describes various non-quantifiable costs of the proposed rule.
Record Retention Period Restarted 5 Years From Date of Certification Expiration or Denial/Withdrawal of the Application for Permanent Employment Certification, or 5 Years From the Date on Which the Immigrant Petition Is Approved or Denied (§ 656.10)
The principal cost associated with the extended record retention period is the incremental cost of storing documentation for a longer period than currently required for applications with extended processing timelines.
The Department was unable to quantify the incremental storage costs associated with the extended retention period with sufficient precision to report a point estimate, for several reasons, and accordingly treats the costs of this provision as a nonquantifiable cost.
First, the additional retention burden is not uniform across the population of employers filing Applications for Permanent Employment Certification, it varies significantly depending on the processing trajectory of each individual application. Employers whose applications proceed through standard adjudication and result in a timely filed and approved Form I-140 petition would experience little or no meaningful extension of their retention obligations relative to the current rule, as the practical difference between a retention period measured from the date of filing and one measured from the date of certification expiration would be modest in the absence of prolonged processing. By contrast, employers whose applications are subject to audit examination, supervised recruitment, BALCA review, or extended USCIS adjudication of the associated Form I-140 petition could face a substantially longer retention period under the proposed rule than under current regulations. Because the Department cannot predict with confidence which applications will follow each processing path at the time of filing, it cannot reliably estimate the average incremental retention duration across the full population of filers.
Additionally, the incremental cost per unit of additional retention time varies significantly across employers depending on whether they maintain physical or electronic records, the volume of documentation associated with each application, and the nature of each employer's existing document management infrastructure. For the substantial majority of employers who already use electronic filing and document management systems, as evidenced by the near-universal rate of electronic filing in the PERM program, ( printed page 73984) with approximately 99.61 percent of Applications for Permanent Employment Certification filed electronically during FY 2021-FY 2025, the marginal cost of retaining records for an extended period is expected to be negligible, consisting primarily of incremental electronic storage costs that are difficult to isolate and are likely to be minimal relative to overall compliance costs. For the small subset of employers who maintain physical records, the incremental cost would depend on local storage rates and individual document volumes that the Department does not have sufficient data to estimate reliably across the regulated community.
The Department notes that the proposed change does not require employers to collect or create any new documentation; it requires only that documentation already required to be prepared and retained under existing program regulations be preserved for a period that more reliably ensures its availability for program oversight purposes throughout the full adjudication lifecycle of the application and associated petition. For these reasons, the Department concludes qualitatively that the incremental retention costs associated with this provision are minimal relative to the significant program integrity benefits of ensuring that complete documentation remains accessible for the full period during which OFLC and partner agencies may have occasion to request and review it.
The Department requests comment and data that could inform its assessment of the incremental costs associated with the proposed record retention requirements. Specifically, the Department requests comment and data on, the proportion of applications that proceed to audit or BALCA review, the time elapsed between PERM approval or denial and I-140 adjudication, the number of employees potentially affected, the extent of any additional recruitment, hiring, delay, or administrative burdens, and costs associated with those impacts. The Department also requests data on how these costs might vary depending on employer size, industry, and other circumstances.
Permanent Labor Certification Validity Limited to One Use and One Employer (§ 656.30)
The primary costs associated with this clarification fall on employers who have previously relied on the interpretation that an approved permanent labor certification remains available for use in connection with subsequent Form I-140 petitions for the same alien, provided it was timely filed with an initial petition. For employers in this situation whose initial petitions are denied, and whose appeals before USCIS or the federal courts are ultimately unsuccessful, the proposed clarification would require them to obtain a new permanent labor certification, including full compliance with the pre-filing recruitment requirements of proposed section 656.17, before filing a subsequent petition with USCIS. This would involve conducting a new labor market test, obtaining a new PWD from OFLC, and preparing and submitting a new Application for Permanent Employment Certification, all of which impose meaningful time and cost on affected employers and their authorized representatives.
The magnitude of this cost is difficult to estimate reliably for several reasons. First, the Department does not have comprehensive data on the number of employers who have reused approved permanent labor certifications in connection with subsequent Form I-140 petitions, as this practice occurs in the context of USCIS adjudication rather than OFLC processing and is therefore not fully captured in the Department's program data. Second, the costs imposed on any individual employer will depend on the circumstances of the underlying denial, the duration of any appellate proceedings before USCIS or the federal courts, and the extent to which labor market conditions and prevailing wage rates have changed in the intervening period, all of which will vary considerably across affected employers.
Because the proposed clarification forecloses a practice that the Department has determined is inconsistent with the intent of the current rule and with the Secretary's statutory obligations under INA section 212(a)(5)(A), the population of employers who would incur these costs going forward is expected to consist primarily of those who have been relying on an interpretation of the regulation that the Department considers erroneous, rather than those engaging in conduct the Department intended to permit.
The Department concludes qualitatively that the costs of this clarification are real, and in some cases may be significant for individual employers affected by it.
Those costs are a necessary consequence of the Department's obligation to administer the permanent labor certification program in a manner consistent with the Secretary's statutory mandate and are proportionate to the program integrity benefits the clarification is designed to achieve.
Recruitment Report Required at Time of Filing (§ 656.10, § 656.17)
The Department acknowledges that requiring submission of the recruitment report at the time of filing will impose an incremental upfront documentation burden on employers and their authorized attorneys or agents. Under the current rule, employers whose applications are not selected for audit are not required to submit the recruitment report at any point during processing. Under the proposed rule, all employers would be required to upload or otherwise submit the recruitment report at the time of filing. Because this change applies to the full universe of applications, rather than only to those selected for audit, it represents a broader expansion of the submission requirement than any prior change in program administration.
Nonetheless, the Department expects the incremental burden of this requirement to be modest for most employers and their representatives and accordingly treats the costs of this provision as a non-quantifiable cost.
Because employers are required to prepare and maintain the recruitment report before filing the Application for Permanent Employment Certification, the substantive work of compiling the report is already required under current regulations. The proposed change imposes only the additional step of transmitting the completed report to OFLC at the time of filing rather than retaining it solely in the employer's records. For approximately 99.61 percent of employers who already file electronically using the FLAG System, this additional step consists primarily of uploading the recruitment report into the electronic filing system as part of the standard submission process, a task the Department considers de minimis in isolation. The incremental time associated with this step will vary depending on the format and organization of the employer's existing recruitment documentation, the complexity of the employer's pre-filing recruitment activities, and the capabilities of the employer's document management systems, making it difficult to estimate reliably across the full population of filers.
Furthermore, the incremental burden of this requirement will also vary depending on the degree to which employers and their authorized attorneys or agents have already integrated recruitment report preparation into their standard pre-filing workflows. For immigration law ( printed page 73985) firms that handle large volumes of PERM filings and have established document management practices, the additional step of uploading the recruitment report concurrently with the application is expected to impose little marginal burden beyond what is already incurred in preparing and organizing the report for retention. For employers who file without outside legal counsel and who may have less standardized pre-filing documentation practices, the incremental burden of ensuring the recruitment report is in a form suitable for submission at the time of filing may be somewhat greater, though the Department expects this cost to decrease over time as employers adapt their pre-filing workflows to the new requirement.
The Department notes that this provision is expected to generate offsetting administrative efficiencies for both employers and OFLC. By enabling OFLC to review the recruitment report for every application as a standard part of adjudication, rather than only upon audit selection, the proposed rule is expected to reduce the frequency of post-filing requests for documentation, support faster adjudication of compliant applications, and reduce the uncertainty and administrative burden associated with responding to audit notices for employers whose applications might otherwise have been selected for that purpose. For these reasons, the Department concludes qualitatively that the incremental costs associated with the universal recruitment report submission requirement are modest and proportionate to the significant program integrity and adjudicative efficiency benefits expected from this change.
Mandatory Electronic Filing
The Department assumes there is a modest cost associated with mandatory electronic filing falls on the small subset of employers who currently file by mail and who may need to establish access to the FLAG System or otherwise adapt their filing practices to comply with the electronic filing requirement. These costs may include the time necessary to establish or access a FLAG System account, convert or upload supporting documentation in an appropriate electronic format, and modify existing administrative procedures used to prepare and submit applications.
The Department expects this transition cost to be minimal for most affected employers, given that the FLAG System is publicly accessible and that the Department proposes to retain a limited exception to mandatory electronic filing for employers who can demonstrate to the OFLC Administrator that they are unable or limited in their ability to use or access electronic filing. This exception ensures that mandatory electronic filing does not impose an unreasonable burden on employers with genuine technological constraints. The Department concludes qualitatively that the transition costs associated with mandatory electronic filing are minimal relative to the cost savings the requirement is expected to generate for the employer community and OFLC.
Antidiscrimination Provision of the INA
This provision will prohibit an employer from rejecting any qualified U.S. worker over a prospective alien based on any cost justifications or basis, including differences in any term, condition, or privilege of employment, and compensation and fringe benefits between any qualified U.S. worker and a prospective alien.
The Department does not expect this provision to impose additional costs on employers because it requires conduct that is already required under existing Federal anti-discrimination laws. Because employers are already required to comply with these legal obligations, the proposed provision does not impose an incremental compliance burden relative to the regulatory baseline; rather, it reinforces and clarifies the application of existing requirements in the PERM context.
2-Year Pre-Filing Anti-Layoff Provision
The Department expects that the proposed expansion of the layoff period from 180 days to 2 years could generate costs that cannot be reliably quantified. The proposed provision could also impose costs on employers by reducing their flexibility to use the permanent labor certification program following a relevant layoff. Employers whose legitimate workforce needs change during the 2-year period may be unable to pursue permanent labor certification for workers in the same or occupations even when subsequent business conditions create a need for additional workers. These employers could incur costs associated with delaying hiring, conducting additional recruitment, retraining or redeploying existing workers, modifying business operations, or using alternative sources of labor. To the extent that relevant visas are thus freed for use by other employers—those with PERM certifications but facing a visa backlog due to statutory caps—there would be partially-offsetting cost savings associated with their accelerated hiring.
Because the Department lacks sufficient information regarding the extent of relevant layoffs, and subsequent employer hiring needs, the effects of this provision are discussed qualitatively. The Department requests public comments and data linking employers' layoff histories to their standing within relevant visa backlogs and invites interested individuals, organizations, and stakeholders to share their feedback and suggestions.
iii. Cost-Savings
In the following sections, the Department presents the cost-savings of this proposed rule.
a. Quantifiable Cost-Savings
Elimination of Mandatory Print Newspaper Advertisements and Expanded Recruitment Method Requirements (§ 656.17, § 656.21)
One of the largest quantifiable cost savings is the Elimination of Mandatory Print Newspaper Advertisements (§ 656.17/§ 656.21). The cost savings of not requiring employers to post their job ads in print newspapers are twofold. Firstly, they save money by not having to pay newspapers money to post their job ad. Secondly, the cost savings also extend to the labor and time spent that companies used to get the ad online.
The average cost of a job posting in a printed newspaper varies. The average print newspaper advertisement cost for a one-inch column, black-and-white ad in a major publication is $681.[179] Assuming this cost is per insertion, the average cost of a job posting in a printed newspaper will be $1,362 to cover two Sundays. The Department will use this figure but is open to public comments on whether it overstates or understates the true average cost of a newspaper job posting.
To estimate the annual cost savings by eliminating mandatory print newspaper advertisements, the Department multiplies the number of certification applications (108,786) by the average cost of a Newspaper job posting ($1,362). This yields an estimated annual cost-savings of approximately $174,465,390 (108,786 × $1,362). Over the ten-year period of analysis, the total undiscounted cost would be approximately $1.74 billion. The annualized cost over the ten-year span is projected at $179.69 million (at a 3 percent discount rate) and $153.71 (at a 7-percent discount rate).
The proposed rule also expands the recruitment method requirements for nonprofessional jobs by requiring advertising on the employer's own ( printed page 73986) website(s), at least one job search engine, social media (if applicable), and at a job fair (or campus recruitment) and for professional occupations from three additional methods selected from a menu of options similar to the options proposed in this rule to four or more, consistent with their customary recruitment methods for job opportunities that are not the subject of a PERM application. The Department assumes that there are no additional costs to employers to place a job advertisement on job search engines and at a job fair (or -campus recruitment). There are several ways to post job advertisements online for free, making it easier for employers and small businesses to find qualified candidates without spending money on advertising. Many job boards, social media platforms, and community websites allow users to publish job vacancies at no cost. Employers can also share job openings in professional networking groups, local community forums, and university career portals to reach a wider audience. Using these free online platforms helps organizations attract potential applicants efficiently while reducing recruitment costs. By choosing the right combination of free resources, employers can successfully promote their job opportunities to a large and diverse audience. We are seeking public comments on this matter and invite interested individuals, organizations, and stakeholders to share their feedback and suggestions
b. Nonquantifiable Cost-Savings
Permanent Labor Certification Validity Limited to One Use and One Employer (§ 656.30)
The Department expects the clarification of permanent labor certification validity to generate meaningful cost savings for USCIS and, indirectly, for the broader employer community, by reducing the volume of Form I-140 petitions filed in reliance on stale or improperly reused permanent labor certifications.
Under the current rule, as interpreted by some employers, and current USCIS procedures, an approved permanent labor certification may be reused in connection with successive Form I-140 petitions for the same alien, potentially years after the underlying labor market test was conducted, so long as it was timely filed with an initial petition. Each such petition requires USCIS to expend adjudicative resources to review and process a filing that the Department has determined is inconsistent with the intent of the permanent labor certification program and with the Secretary's statutory obligations under INA section 212(a)(5)(A). Where USCIS denies such petitions, additional costs are incurred through the appellate process, including administrative appeals before the USCIS Administrative Appeals Office and, in some cases, litigation in the federal courts. By clarifying that a permanent labor certification is valid for use in connection with one initial Form I-140 petition only, the proposed rule is expected to reduce the frequency of such filings going forward, generating cost savings for USCIS in the form of reduced adjudicative and administrative costs associated with processing petitions based on stale certifications.
To the extent that the clarification also reduces the frequency of federal court litigation arising from USCIS denials of petitions based on reused certifications, the proposed rule is expected to generate additional cost savings for both USCIS and DOJ in the form of reduced litigation costs. The Department is unable to quantify these savings with precision, as it does not have comprehensive data on the volume of petitions currently filed in reliance on reused certifications or the rate at which such petitions are denied and subsequently litigated. However, the Department concludes qualitatively that these cost savings are real and contribute meaningfully to the overall net benefits of the proposed clarification.
Mandatory Electronic Filing (§ 656.10 & § 656.40)
The Department expects the requirement to file Applications for Permanent Employment Certification and Applications for Prevailing Wage Determination electronically through the FLAG System to generate meaningful cost savings for both employers and OFLC.
