Because approximately 99.61 percent of Applications for Permanent Employment Certification and more than 99 percent of Applications for Prevailing Wage Determination were already submitted electronically during FY 2021-FY 2025, mandatory electronic filing does not represent a change in practice for the substantial majority of employers and their authorized representatives. Rather, it codifies and extends to the full population of filers the efficiencies that the Department and most employers have already realized through voluntary adoption of electronic filing.
For employers who currently file electronically, the cost savings associated with mandatory electronic filing are already being realized and are not incremental to the proposed rule. For the small subset of employers who currently file by mail (approximately 0.39 percent of PERM filers based on recent program data) mandatory electronic filing is expected to eliminate the costs associated with paper-based submission, including printing, postage, and private courier or expedited mailing fees. The Department notes that nearly all employers who currently file by mail disclosed valid email addresses on their applications, suggesting that these filers have access to the internet and are likely capable of transitioning to electronic filing without significant investment in new technology or infrastructure.
For OFLC, mandatory electronic filing is expected to generate operational cost savings by reducing the administrative burden associated with receiving, sorting, scanning, and manually processing paper applications. Electronic filing also reduces the frequency of processing delays attributable to illegible, incomplete, or misdirected paper submissions, and supports more efficient interagency data sharing with USCIS and DOS by enabling direct electronic transmission of certified applications and PWDs. The Department considers these savings to be genuine reductions in Federal administrative costs that contribute to the overall net benefits of the proposed rule.
Elimination of Duplicate Labor Certifications (§ 656.30)
The Department expects the elimination of the process for requesting duplicate paper permanent labor certifications under current section 656.30(e) to generate cost savings for employers (potentially), their authorized representatives (potentially), and OFLC (more certainly) by removing an administrative procedure that is no longer necessary given the Department's transition to fully electronic processing and direct electronic transmission of approved certifications to USCIS and DOS.
Under the current rule, employers, aliens, and their authorized attorneys or agents may request duplicate paper copies of approved permanent labor certifications from the CO for submission to USCIS or DOS. This process requires employers or their representatives to identify the need for a duplicate, submit a request to OFLC, and await issuance of the duplicate certification before proceeding with downstream immigration filings. For OFLC, processing duplicate certification requests requires staff time to locate, verify, and issue the requested documentation. These costs, derived by ( printed page 73987) both employers and the Federal Government, are entirely avoidable given that OFLC now transmits approved permanent labor certifications directly to USCIS electronically through existing data sharing agreements, and that employers, aliens, and their authorized representatives have real-time access to approved certification records through their FLAG System accounts.
By eliminating the duplicate certification process, the proposed rule removes a procedural step that generates administrative costs without producing program benefits that are meaningful under current operating conditions as they might have been under other conditions. The Department expects that, for employers and authorized representatives who did not use the duplicate certification process, this elimination will have no effect on their burden. For those who voluntarily used the process, its elimination represents a loss of flexibility and a potential cost, as these entities will need to rely on FLAG System access or other means to provide certification documentation to USCIS or DOS, rather than a straightforward savings. For OFLC, cost savings would be in the form of reduced staff time spent processing requests that are no longer necessary for the downstream submission process. The Department also expects this change to strengthen program integrity by reducing the number of original paper permanent labor certifications in circulation, thereby reducing the risk of fraud associated with the possession and potential misuse of paper certification documents. The Department concludes qualitatively that eliminating the duplicate certification process would generate administrative cost savings for the Federal Government and potentially for employers and their representatives who did not rely on the duplicate process, there would be no change in burden. For those who did rely on it, they may incur costs from the loss of flexibility to request duplicate certifications. Such costs could include additional time and resources needed to obtain or verify certification records through alternative procedures. The Department is unable to reliably quantify these costs and seeks public comment on their potential magnitude, including the time and resources needed to obtain or verify certification records through alternative means. The Department requests comment and data that could inform its assessment of this provision.
Elimination of Post-PWD Supplemental Information Submission (§ 656.40)
The Department expects the elimination of the supplemental information submission step currently available under section 656.40(h), which requires employers to submit a request for redetermination before they may seek Center Director Review (CDR) of a PWD under section 656.41, to generate cost savings for employers and their authorized representatives by streamlining the prevailing wage review process and eliminating a procedural step that imposes compliance costs without commensurate program integrity benefits.
Under the current rule, employers who disagree with a PWD issued by OFLC must first submit supplemental information to the NPC under section 656.40(h) before they are eligible to request review by the Center Director under section 656.41. This two-stage review process requires employers and their authorized representatives to prepare and submit supplemental information at the redetermination stage, await a response from the NPC, and then, if the redetermination is unfavorable, prepare and submit a separate request for Center Director Review (CDR). Each stage of this process imposes incremental costs on employers in the form of attorney or agent time spent preparing submissions, coordinating with the NPC, and managing the administrative timeline associated with a multi-stage review process.
By eliminating the redetermination step and permitting employers to proceed directly to Center Director Review (CDR) under section 656.41 when they disagree with a PWD, the proposed rule reduces the number of procedural steps employers must navigate to obtain review of an unfavorable determination. The Department expects this change to generate cost savings for employers by reducing the total time and professional fees associated with challenging a PWD, and to generate operational efficiencies for OFLC by reducing the volume of supplemental submissions the NPC must review and adjudicate outside of the standard RFI process established in proposed section 656.40(c). The Department concludes qualitatively that the elimination of the post-PWD supplemental information submission step generates meaningful cost savings for the regulated community and contributes to the Department's broader goal of streamlining and modernizing the administration of the PWD process.
4. Summary of the Analysis
Exhibit 2 summarizes the estimated total costs, cost savings, and net cost-savings of the proposed rule over the 10-year analysis period. The Department estimates the total net cost-savings of the proposed rule at $164.30 million at a discount rate of 7 percent and $164.37 million at a discount rate of 3 percent.

5. Regulatory Alternatives
OMB Circular A-4, which outlines best practices in regulatory analysis, directs agencies to analyze alternatives if such alternatives best satisfy the philosophy and principles of E.O. 12866. The Department considered two primary alternatives:
Alternative 1: Mandating Additional Recruitment Methods and Longer Recruitment Periods
As an alternative to the proposed rule, the Department considered mandating additional recruitment methods and longer recruitment periods. Under this alternative, the Department could have required employers to conduct a more extensive labor market test by mandating additional recruitment methods and longer recruitment periods beyond those proposed in this NPRM. For example, the Department could have required all employers to conduct recruitment through multiple commercial job search websites, mandatory social media advertising regardless of customary practice, and mandatory employee referral programs. Similarly, the Department could have required all recruitment advertisements to remain active for periods exceeding 90 calendar days, such as 120 or 180 calendar days, to maximize labor market exposure and increase opportunities for U.S. workers to apply. Although the Department recognizes that these more stringent requirements could potentially provide additional protections for U.S. workers and further strengthen program integrity, the Department determined that such approaches would likely impose substantially greater administrative and compliance burdens on employers, increase processing complexity, and reduce operational flexibility for employers with legitimate workforce needs. The Department also determined that these approaches could significantly increase application preparation costs and prolong recruitment timelines without proportionate additional benefits in many cases. Accordingly, the Department determined that the proposed rule appropriately balances the need to strengthen labor market protections and program integrity while avoiding unnecessary burdens on employers and the administration of the PERM program. ( printed page 73989)
Alternative 2: Elimination of Print Newspaper Advertisement Requirements
As an alternative to the proposed rule, the Department considered a narrower regulatory approach that would have focused solely on eliminating the mandatory print newspaper advertisement requirements currently applicable to the PERM labor certification process, while otherwise retaining the existing recruitment framework and procedural requirements under 20 CFR part 656. Under this alternative, employers would continue to comply with the existing PERM labor certification structure, including the distinction between professional and nonprofessional occupations, the current menu-based recruitment system for professional occupations, and existing recruitment timing and notice requirements. However, the Department would eliminate the requirement that employers place print advertisements in newspapers of general circulation on two different Sundays or in professional journals, as currently required under section 656.17.
In place of mandatory print advertisements, the Department considered allowing employers to satisfy the recruitment requirement through alternative electronic recruitment methods, such as commercial job search websites or employer websites, while preserving the remainder of the current regulatory framework. This alternative would have recognized significant changes in labor market recruitment practices and the declining utility of print newspapers as a meaningful recruitment tool for many occupations.
The Department considered this alternative because current labor market practices increasingly rely on electronic recruitment methods rather than traditional print media. Online job platforms, employer career websites, and social media platforms have become the primary means through which employers advertise vacancies and workers search for employment opportunities. In contrast, print newspaper circulation has significantly declined in recent years, including among local and ethnic newspapers traditionally relied upon for recruitment. As a result, the Department recognized that retaining mandatory print newspaper advertisements may impose costs on employers while producing diminishing recruitment value for U.S. workers.
However, after consideration, the Department determined that this limited alternative would not adequately address broader structural and integrity concerns within the PERM labor certification process. Although eliminating print newspaper advertisements would modernize one component of the labor market test, the Department concluded that retaining the remainder of the current recruitment framework would fail to sufficiently align PERM recruitment requirements with contemporary labor market conditions and employer hiring practices. In particular, the Department determined that simply removing print newspaper advertisements without adopting broader modernization measures would not adequately address inconsistencies in recruitment quality, insufficient labor market exposure, or concerns regarding employer recruitment practices that may deter participation by qualified U.S. workers. The Department also determined that this alternative would not sufficiently strengthen protections for U.S. workers impacted by layoffs, improve transparency during adjudication, or address integrity concerns involving third-party placements, inadequate notice procedures, or inconsistent employer recruitment practices.
The Department invites public comments on these or other possible alternatives with the goal of ensuring a thorough consideration and discussion at the final rule stage.
B. Regulatory Flexibility Act, Small Business Regulatory Enforcement Fairness Act of 1996, and Executive Order 13272 (Proper Consideration of Small Entities in Agency Rulemaking)
The Regulatory Flexibility Act of 1980 (RFA), 5 U.S.C. 601 et seq., as amended by the Small Business Regulatory Enforcement Fairness Act of 1996, Public Law 104-121 (Mar. 29, 1996), hereafter jointly referred to as the RFA, requires agencies to prepare an initial regulatory flexibility analysis (IRFA) when proposing, and a final regulatory flexibility analysis (FRFA) when issuing, regulations that will have a significant economic impact on a substantial number of small entities. The Department conducted the IRFA below of the effect on small entities from the proposed rule.
1. Why the Department Is Considering Action
The Department is considering this rulemaking to modernize and streamline the permanent labor certification (PERM) program and ensure that its regulations continue to fulfill the Department's statutory responsibilities under section 212(a)(5)(A) of the INA. Under the INA, before certain employment-based immigrants may be admitted to the United States for permanent employment, the Secretary must determine and certify that there are not sufficient U.S. workers who are able, willing, qualified, and available to perform the work and that the employment of the alien will not adversely affect the wages and working conditions of similarly employed U.S. workers.
The Department's current PERM regulations were largely established in 2004 and have not undergone comprehensive modernization in approximately two decades. During that time, substantial changes have occurred in labor market conditions, recruitment practices, workforce mobility, and the use of technology in hiring. Employers increasingly rely on electronic recruitment methods, including online job boards, employer websites, social media platforms, and virtual recruitment events, while the use and effectiveness of traditional print newspaper advertising have declined significantly. The Department has determined that the current regulatory framework does not fully reflect these developments and may not consistently ensure that available job opportunities are advertised to the broadest possible pool of qualified U.S. workers.
The Department is also considering this action to strengthen protections for U.S. workers and improve program integrity. Through its administration of the PERM program, the Department has identified areas where existing regulations could be clarified or updated to better address issues involving layoffs, third-party worksite arrangements, notice requirements, recruitment practices, employer accountability, and compliance oversight. The Department believes that updating these requirements would improve transparency, enhance the effectiveness of labor market testing, and provide greater assurance that employers seeking permanent labor certification are conducting recruitment in good faith and providing qualified U.S. workers a meaningful opportunity to compete for available positions.
In addition, the Department seeks to modernize and streamline program administration by codifying electronic filing and communication practices, clarifying regulatory standards, reducing reliance on outdated procedures, and improving operational efficiency. These changes are intended to support timely processing, reduce administrative burdens associated with obsolete requirements, and improve consistency and integrity across the Department's foreign labor certification programs ( printed page 73990) while continuing to provide employers with access to lawful employment-based immigration pathways when qualified U.S. workers are unavailable.
2. Objectives of and Legal Basis for the NPRM
The objective of this NPRM is to modernize the permanent labor certification (PERM) program, improve protections for U.S. workers, strengthen program integrity, and increase the efficiency and transparency of the Department's administration of employment-based permanent labor certifications. Through this rulemaking, the Department seeks to update regulatory standards and procedures that have remained largely unchanged since implementation of the current PERM framework in 2005, while continuing to provide employers with access to permanent employment-based immigration pathways when qualified and available U.S. workers cannot be found.
The proposed rule would update labor market test requirements to better reflect contemporary recruitment practices and labor market conditions. Among other changes, the Department proposes to replace outdated print-focused recruitment requirements with modern electronic recruitment methods, establish a more uniform recruitment framework applicable to most occupations, clarify recruitment content and timing requirements, and require employers to recruit U.S. workers in a manner that is comparable to recruitment conducted for similar positions outside the PERM process. The Department also proposes to strengthen worker protections by expanding requirements related to layoffs, NOF obligations, and consideration of qualified U.S. workers throughout the labor certification process.
In addition, the NPRM proposes measures intended to enhance program integrity and improve compliance with statutory and regulatory requirements. These measures include revised employer attestations, expanded audit and supervised recruitment authorities, strengthened debarment provisions, successor-in-interest accountability standards, and additional requirements governing attorneys and agents participating in the PERM process. The Department also proposes to modernize program administration through mandatory electronic filing and communications, clarification of PWD procedures, and codification of existing electronic processing practices.
The legal basis for this NPRM is found principally in section 212(a)(5)(A) of the INA, 8 U.S.C. 1182(a)(5)(A), which requires the Secretary to determine and certify, before certain employment-based immigrants may be admitted to the United States, that there are not sufficient U.S. workers who are able, willing, qualified, and available to perform the labor or services involved in the employment opportunity and that employment of the alien will not adversely affect the wages and working conditions of similarly employed U.S. workers. Additional authority is provided by section 212(p) of the INA, 8 U.S.C. 1182(p), governing PWDs, and other statutory authorities delegated to the Secretary for administration of the permanent labor certification program. The Department is issuing this NPRM pursuant to these statutory authorities and its responsibility to establish procedures necessary to carry out the labor certification requirements of the INA.

The Department, however, lacks the data needed to identify small employers among the 95,656 unique PERM employers.

4. Compliance Requirements of the NPRM, Including Reporting and Recordkeeping
The Department has considered the incremental costs (and cost-savings) for small entities from the baseline ( i.e., the current practices for complying, at the minimum, with the regulations governing permanent labor certifications at 20 CFR part 656 and labor condition applications at 20 CFR part 655, subpart H) to this NPRM. We estimated the cost (cost-savings) of (1) the time to read and review the NPRM; (2) elimination of mandatory print newspaper advertisements and expanded recruitment method requirements; (3) employer's signature required on all supporting documents; (4) expanded NOF duration and locations; and (5) new attestations. These estimates are consistent with those presented in the E.O. 12866 section.
5. Calculating the Impact of the NPRM on Small Entities
The Department estimates that small entities using the PERM program would incur a one-time cost of $62.68 to familiarize themselves with the rule. In addition to the rule familiarization, each small entity using the PERM program would incur $62.68 for providing employer's signature on all supporting documents. $15.67 for expanded NOF locations. Cost-savings of eliminating mandatory print newspaper advertisements for a job posting is $1,362 per year. The cost of the new and expanded attestation requirements is $31.34 annually per small entities. In total, typical small entities affected will have an annualized cost-savings of $1,190. with 7% discounting.

Therefore, the Department assumes that, on average, small employers have one certification per year.

6. Relevant Federal Rules Duplicating, Overlapping, or Conflicting With the NPRM
The proposed rule concerns the administration of the permanent labor certification process under section 212(a)(5)(A) of the INA, an area of authority specifically delegated to DOL. The regulations in 20 CFR part 656 govern the standards and procedures by which the Department determines whether there are sufficient qualified and available U.S. workers for a job opportunity and whether the employment of an alien would adversely affect the wages and working conditions of similarly employed U.S. workers.
Although other Federal agencies, including DHS and DOS, administer related aspects of the employment-based immigration system, their regulations address separate statutory responsibilities, such as immigrant visa petition adjudication, visa issuance, and admission of aliens. The proposed revisions in this NPRM are intended to complement, rather than duplicate or conflict with those requirements. However, as USCIS administers the immigrant petition process after DOL grants permanent labor certification, the proposed revisions may require DHS to make technical revisions to 8 CFR 204.5 in the event the Department eliminates Schedule A and other minor changes regarding USCIS requests to OFLC for duplicate Applications for Permanent Employment Certification and outdate references to discontinued DOL Form ETA-750.
7. Alternatives to the Proposed Rule
The RFA requires agencies to consider significant alternatives that accomplish the objectives of the proposed rule while minimizing impacts on small entities. The Department considered the following alternatives.
Alternative 1: Reduced Recruitment Duration for Small Employers
As an alternative to the proposed requirement that most recruitment activities remain active for at least 90 calendar days, the Department considered allowing small employers to satisfy the recruitment requirement through a shorter advertising period, such as 30 or 60 calendar days. This approach would reduce recruitment costs and administrative burdens for small businesses by shortening the duration of required job advertisements and reducing the time needed to complete the labor market test. However, the Department determined that a uniform recruitment period better promotes consistency across employers and provides U.S. workers with a meaningful opportunity to learn about and apply for available job opportunities. Accordingly, the Department is proposing a single recruitment standard applicable to all employers.
Alternative 2: Retention of Current Recruitment Framework With Limited Modernization
The Department considered retaining the existing PERM recruitment framework while eliminating only the ( printed page 74001) mandatory newspaper advertisement requirements and permitting electronic recruitment methods as substitutes. Under this alternative, employers would continue to follow the current distinction between professional and nonprofessional occupations and the existing recruitment timelines. This approach would reduce compliance costs for small businesses by avoiding many of the new recruitment, notice, and reporting requirements proposed in this NPRM. However, the Department concluded that this narrower approach would not sufficiently address concerns regarding the effectiveness of the labor market test, evolving recruitment practices, program integrity, and protections for U.S. workers. Therefore, the Department determined that the proposed rule more effectively advances its statutory objectives.
The Department welcomes commenter feedback on these regulatory alternatives and invites suggestions for any other regulatory alternatives that commenters believe would be feasible and appropriate.
C. Review Under the Paperwork Reduction Act
In order to meet its statutory responsibilities under the INA, the Department collects information necessary to render determinations on requests for permanent labor certification, which allow employers to bring foreign labor into the United States on a permanent basis under the PERM program. This information collection is authorized by INA sections 203(b)(2) and (b)(3) and 212(a)(5)(A) and 8 U.S.C. 1153(b)(2) and (b)(3) and 1182(a)(5)(A), DOL and DHS have promulgated regulations to implement provisions of the INA at 20 CFR part 656 and 8 CFR 204.5. This information is subject to the Paperwork Reduction Act (PRA), 44 U.S.C. 3501 et seq. A Federal agency generally cannot conduct or sponsor a collection of information, and the public is generally not required to respond to an information collection, unless it is approved by OMB under the PRA and displays a currently valid OMB control number. In addition, notwithstanding any other provisions of law, no person shall generally be subject to penalty for failing to comply with a collection of information that does not display a valid control number. See 5 CFR 1320.5(a) and 1320.6. The Department obtained OMB approval for this information collection under Control Number 1205-0451. The PRA helps ensure that requested data is provided in the desired format, reporting burden (time and financial resources) is minimized, collection instruments are clearly understood, and the impact of collection requirements on respondents can be properly assessed.
The NPRM, Modernizing the Labor Market Test and Improving Protections for U.S. Workers in the PERM Immigrant Visa Program, proposes to update the PERM program by modernizing recruitment to attract high-quality American talent, improving the safeguards for U. S. workers impacted by layoffs, strengthening employer compliance and program integrity, and modernizing the prevailing wage and PERM application process. The Department now proposes revisions to the information collection tools covered under OMB Control Number 1205-0451, to reflect the regulatory changes proposed in this proposed rule. The revisions to Form ETA-9089 and General Instructions the Department is proposing through this proposed rule will align information collection requirements with the proposed regulatory framework and the Department's continued ongoing efforts to provide greater clarity to employers on regulatory requirements, standardize and streamline information collection to reduce employer time and burden preparing applications, minimize processing delays associated with deficient applications, and promote greater efficiency and transparency in the review and issuance of decisions under the PERM program.
Specifically, the Department is proposing conforming changes to the Form ETA-9089, and the General Instructions which include, for example, updating language based on proposed revised regulatory language, eliminating fields that have and will become obsolete due to the Department's proposed changes to eliminate certain steps of the process, and adding new fields to reflect the proposed regulatory changes that will result in new or revised requirements to be implemented. In addition, DOL seeks to extend, without changes, the validity of Form ETA-9089, Application for Permanent Employment Certification; Form ETA-9089, Final Determination: Permanent Employment Certification Approval; Form ETA-9089, Appendix A, Foreign Worker Information; Form ETA-9089, Appendix B, Additional Worksite Information; Form ETA-9089, Appendix C, Supplemental Information; Form ETA-9089, Appendix D, Optional Pre-filing Recruitment for College and University Teachers and Professional Athletes and Professional Sports Team Coaches, and Form ETA-9089, General Instructions. The Department is, however, proposing the creation and implementation of a new Form ETA-9089, Appendix E, Assurances and Obligations to comply with the proposed revised provisions at 656.13 and to enumerate by topic the assurances and obligations under the PERM labor certification program to which an employer must attest compliance. This form will include existing obligations contained in current sections 656.10, 656.12, 656.17, and 656.20 that are being relocated to proposed section 656.13, which also further revises them to create clear employer attestations in efforts to clarify and/or update compliance requirements. The proposals in this rule strengthen U.S. worker protections and bolster program integrity by holding employers who choose to participate in the PERM program accountable to the Department, and to their responsibilities to both U.S. workers and aliens, with regard to the employer's filing of the of the Application for Permanent Employment Certification and participation in the PERM labor certification program.
The rule also proposes that employers seeking to sponsor workers under DHS' National Interest Waiver program must continue to file Form ETA-9089 directly with DHS. This will continue to eliminate the intake of this information by the Department and will transfer the burden imposed by the requirement to another agency so it is not accounted for in the reporting burden disclosure the Department submits to OMB.
In addition, the Department is proposing to revise its regulations covering Schedule A occupations to eliminate the exception to pre-filing recruitment requirements. This change, if approved, would result in employers who utilize Schedule A having to conduct pre-filing requirements and submitting the Form ETA-9089 to the Department, instead of being submitted with DHS, which is the current process.
DOL is particularly interested in comments that:
Evaluate whether the proposed collection of information is necessary for the proper performance of the functions of the agency, including whether the information will have practical utility;
Evaluate the accuracy of the agency's estimate of the burden of the proposed collection of information, including the validity of the methodology and assumptions used;
Enhance the quality, utility, and clarity of the information to be collected; and
Minimize the burden of the collection of information on those who are to respond, including through the use of appropriate automated, ( printed page 74002) electronic, mechanical, or other technological collection techniques or other forms of information technology ( e.g., permitting electronic submission of responses).
The Department is also considering which questions or instructions could be removed to reduce public burden without impacting the general collection of information to assess and process Applications for Permanent Employment Certification or revised to better collect the information needed to fulfill the Department's statutory obligations regarding the requests for permanent employment certification. For example, questions confirming whether a required appendix has been attached could potentially be removed if the electronic platform would not permit submission without the required appendix. Similarly, redundant questions about the employer's acceptance of foreign diploma/degree equivalency could be removed from Form ETA-9089 because the information is already collected through the current Form ETA-9141. The Department is also interested in feedback regarding ways to improve the information collected to ensure consistency across the forms and appendices the Department relies on to collect information in order to process Applications for Permanent Employment Certification or requesting more information that would assist employers establish or clarify any successor in interest scenario or travel requirements ( e.g., frequency of travel). As discussed above, the Department seeks to improve the collection of worksite information to better reflect modern concepts of remote work and telework, without adding burden to employers. The Department is therefore seeking comments on how to best approach these changes and may consider adding, eliminating or revising questions, as necessary to accomplish the goals of this proposed rule. Lastly, the Department is seeking comments on how an employer's future filings of the ETA Form 9089 under Supervised Recruitment are clearly identifiable.

D. Review Under Executive Order 13132
E.O. 13132, Federalism,64 FR 43255 (Aug. 10, 1999), imposes certain requirements on Federal agencies formulating and implementing policies or regulations that preempt State law or that have federalism implications. The E.O. requires agencies to examine the constitutional and statutory authority supporting any action that would limit the policymaking discretion of the States and to carefully assess the necessity for such actions. The E.O. also requires agencies to have an accountable process to ensure meaningful and timely input by State and local officials in the development of regulatory policies that have federalism implications.
The Department has examined this proposed rule and has determined that it would not have a substantial direct effect on the States, on the relationship between the national government and the States, or on the distribution of power and responsibilities among the various levels of government.
E. Executive Order 13175 (Consultation and Coordination With Indian Tribal Governments)
The Department has reviewed this proposed rule in accordance with E.O. 13175 and has determined that it does not have tribal implications. This proposed rule does not have substantial direct effects on one or more Indian tribes, on the relationship between the Federal Government and Indian tribes, or on the distribution of power and responsibilities between the Federal Government and tribal governments.
F. Review Under Executive Order 12988
With respect to the review of existing regulations and the promulgation of new regulations, section 3(a) of E.O. 12988, “Civil Justice Reform,” imposes on Federal agencies the general duty to adhere to the following requirements: (1) eliminate drafting errors and ambiguity, (2) write regulations to minimize litigation, (3) provide a clear legal standard for affected conduct rather than a general standard, and (4) promote simplification and burden reduction. 61 FR 4729 (Feb. 7, 1996). Regarding the review required by section 3(a), section 3(b) of E.O. 12988 specifically requires that Executive agencies make every reasonable effort to ensure that the regulation: (1) clearly specifies the preemptive effect, if any, (2) clearly specifies any effect on existing Federal law or regulation, (3) provides a clear legal standard for affected conduct while promoting simplification and burden reduction, (4) specifies the retroactive effect, if any, (5) adequately defines key terms, and (6) addresses other important issues affecting clarity and general draftsmanship under any guidelines issued by the Attorney General.
Section 3(c) of E.O. 12988 requires Executive agencies to review regulations in light of applicable standards in section 3(a) and section 3(b) to determine whether they are met or it is unreasonable to meet one or more of them. The Department has completed the required review and determined that, to the extent permitted by law, this proposed rule meets the relevant standards of E.O. 12988.
G. Review Under the Unfunded Mandates Reform Act
The Unfunded Mandates Reform Act of 1995 (UMRA) (Pub. L. 104-4, codified at 2 U.S.C. 1501 et seq.) is intended, among other things, to curb the practice of imposing unfunded Federal mandates on state, local, and tribal governments. UMRA requires Federal agencies to assess a regulation's effects on state, local, and tribal governments, as well as on the private sector, except to the extent the regulation incorporates requirements specifically set forth in law. Title II of the UMRA requires each Federal agency to prepare a written statement assessing the effects of any regulation that includes any Federal mandate in a proposed or final agency rule that may result in $100 million or more expenditure (adjusted annually for inflation) in any one year by State, local, and Tribal governments, in the aggregate, or by the private sector. A ( printed page 74003) Federal mandate is any provision in a regulation that imposes an enforceable duty upon State, local, or tribal governments, or upon the private sector, except as a condition of Federal assistance or a duty arising from participation in a voluntary Federal program.
The Department examined this proposed rule according to UMRA and its statement of policy and determined that this rule does not contain a Federal intergovernmental mandate, nor is it expected to require expenditures of $100 million or more in any one year by State, local, and Tribal governments, in the aggregate, or by the private sector.
H. Review Under Executive Order 12630
Pursuant to E.O. 12630, Governmental Actions and Interference with Constitutionally Protected Property Rights,53 FR 8859 (Mar. 18, 1988), the Department has determined that this proposed rule would not result in any takings that might require compensation under the Fifth Amendment to the U.S. Constitution.
I. Review Under the Treasury and General Government Appropriations Act, 1999
Section 654 of the Treasury and General Government Appropriations Act, 1999 (Pub. L. 105-277) requires Federal agencies to issue a Family Policymaking Assessment for any rule that may affect family well-being. This proposed rule would not have any impact on the autonomy or integrity of the family as an institution. Accordingly, the Department has concluded that it is not necessary to prepare a Family Policymaking Assessment.
J. Review Under the Treasury and General Government Appropriations Act, 2001
Section 515 of the Treasury and General Government Appropriations Act, 2001 (44 U.S.C. 3516, note) provides for Federal agencies to review most disseminations of information to the public under information quality guidelines established by each agency pursuant to general guidelines issued by OMB. OMB's guidelines were published at 67 FR 8452 (Feb. 22, 2002). The Department has reviewed this proposed rule under the OMB guidelines and has concluded that it is consistent with applicable policies in those guidelines.

Department of Labor
For the reasons stated in the preamble, the Department of Labor proposes to amend part 656 of Chapter V, Title 20, Code of Federal Regulations, as follows:
PART 656—LABOR CERTIFICATION PROCESS FOR PERMANENT EMPLOYMENT OF ALIENS IN THE UNITED STATES
1. The authority citation for part 656 continues to read as follows:
Authority: 8 U.S.C. 1182(a)(5)(A), 1182(p)(1); sec. 122, Public Law 101-649, 109 Stat. 4978; and Title IV, Public Law 105-277, 112 Stat. 2681.
2. Revise subpart A to read as follows:
Subpart A—General Provisions
656.1
Purpose and scope.
656.2
Statutory authority and burden of proof.
656.3
Definition of terms.
656.4
Persons and entities authorized to file and sign.
656.5
Requirements for agents and attorneys.
656.6
Successors in interest.
656.7
Transition procedures.
§ 656.1
Purpose and scope.
(a) Purpose. This part establishes the requirements and the procedures by which the Department of Labor (Department or DOL) makes a determination in connection with Applications for Permanent Employment Certification and certifies its determination to the Department of Homeland Security (DHS) and Department of State (DOS). A permanent labor certification issued under this part reflects a determination by the Secretary of Labor (Secretary), pursuant to 8 U.S.C. 1182(a)(5)(A), that:
(1) There are not sufficient workers who are able, willing, and qualified United States (U.S.) workers available at the time of application for a visa and admission to the United States and at the place where the alien is to perform the skilled or unskilled services; and
(2) The employment of the alien will not adversely affect the wages and working conditions of workers in the United States similarly employed.
(b) Scope. This part sets forth the procedures governing the labor certification process for the permanent employment of aliens in the United States. It also establishes standards and obligations with respect to the terms and conditions of the permanent labor certification with which employers must comply and sets forth integrity measures for ensuring employers' good faith recruitment of U.S. workers and continued compliance with the terms and conditions of the permanent labor certification.
§ 656.2
Statutory authority and burden of proof.
(a) The Secretary has delegated the authority to issue permanent labor certifications and execute other statutory responsibilities required by 8 U.S.C. 1182(a)(5)(A) to the Office of Foreign Labor Certification (OFLC). Determinations on an Application for Permanent Employment Certification are made by the OFLC Administrator who, in turn, may delegate this responsibility to designated staff ( e.g., a Certifying Officer (CO)).
(b) The employer has the burden of proof to establish eligibility to receive certification of an Application for Permanent Employment Certification.
§ 656.3
Definition of terms.
For purposes of this part, the following definitions apply:
Act. The Immigration and Nationality Act, as amended (INA), 8 U.S.C. 1101 et seq.
Actual minimum requirements. The minimum requirements for education, experience, training, skills, licenses, or other requirements that are necessary for prospective applicants to perform the employer's job opportunity in a normally acceptable manner. The employer's actual minimum requirements must be normal for the occupation and must not exceed the Specific Vocational Preparation (SVP) required in the Standard Occupational Classification (SOC) code, as indicated by the Occupational Network (O*NET) Job Zones, or must not be materially different from the requirements stated in the employer's recruitment for job opportunities that were not subject to PERM labor certification in the same or substantially comparable occupational classifications during the 12 months preceding the date on which recruitment began for the job opportunity stated in the Application for Permanent Employment Certification, unless the employer is able to establish a business necessity for any requirements that are not normal to the occupation or exceed the SVP.
Administrative Law Judge (ALJ). A person within the Department's Office of Administrative Law Judges appointed pursuant to 5 U.S.C. 3105.
Agent. A legal entity or person that:
(i) Is authorized to act on behalf of the employer for permanent labor certification purposes; ( printed page 74004)
(ii) Is not itself an employer as defined in this subpart with respect to a specific application; and
(iii) Is not under suspension, debarment, expulsion, or disbarment from practice before any court, the Department, or the Executive Office for Immigration Review or DHS under 8 CFR 292.3 or 1003.101.
Applicant. A U.S. worker who is applying for a job opportunity for which an employer anticipates an Application for Permanent Employment Certification.
Application for Permanent Employment Certification. The Office of Management and Budget (OMB)-approved Form ETA-9089, including the PWD (Form ETA-9141) and appropriate appendices, or any successor forms submitted by an employer to secure a permanent labor certification determination from DOL.
Area of intended employment (AIE). The geographic area within normal commuting distance of the place(s) of employment for which permanent labor certification is sought. There is no rigid measure of distance that constitutes a normal commuting distance or normal commuting area, because there may be widely varying factual circumstances among different areas ( e.g., average commuting times, barriers to reaching the place of employment, or quality of the regional transportation network). If a place of employment is within an MSA, including a multistate MSA, any place within the MSA is deemed to be within normal commuting distance of the place of employment. The borders of MSAs are not controlling in the identification of the normal commuting area; a place of employment outside of an MSA may be within normal commuting distance of a place of employment that is inside ( e.g., near the border of) the MSA.
Attorney. Any person who is a member in good standing of the bar of the highest court of any State, possession, territory, or commonwealth of the United States, or the District of Columbia (DC). Such a person is also permitted to act as an agent under this subpart. No attorney who is under suspension, debarment, expulsion, or disbarment from practice before any court, the Department, or the Executive Office for Immigration Review or DHS under 8 CFR 292.3 or 1003.101 may represent an employer under this part.
Barter. The transfer of ownership of a permanent labor certification or application from one person to another by voluntary act or agreement in exchange for a commodity, service, property or other valuable consideration.
Board of Alien Labor Certification Appeals (BALCA or Board). The permanent Board established by this part, chaired by the Chief Administrative Law Judge (Chief ALJ), and consisting of Administrative Law Judges (ALJs) appointed pursuant to 5 U.S.C. 3105 and designated by the Chief ALJ to be members of the Board.
Business day. Any day other than a Saturday, Sunday, or Federal holiday. The terms “business day” and “working day,” wherever used in this part, have the same meaning.
Business Necessity. An employer's demonstration that the job duties and requirements of the job opportunity bear a reasonable relationship to the occupation in the context of the employer's business and are essential to perform the job in a reasonable manner.
Certifying Officer (CO). The person who makes a determination on an Application for Permanent Employment Certification filed under this part. The OFLC Administrator is the national CO. Other COs may be designated by the OFLC Administrator to also make the determinations required under this part.
Chief Administrative Law Judge (Chief ALJ). The chief official of the Department's Office of Administrative Law Judges or the Chief ALJ's designee.
Closely held corporation. A corporation that typically has relatively few shareholders and whose shares are not generally traded in the securities market.
Department of Homeland Security (DHS). The Department of Homeland Security, as established by 6 U.S.C. 111.
Employee. A person who is engaged to perform work for an employer, as defined under the general common law of agency. Some of the factors relevant to the determination of employee status include: the hiring party's right to control the manner and means by which the work is accomplished; the skill required to perform the work; the source of the instrumentalities and tools for accomplishing the work; the location of the work; the hiring party's discretion over when and how long to work; and whether the work is part of the regular business of the hiring party. Other applicable factors may be considered and no one factor is dispositive.
Employer. An individual or entity ( e.g., partnership, association, corporation, cooperative, firm, joint stock company, trust, public institution, or other organization with legal rights and duties) that seeks to permanently employ a full-time alien at a place within the United States and:
(1)(i) Has or will have an employment relationship (such as the ability to hire, pay, fire, supervise, or otherwise control the work performed by the employee) with respect to a worker;
(ii) Possesses a valid Federal Employer Identification Number (FEIN);
(iii) Has a place of business (physical location) in the U.S. and a means by which it may be contacted for employment The employer must employ at least one individual full-time at the place of business;
(iv) Files an Application for Permanent Employment Certification other than as an agent or attorney; and
(v) Is an individual or entity on whose behalf an Application for Permanent Employment Certification is filed.
(2) Persons who are temporarily in the United States, including but not limited to, individuals with temporary employment visas, adjustment applicants whose underlying work authorization was based on a temporary employment visa, foreign diplomats, intra-company transferees, students, and exchange visitors, visitors for business or pleasure, and representatives of foreign information media cannot be employers for the purpose of obtaining a labor certification for permanent employment in the United States.
(3) A foreign mission, determined applicable by the Secretary of State, e.g.22 U.S.C. 4302(a)(3) inter alia, cannot be an employer for purposes of obtaining a permanent labor certification.
Equal Employment Opportunity Commission (EEOC). The Equal Employment Opportunity Commission, an independent Federal agency established by Title VII of the Civil Rights Act of 1964.
Familial relationship. A familial relationship includes any relationship established by blood, marriage, or adoption, even if distant. For example, cousins of all degrees, aunts, uncles, grandparents and grandchildren are included. It also includes any relationship established through marriage (including domestic partnerships, common-law marriages or civil unions), such as in-laws and step families.
Federal holiday. Legal public holiday as defined at 5 U.S.C. 6103.
Fraud. A false representation of a material fact, or the concealment of a material fact, made with knowledge of its falsity and with the intent to deceive the Department, DHS, the Department of State, or another Government entity, or to obtain a benefit under this part or the Act to which the person is not entitled. Reliance by the Department on the false representation or concealment is not required.
Immigrant Petition. The U.S. Citizenship and Immigration Services (USCIS) Form I-140, Immigrant Petition ( printed page 74005) for Alien Workers, or successor form, and accompanying documentation required by DHS for employers seeking to employ foreign persons under an employment-based immigrant visa.
Job opportunity. Full-time employment at a place in the United States to which U.S. workers can be referred. In the event of an audit, the employer must be prepared to document the permanent and full-time nature of the position by furnishing position descriptions and payroll records for the job opportunity involved in the Application for Permanent Employment Certification.
Job offer. The offer made by an employer or potential employer of an alien(s) to both U.S. and aliens describing all the material terms and conditions of employment, including those relating to wages, working conditions, and other benefits for the job opportunity.
Job order. The document containing the material terms and conditions of employment that is posted by the State Workforce Agency (SWA) on its interstate and intrastate job clearance systems to recruit prospective U.S. applicants for the employer's job offer.
Layoff. An involuntary separation of one or more workers by the employer without cause or prejudice, such as a personnel action characterized by the employer as a reduction in force, restructuring, or downsizing.
Material. A fact, statement, representation, or omission is material if it has a natural tendency to influence, or is capable of influencing, a decision of the Department under this part or a decision of DHS or the Department of State on an Immigrant Petition or visa application that relies on a permanent labor certification, regardless of whether it actually influenced the decision. A misrepresentation is not immaterial merely because the Department could have discovered the truth through its own inquiry.
Metropolitan Statistical Area (MSA). A geographic entity defined by OMB for use by Federal statistical agencies in collecting, tabulating, and publishing Federal statistics. An MSA contains a core urban area of 50,000 or more population, and a Micropolitan Statistical Area contains an urban core of at least 10,000 (but fewer than 50,000) population. Each metropolitan or micropolitan area consists of one or more counties and includes the counties containing the core urban area, as well as any adjacent counties that have a high degree of social and economic integration (as measured by commuting to work) with the urban core.
National Processing Center (NPC). The offices within OFLC in which the COs operate and which are charged with the adjudication of Applications for Permanent Employment Certification.
Non-profit or tax-exempt organization. An organization that:
(1) Is defined as a tax exempt organization under the Internal Revenue Code of 1986, section 501(c)(3), (c)(4), or (c)(6) (26 U.S.C. 501(c)(3), (c)(4) or (c)(6)); and
(2) Has been approved as a tax-exempt organization for research or educational purposes by the Internal Revenue Service.
Offered wage. The salary or wage, or salary or wage range offered by the employer for the job opportunity. The offered wage must be included in the employer's Application for Permanent Employment Certification and advertisements and must equal or exceed the highest of the prevailing wage or Federal, State, or local minimum wage.
Office of Foreign Labor Certification (OFLC). The organizational component within DOL that provides national leadership and policy guidance and develops regulations and procedures to carry out the responsibilities of the Secretary under the INA, as amended, concerning aliens seeking admission to the United States in order to work under section 212(a)(5)(A) of the INA, as amended.
OFLC Administrator. The primary official of OFLC, or the OFLC Administrator's designee.
Occupational Network (O*NET). The system developed by DOL, Employment and Training Administration, to provide to the general public information on skills, abilities, knowledge, work activities, interests and SVP levels associated with occupations. O*NET is based on the SOC system. Further information about O*NET can be found at http://www.onetcenter.org.
Pattern or practice. A course of conduct in which the act or omission in question is repeated, routine, or the regular rather than conduct in which the act or omission in question represents an isolated, sporadic, or accidental act. A pattern or practice may be established by conduct on two or more Applications for Permanent Employment Certification, whether filed by or on behalf of the same employer or different employers, and, in the case of an attorney or agent, by conduct across the applications of different clients.
Permanent employment. Employment in a job opportunity of indefinite duration in which an employee will ordinarily have an expectation of continued employment. To establish that a job opportunity is “permanent,” an employer must establish, at a minimum, that it: (1) has a bona fide need for a worker to fill a job opportunity whose services or labor are needed year-round, except for job opportunities such as members of the teaching profession and professional athletes; and (2) the employer will not require breaks or periods of inactivity, except where such periods stem from an established employer policy for leave that applies to other employees similarly employed by the employer; and (3) will not enter into a temporary staffing agreement to place the alien with one or more third-party clients.
Permanent labor certification. Certification made by the OFLC Administrator, based on the Application for Permanent Employment Certification and all supporting documentation, with respect to an employer seeking to file an Immigrant Petition with DHS to employ an alien under an immigrant visa, pursuant to section 212(a)(5)(A) of the Act, 8 U.S.C. 1182(a)(5)(A), and this part.
Place of employment. A worksite or physical location where work under the Application for Permanent Employment Certification is actually performed.
Prevailing wage determination (PWD). The prevailing wage for the job opportunity that is the subject of the Application for Permanent Employment Certification. The PWD is issued by an NPC on the Form ETA-9141, Application for Prevailing Wage Determination, or successor form.
Professional athlete. An individual who is employed as an athlete by (1) A team that is a member of an association of six or more professional sports teams whose total combined revenues exceed $10,000,000 per year, if the association governs the conduct of its members and regulates the contests and exhibitions in which its member teams regularly engage; or (2) Any minor league team that is affiliated with such an association, as defined by 8 U.S.C. 1182(a)(5)(A)(iii)(II).
Purchase. The transfer of ownership of a permanent labor certification application or certification from one person to another by voluntary act and agreement, based on a valuable consideration.
Sale. An agreement between two parties, called, respectively, the seller (or vendor) and the buyer (or purchaser) by which the seller, in consideration of the payment or promise of payment of a certain price in money terms, transfers ownership of a permanent labor certification application or certification to the buyer. ( printed page 74006)
Secretary of Labor (Secretary). The chief official of the Department or the Secretary's designee.
Secretary of Homeland Security. The chief official of DHS or the Secretary of Homeland Security's designee.
Secretary of State. The chief official of the U.S. Department of State or the Secretary of State's designee.
State Workforce Agency (SWA). A State government agency that receives funds pursuant to the Wagner-Peyser Act, 29 U.S.C. 49 et seq., to administer the State's public labor exchange activities.
Strike. A concerted stoppage of work by employees as a result of a labor dispute, or any concerted slowdown or other concerted interruption of operation (including stoppage by reason of the expiration of a collective bargaining agreement (CBA)).
Substantially comparable. A job or position where more than a majority ( i.e., 51 percent or more) of the duties performed are the same as those disclosed in the Application for Permanent Employment Certification and justified based on organizational charts and position descriptions, the amount and percentage of time spent performing the duties, and payroll records.
United States. The continental United States, Alaska, Hawaii, the Commonwealth of Puerto Rico, and the territories of Guam, the U.S. Virgin Islands, and the Commonwealth of the Northern Mariana Islands.
U.S. Citizenship and Immigration Services (USCIS). An operational component of DHS.
U.S. worker. A worker who is:
(i) A U.S. citizen or national of the United States;
(ii) An individual who is lawfully admitted for permanent residence in the United States, is admitted as a refugee under 8 U.S.C. 1157 or is granted asylum under 8 U.S.C. 1158.
Wages. All forms of cash remuneration to a worker by an employer in payment for labor or services.
Willful. Done knowingly, deliberately, and voluntarily, or in reckless disregard of the requirements of this part or of the truth or falsity of a representation, as distinguished from an act or omission that is accidental, inadvertent, or merely negligent. A person acts willfully with respect to a requirement of this part when the person knows of the requirement or shows reckless disregard for whether the person's conduct complies with it.
Willful misrepresentation. A false representation of a material fact, made willfully, in connection with an Application for Permanent Employment Certification, a prevailing wage determination, or any other document, form, or communication submitted under this part. Intent to deceive is not an element of willful misrepresentation; it is sufficient that the person knew the representation was false, or made it with reckless disregard for its truth or falsity, and made it voluntarily.
§ 656.4
Persons and entities authorized to file and sign.
(a) Persons authorized to file. In addition to the employer, an Application for Permanent Employment Certification may be filed by an attorney or agent, as defined in § 656.3.
(b) Employer's signature required. Regardless of whether the employer is represented by an attorney or agent, the employer is required to sign the Application for Permanent Employment Certification and all documentation submitted to DOL.
§ 656.5
Requirements for agents and attorneys.
(a) Representation agreement required. An agent or attorney filing an Application for Permanent Employment Certification on behalf of an employer must retain an original executed agreement or other document demonstrating their authority to represent the employer and make this documentation available upon request by the CO. Documentation to prove compliance with this requirement must be retained by the agent or attorney for a period of 5 years from the date on which the certified Application for Permanent Employment Certification expires or from the date of determination if the Application for Permanent Employment Certification or Immigrant Petition is denied or withdrawn.
(b) Consideration of U.S. workers.
(1) The alien and/or the alien's agent and/or attorney may not interview or consider U.S. workers for the job offered to the alien, unless the agent and/or attorney is the employer's representative, as described in paragraph (b)(2) of this section.
(2) The employer's representative who interviews or considers U.S. workers for the job offered to the alien must be a U.S. worker and one who normally interviews or considers, on behalf of the employer, applicants for job opportunities such as that offered the alien which do not involve labor certifications.
§ 656.6
Successors in interest.
(a) Liability of successors in interest. Where an employer, agent, or attorney has violated the requirements of this part, a successor in interest to that employer, agent, or attorney may be held liable for the duties and obligations of the violating employer, agent, or attorney in certain circumstances, regardless of whether such successor in interest has succeeded to all the rights and liabilities of the predecessor employer, agent, or attorney.
(b) Definition of successors in interest. The following factors, including those as used under Title VII of the Civil Rights Act and the Vietnam Era Veterans' Readjustment Assistance Act, may be considered in determining whether an employer, agent, or attorney is a successor in interest; however, these factors are not exhaustive, and no one factor is dispositive, but all of the circumstances will be considered as a whole:
(1) Substantial continuity of the same business operations;
(2) Use of the same facilities;
(3) Continuity of the workforce;
(4) Similarity of jobs and working conditions;
(5) Similarity of supervisory personnel;
(6) Whether the former management or owner retains a direct or indirect interest in the new enterprise;
(7) Similarity in machinery, equipment, and production methods;
(8) Similarity of products and services;
(9) The ability of the predecessor to provide relief; and
(10) For purposes of debarment, the personal involvement of the firm's ownership, management, supervisors, and others associated with the firm in the violation(s) at issue.
(c) Effect of debarment on successors in interest. When an employer, agent, or attorney is debarred under § 656.31(f), any successor in interest to the debarred employer, agent, or attorney is also debarred. No Application for Permanent Employment Certification may be filed by or on behalf of or approved for a successor in interest to a debarred employer, agent, or attorney, subject to the term limits set forth in § 656.31(f). If the CO determines that an Application for Permanent Employment Certification was filed by or on behalf of a successor in interest to a debarred employer, agent, or attorney during the period of debarment as set forth in § 656.31(f), the CO will deny the application pursuant to § 656.24(d) solely on the basis that the entity is a successor in interest to a debarred employer, agent, or attorney. If the OFLC Administrator determines that a permanent labor certification was issued to a successor in interest to a debarred employer, the OFLC ( printed page 74007) Administrator may revoke the certification pursuant to § 656.32. The employer, agent, or attorney may appeal its status as a successor in interest to the debarred entity, pursuant to the procedures for appeals of CO determinations at § 656.26.
§ 656.7
Transition procedures.
(a) Processing of cases under prior regulations. The NPC will continue to process an Application for Permanent Employment Certification or an Application for Prevailing Wage Determination submitted prior to February 16, 2027, in accordance with the requirements of this part that were in effect prior to January 19, 2027.
(b) Processing of cases under new regulations. The NPC will process an Application for Permanent Employment Certification or an Application for Prevailing Wage Determination submitted on or after February 16, 2027, in accordance with the requirements of this part that went into effect on January 19, 2027.
